Introducing pixelation with applications
J. Daisie Rock

Abstract
. Motivated by the desire for a new kind of approximation, we define a type of localization called pixelation . We present how pixelation manifests in representation theory and in the study of sites and sheaves. A path category is constructed from a set, a collection of “paths” into the set, and an equivalence relation on the paths. A screen is a partition of the set that respects the paths and equivalence relation. For a commutative ring, we also enrich the path category over its modules (=linearize the category with respect to the ring) and quotient by an ideal generated by paths (possibly 0). The pixelation is the localization of a path category, or the enriched quotient, with respect to a screen. The localization has useful properties and serves as an approximation of the original category. As applications, we use pixelations to provide a new point of view of the Zariski topology of localized ring spectra, provide a parallel story to a ringed space and sheaves of modules, and construct a categorical generalization of higher Auslander algebras of type A .



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 18E35, 18A25, 18F20. Secondary: 18G15, 16G20, 16G99

Introduction

Context and Motivation

There is an increasing interest in studying representations of categories with infinitely-many objects.
That is, studying the category of functors
C\to\mathcal{K}
where
C
is a small category with infinitely-many objects and
\mathcal{K}
is some well-understood abelian category.
Work typically begins by studying the case where
C
is either a line (type
A
, though not necessarily totally-ordered) or a circle (type
\tilde{A}
, though not necessarily cyclicly ordered).

Often,
C
is replaced by some additive category
\mathcal{A}
so that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{K}
are both enriched over
\Bbbk
-modules, for some commutative ring
\Bbbk
, and the functors are replaced with additive functors enriched over
\Bbbk
-modules as well.
In the language of representation theory: we say
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{K}
, and the functors
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{K}
are
\Bbbk
-linear.

In topological data analysis, a persistence module is usually a functor from a small category into the category of finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector spaces, for some field
\Bbbk
.
In this case the persistence modules decompose uniquely (up to isomorphism) into indecomposable summands, each of which has a local endomorphism ring
[
8
,
5
]
.
Recent work has begun in decomposing infinite persistence modules of type
A
over other types of rings, such as PIDs
[
13
]
.
One may also consider multiparameter persistence modules, which can be interpreted as representations as
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Here, complete decomposition/structure theorems are not possible but progress is made to understand persistence modules in other ways using invariants
[
1
]
.

A structure similar to persistence modules appears as Fock space representations of continuum quantum groups of types
A
and
\tilde{A}

[
17
]
.
Finitely-indexed persistence modules can also be considered as representations of quivers.
An infinite generalization of quiver representations was introduced in
[
2
]
.
A further generalization and partial structure theorem and classifications appears in
[
15
]
.
In all of these cases, the category
C
of interest has a notion of paths that are not just morphisms but also in a sense topological or geometric.

One way to better understand representations in these cases is to consider approximations. In
[
15
]
a technique was used to reduce representations to smaller, understandable parts.
The technique splits representations into noise and noise-free pieces.
One can view this as an approximation by deciding what kind of noise is allowed in the representations.
The precursor to this technique was used in
[
9
]
and this technique is the precursor to pixelation in the present paper.
A homological type of approximation was used in
[
3
]
on multiparameter persistence modules.

In the present paper, we think about approximation via localization.
While the applications are heavily tied to representation theory, with the exception of some results on sites and ring spectra in Section
4.2
, the main techniques and results in the present paper are primarily categorical in nature.
In the categorical sense localization as approximation is philosophically straightforward: one groups objects together based on some parameters, including morphisms, and this is an approximation of the original category.
We can think of localizations of rings as approximations as well, by looking at how the corresponding ring spectra are related.
We look at the categories of open sets and inclusions and see if one is related to the other by some kind of categorical localization process.

Our approach to approximation draws inspiration from a real world process: digital photography.
One takes a photo of what (feels like) infinitely-many tiny atoms and obtains a photo with a finite number of pixels.
In its infancy, digital photos were very clearly (poor) approximations of what we see in the real world.
In 2026, we can produce digital photos with more than enough pixels to accurately approximate what we see.
However, even at the beginning, we only needed a few pixels to know if the coffee pot was full.
1
1

1

The first digital camera had a resolution of 128x128 pixels and was used to check the coffee pot in a break room at the University of Cambridge. The client software was written by Quentin Stafford-Fraser and the server software was written by Paul Jardetzky.

Organization and contributions

Here we give an overview of the paper and highlight what the author considers to be the main results: Theorems
A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
.
However, given the extremely varied interests of those who use representation theory, the reader may find other results to be the “highlight”.

Path categories and pixelation

In Section
1
we define triples
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
, where
\mathbb{X}
is a set,
\Gamma
acts like a set of paths in
\mathbb{X}
, an
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
\Gamma
(Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
).
Throughout the paper we use the running examples of
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, starting with Example
1.5
.
Inspired by our approach to approximation, we define a special type of partition on the set
\mathbb{X}
, called a
screen
, that “plays nice” with
\Gamma
and
\sim
(Definition
1.12
).
The elements of a screen are called
pixels
.
We prove that the set
\mathscr{P}
of screens on a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
has at least one maximal element (Proposition
1.16
) and show that triples and screens behave well with respect to products (Propositions
1.10
and
1.25
).

In Section
2
we study path categories (Definition
2.1
).
A
path category

C
is constructed from a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.
The objects are points in
\mathbb{X}
and morphisms are equivalence classes
[\gamma]
of paths in
\Gamma
using the relation
\sim
.
The category
\boldsymbol{PCat}
is the subcategory of
\boldsymbol{Cat}
(the category of small categories) whose objects are path categories and whose morphisms are functors between path categories.
We also define a
\Bbbk
-linear version
\mathcal{C}
, for a commutative ring
\Bbbk
.
A
path-based ideal

\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{C}
is an ideal that behaves well with respect to paths (Definition
2.8
).
The quotient
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{I}
is written
\mathcal{A}
and is the object of study in the
\Bbbk
-linear case.

We show that, given a screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
, there is an induced class
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
of morphisms in
C
that admits a calculus of fractions (Proposition
2.17
).
The localization
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}=C[\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}^{-1}]
is called a
pixelation
(Definition
2.20
).
We note that pixelation depends on the choice of triple and ask when is the localization of a path category is a pixelation with respect to a triple and a screen (Remark
2.23
and Question
2.24
).

In
\mathcal{C}
we expand
\mathcal{I}
to an ideal
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
compatible with
\mathfrak{P}
and take a further quotient
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}=\mathcal{C}/{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Notice
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
is also a quotient of
\mathcal{A}
.
In this case we also have an induced class of morphisms
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
that admits a calculus of fractions (Proposition
2.19
).
We also call the localization
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}={\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}[\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}^{-1}]
a pixelation.

Given a screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
, we construct categories
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
equivalent to
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, respectively (Theorem
2.37
).
Each of
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
are its own respective skeleton and are constructed from quivers.

We essentially prove that pixelation yields a new path category, in the non-
\Bbbk
-linear case.

Theorem A

(Theorem
2.43
)
.

Let
C
be the path category constructed from
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
and let
\mathfrak{P}
be a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.
Then
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
is isomorphic to a path category and so
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is equivalent to a path category.

We introduce finitary refinements (Definition
2.44
).
If
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
are screens, and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
refines
\mathfrak{P}
, then we have induced functors
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, since pixelation is a localization.
This yields induced functors
\overline{Q(C,{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}})}\to\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}})}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
, respectively.
If
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{P}
, then we also have a functor
\Init:\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}\to\overline{Q(C,{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}})}
(Proposition
2.45
).

Representations

In Section
3
we study representations of path categories with values in a
\Bbbk
-linear abelian category
\mathcal{K}
.
A
representation of
\mathcal{A}
with values in
\mathcal{K}
is a functor
M:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{K}
(Definition
3.1
).
The category of such representations is denoted
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.
We discuss the
\Bbbk
-linear version here but nearly all of the statements hold for representations of
C
.
A representation
M
is
pixelated
if there is a compatible screen
\mathfrak{P}
and we say the screen
\mathfrak{P}

pixelates

M
(Definition
3.4
).
We show that every pixelated representation
M
comes from a representation of some
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
, where
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
M
(Theorem
3.7
).

Let
\mathscr{L}\subseteq\mathscr{P}
be a set of screens of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
such that, for any finite collection
\{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathscr{L}
, there exists a
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{L}
that refines each
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
.
The category
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
is the full subcategory of
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
such that if
M
is a representaiton in
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
then there is a screen
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{L}
such that
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
M
.

Theorem B

(Theorem
3.10
and Corollary
3.14
)
.

The category
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
is a wide subcategory of
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.
That is,
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
is abelian and the embedding
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})\to\RepK(\mathcal{A})
is exact.
Moreover, any exact structure
\mathbb{E}
on
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
restricts to an exact structure on
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
.

Sites and sheaves

In section
4
we study how pixelation interacts with sites and sheaves.
We show that if a path category
C
is a site then so is
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and the induced functor
C\to\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
is continuous (Theorem
4.8
).
We also show that a distributive lattice can be interpreted as a path category.
When
C
is a sublattice of a larger lattice
L
, we define a screen
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
for each
Y\in L
(Definition
4.9
) and prove
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}_{Y}}
is isomorphic to a sublattice of
C
(Theorem
4.12
).
The theorem may be contextualized as follows, where we write
\mathrm{Top}(X)
to be the category whose objects are open sets of
X
and whose morphisms are the inclusion maps of the open sets.

Theorem C

(Corollary
4.13
)
.

In increasing specificity:

(1)

Let
X
be a topological space and
Y
a subset with the subspace topology.
Then
\mathrm{Top}(Y)
is canonically isomorphic to
\overline{Q(\mathrm{Top}(X),\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
.

(2)

Let
R
be a commutative ring and
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime ideal in
R
.
Let
Y(\mathfrak{p})=\{q\in\mathrm{Spec}(R)\mid\mathfrak{q}\subset\mathfrak{p}\}
.
Then
\mathrm{Top}(\mathrm{Spec}(R_{\mathfrak{p}}))
is canonically isomorphic to
\overline{Q(\mathrm{Top}(\mathrm{Spec}(R)),\mathfrak{P}_{Y(\mathfrak{p})})}
.
That is, we may view
\mathrm{Top}(\mathrm{Spec}(R_{\mathfrak{p}}))
as a pixelation of
\mathrm{Top}(\mathrm{Spec}(R))
.

We also present a parallel story to ringed sites
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
and
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules.
We define
pathed sites

(E,\mathcal{O}_{E})
to be a site
E
with a sheaf of path categories
\mathcal{O}_{E}
.
As the “standard” example, we show that if
\mathscr{P}
admits a subcategory
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
that is a site (Definition
4.15
), then there is a sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
on
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
where a screen
\mathfrak{P}
is sent to
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and the morphisms are the
\Init
functors from Section
2
.

The parallel to an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module is an
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-representation
(Definition
4.20
).
Similar requirements for an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module are axiomized in a way that works with representations in our setting (functors) without running into trouble with foundations.
We provide a “classical” example of an
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-representation (Example
4.21
) and an example of an
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
-representation, where
\mathscr{P}
is the set of screens of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(Example
4.23
).

Higher Auslander categories

In Section
5
we present an application of pixelation to higher homological algebra.
Specifically, we look at higher Auslander categories, assume
\Bbbk
is a field, and
\mathcal{K}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{vec}
, the category of finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector spaces.
We provide a continuous version of the story in
[
14
]
, using some perspective from
[
12
]
.
We define the
higher Auslander category of type
A
, denoted
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
, for any
n\geq 1
(Definition
5.10
).

In this setting we consider finitely-presented modules, which coincide with a particular
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
, as defined in Section
3.2
, with a specified
\mathscr{A}^{(n)}\subset\mathscr{P}
.
The category
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
is the category objects are functors into finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector spaces, called
pointwise finite-dimensional
, and
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
is a subcategory of
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.
For each
n\geq 1
we define a subcategory
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
of
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
and show
\add\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
is
(n-1)
-cluster tilting in
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
(Proposition
5.20
).

Since we are working in the continuum, we have to take a quotient of
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
, denoted
\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}
(Definition
5.21
).
Then we have the following result.

Theorem D

(Theorem
5.22
)
.

Let
n\geq 1
in
\mathbb{N}
.
Then
{\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}}^{\text{op}}\cong\mathcal{A}^{(n+1)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Conventions

We use
\Bbbk
for an associative, commutative ring with unit and use
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
for the category of
\Bbbk
-modules.
For the category theorists: when we say “
\Bbbk
-linear” we mean “enriched over
\Bbbk
-modules”, for our commutative ring
\Bbbk
.
When we say “a
\Bbbk
-linear functor” we mean “an additive
\Bbbk
-linear functor.”
When
\Bbbk
is a field, the categories
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Vec}
and
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{vec}
are the category of all
\Bbbk
-vector spaces and the category of finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector spaces, respectively.
Finally, the author is of the opinion that
0\in\mathbb{N}
.

Future directions

There are a number of ways to proceed with pixelation.
The first consideration is multi-parameter persistence modules.
In
[
3
,
4
,
1
,
7
]
, work is being done to understand invariants of persistence modules since, in full generality, there is no hope for a complete structure theorem.
The process of pixelation and the results in Section
3
are directly connected to homological approximation and using these kinds approximations to understand invariants.
However, proofs and careful computations need to be done in order to properly establish such a connection.
There is also the interesting case of Möbius homology (see
[
16
]
).
It would be interesting to see if the results in Sections
3
and
4
can be merged together in this particular context and, if so, in what way.

Additionally, the story for pathed sites has barely begun.
Is the category of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-representations abelian?
If not, why? What modifications to the definitions can be made so that the category of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-representations is abelian.
Assuming the category of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-representations is abelian, what is the parallel construction to (quasi-)coherent sheaves in the pathed site story?
What requirements are needed on
E
or the path categories in
\mathcal{O}_{E}
to tell this story?
What can we learn in the word of algebraic geometry by taking this perspective?

Finally, the definition of path categories in the present paper does not allow for “parallel paths,” i.e. paths whose images are the same but count as separate.
For example the arrows of the Kronicker quiver are not permitted in the definition of a path category in the present paper:
1\rightrightarrows 2
.
It should be possible to modify or augment the presented constructions to allow such parallel paths but the present paper is already
pages long.

Funding

The author is supported by FWO grant 1298325N.
Work on this project began while the author was supported by BOF grant 01P12621 from Universiteit Gent.
The author was also partially supported by the FWO grants G0F5921N (Odysseus) and G023721N, and by the KU Leuven grant iBOF/23/064.

1.
Paths and screens

In this section we introduce the two main structures that we will use in the present paper.
In Section
1.1
we introduce a coherent triple of a set
\mathbb{X}
, a collection of “paths”
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{X}
, and an equivalence relation
\sim
on the paths.
In Section
1.2
we introduce a special type of partition, called a
screen
.
We will extensively use the properties of these triples and screens for the rest of the paper.

1.1.
Paths

We begin by defining a coherent set of paths
\Gamma
(Definition
1.1
) and a useful equivalence relation
\sim
on the paths (Definition
1.2
).

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a nonempty set.
Even though
\mathbb{X}
need not come from a topological space, we refer to a function
[0,1]\to\mathbb{X}
as a
path
.
We compose paths similar to those in a topological setting.
Given
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{X}
and
\gamma^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{X}
such that
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
, we create a new path
\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{X}
given by

\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}(t)=\begin{cases}\gamma(2t)&0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
\gamma^{\prime}(2(t-\frac{1}{2}))&\frac{1}{2}\leq t\leq 1.\end{cases}

Definition 1.1

(
\Gamma
)
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a nonemtpy set and let
\Gamma
be a subset of paths in
\mathbb{X}
that satisfies the following.

(1)

Closed under composition
: If
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
and
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
then
\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
.

(2)

Closed under subpaths
: For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
and each (weakly) order preserving map
\phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
such that
\phi
sends intervals to intervals, the path
\gamma\phi
is in
\Gamma
, where
\gamma\phi
is the functional composition.

(3)

Closed under constant paths
: For each
x\in\mathbb{X}
, the constant path at
x
is in
\Gamma
.
That is, for each
x\in\mathbb{X}
there is a
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that for all
t\in[0,1]
we have
\gamma(t)=x
.

We note that we explicitly do not assume anything about
\Gamma
beyond the items in the definition.
In particular, we are not assuming any finiteness, discreteness, cardinality, partial order, cyclic order, etc.

The most natural example of
\mathbb{X}
and
\Gamma
is to take
\mathbb{X}
as a topological space and
\Gamma
as all paths into
\mathbb{X}
.
However, this is exceedingly cumbersome.
A more convenient example is to take a manifold
\mathbb{X}
with some kind of flow and take the paths
\Gamma
that follow the flow.

An interesting example is to take
\mathbb{X}
to be a poset with relation
\leq
and insist that, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we have
\gamma(t)\leq\gamma(s)
if and only if
t\leq s
in
[0,1]
.

One could also consider thread quivers from
[
2
,
15
]
.
Then, in most cases, the paths in the thread quivers are the images of the paths in
\Gamma
.

Definition 1.2

(
\sim
)
.

Given a nonempty set
\mathbb{X}
and a
\Gamma
satisfying Definition
1.1
, we define an equivalence relation
\sim
on
\Gamma
satisfying the following requirements.

(1)

A constant path is only equivalent to itself
: If
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma
is constant then so is
\gamma^{\prime}
.

(2)

Equivalence classes are closed under reparameterization
: If
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
\phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
is a weakly order-preserving map that sends intervals to intervals, 0 to 0, and 1 to 1, then
\gamma\sim\gamma\phi
.

(3)

Equivalence classes compose
:
Given
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\rho\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
are in
\Gamma
, we have
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
if and only if
\rho\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
.

Immediately we see that if
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
,
\rho_{1}\sim\rho^{\prime}_{1}
,
\rho_{2}\sim\rho^{\prime}_{2}
,
\rho_{1}(1)=\rho^{\prime}_{1}(1)=\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
, and
\rho_{2}(0)=\rho^{\prime}_{2}(0)=\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
, then
\rho_{1}\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho_{2}\sim\rho^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}_{2}
by using Definition
1.2
(
3
) above.
First changing
\rho_{1}
to
\rho^{\prime}_{1}
, then
\rho_{2}
to
\rho^{\prime}_{2}
, and finally
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
.

Given
\mathbb{X}
,
\Gamma
, and
\sim
, we write the triple as
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.

Remark 1.3
.

In a topological setting, our equivalence relation
\sim
is generally finer than homotopy equivalence of paths.
The relation
\sim
cannot be homotopy equivalence of paths if
\Gamma
contains a path
\gamma
, with
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)
and some some
a\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(a)\neq\gamma(0)
, where
\gamma
is homotopy equivalent to a constant path.

Notation 1.4

(
\sim
-equivalence classes)
.

Given
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the set of all
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
is denoted
[\gamma]
.
I.e.,
[\gamma]
is the
\sim
-equivalence class of
\gamma
.

The following example is the start of our running example throughout the paper.

Example 1.5

(running example)
.

Let
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}
and let
\Gamma
be all continuous functions
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
where
s\leq t
implies
\gamma(s)\leq\gamma(t)
.
For the purposes of the word “continuous”, we consider
[0,1]
and
\mathbb{R}
to have the usual topologies.
Let
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
if
\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
.
Then it is straightforward to check that
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
satisfies Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
.

Notation 1.6

(
\mathbb{R}
)
.

Overloading notation, if we write about
\mathbb{R}
as if it is a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
, then we mean the triple
(\mathbb{R},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in Example
1.5
.

Our triples
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
form a category in a natural way.

Definition 1.7

(
\mathbf{X}
)
.

We define the category
\mathbf{X}
to be the category whose objects are triples
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
satisfying Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
and whose morphisms are defined as follows.

Let
(\mathbb{X}_{1},\Gamma_{1}{/}{\sim_{1}})
and
(\mathbb{X}_{2},\Gamma_{2}{/}{\sim_{2}})
satisfy Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
and let
f:\mathbb{X}_{1}\to\mathbb{X}_{2}
be a function of sets.
We say
f:(\mathbb{X}_{1},\Gamma_{1}{/}{\sim_{1}})\to(\mathbb{X}_{2},\Gamma_{2}{/}{\sim_{2}})
is a morphism in
\mathbf{X}
if the following conditions are satisfied.

(1)

If
\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}
then
f\circ\gamma\in\Gamma_{2}
.

(2)

If
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{1}
and
\gamma\sim_{1}\gamma^{\prime}
then
f\circ\gamma\sim_{2}f\circ\gamma^{\prime}
.

Proposition 1.8
.

The
\mathbf{X}
in Definition
1.7
is a category.

Proof.

Suppose
f:(\mathbb{X}_{1},\Gamma_{1}{/}{\sim_{1}})\to(\mathbb{X}_{2},\Gamma_{2}{/}{\sim_{2}})
and
g:(\mathbb{X}_{2},\Gamma_{2}{/}{\sim_{2}})\to(\mathbb{X}_{3},\Gamma_{3}{/}{\sim_{3}})
are morphisms.
We see that if
\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}
then
f\circ\gamma\in\Gamma_{2}
and so
g\circ(f\circ\gamma)\in\Gamma_{3}
.
Moreover, if
\gamma\sim_{1}\gamma^{\prime}
, for
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{1}
, then we know
f\circ\gamma\sim_{2}f\circ\gamma^{\prime}
and so
g\circ(f\circ\gamma)\sim_{3}g\circ(f\circ\gamma^{\prime})
.
Thus,
g\circ f
is also a morphism.

Trivially, the identity map on any
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
is a morphism.
And, since functions between sets compose associatively, we see that morphisms also compose associatively.
Finally, recall that each
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
is a collection of sets and relations.
Therefore, there is a category of triples
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
satisfying Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
with the morphisms we have just described.
∎

The terminal objects in
\mathbf{X}
are the objects in the the isomorphism class of
(\{*\},\{\gamma\}{/}{\sim})
, where
\gamma
is the constant path at
*
.
The equivalence relation is trivial.

We now describe products in
\mathbf{X}
.

Definition 1.9

(product of triples)
.

Let
\{(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})\}_{i\in I}
be a set-sized collection of triples in
\mathbf{X}
.
We define a new triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
as follows.

•

Define
\mathbb{X}=\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{X}_{i}
.

•

We say a function
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{X}
is in
\Gamma
if
\pi_{i}\circ\gamma\in\Gamma_{i}
, for each
i\in I
.

•

For a pair
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, we say
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
if
\pi_{i}\circ\gamma\sim_{i}\pi_{i}\circ\gamma^{\prime}
for each
i\in I
.

We also write
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
as
\prod_{i\in I}(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
.

The following proposition says that we have all products in
\mathbf{X}
.

Proposition 1.10
.

Let
\{(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})\}_{i\in I}
be a set-sized collection of triples in
\mathbf{X}
.
Then
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})=\prod_{i\in I}(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
is the product in
\mathbf{X}
.

Proof.

First we show
\prod_{i\in I}(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
is a triple in
\mathbf{X}
and then we show that it is indeed the product in
\mathbf{X}
.
It is straightforward to check that
\Gamma
satisfies Definition
1.1
, so we focus on showing that Definition
1.2
is satisfied.

Definition
1.2
(
1
)
.
Suppose
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
,
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
, and
\gamma
is constant.
Then, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
\pi_{i}\gamma\sim_{i}\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime}
and each
\pi_{i}\gamma
is constant.
Then
\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime}
is constant for each
i\in I
and so
\gamma^{\prime}
is constant also.

Definition
1.2
(
2
)
.
Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
and let
\phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be a weakly order-preserving map that sends intervals to intervals, 0 to 0, and 1 to 1.
For each
i\in I
, we have
\pi_{i}\gamma\phi\sim_{i}\pi_{i}\gamma
.
Then we have
\gamma\phi\sim\gamma
.

Definition
1.2
(
3
)
.
Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\rho\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
are also in
\Gamma
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\pi_{i}\gamma\sim_{i}\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime},\ \ \forall i\in I

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\pi_{i}\rho\cdot\pi_{i}\gamma\cdot\pi_{i}\rho^{\prime}\sim_{i}\pi_{i}\rho\cdot\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\pi_{i}\rho^{\prime},\ \ \forall i\in I

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\pi_{i}(\rho\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime})\sim_{i}\pi_{i}(\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}),\ \ \forall i\in I

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\rho\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}.

Now we show that
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})=\prod_{i\in I}(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
is the product in
\mathbf{X}
.
Notice that, by construction, each
\pi_{i}
is a morphism
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})\to(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
.
Let
(\mathbb{Y},\Delta{/}{\approx})
be a triple in
\mathbf{X}
and, for each
i\in I
, let
f_{i}:(\mathbb{Y},\Delta{/}{\approx})\to(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
be a morphism.
Define
f:\mathbb{Y}\to\mathbb{X}
by
y\mapsto(f_{1}(y),\ldots,f_{n}(y))
.
We see immediately that, as functions of sets,
\pi_{i}\circ f=f_{i}
, for each
i\in I
.
It remains to show that
f
is a morphism in
\mathbf{X}
.

Let
\delta\in\Delta
.
Then
f(\delta)=(f_{1}(\delta),\ldots,f_{n}(\delta))
.
We know that each
f_{i}(\delta)\in\Gamma_{i}
, for
i\in I
.
Thus, by definition,
f(\delta)\in\Gamma
.
Suppose
\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}
.
Then, for each
i\in I
,
f_{i}\circ\delta\sim_{i}f_{i}\circ\delta^{\prime}
.
Thus, by definition,
f\circ\delta\sim f\circ\delta^{\prime}
.
Therefore
f
is a morphism and so
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
is the product in
\mathbf{X}
.
∎

Example 1.11

(running example)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>1}
and let
(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})=\mathbb{R}
(from Example
1.5
), for each
1\leq i\leq n
.
We will often denote by simply
\mathbb{R}^{n}
the product
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{R}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
in
\mathbf{X}
.

1.2.
Screens

In this section we consider partitions of
\mathbb{X}
that satisfy some conditions, called screens (Definition
1.12
), and prove some fundamental properties we will need later.

We use
\mathfrak{P}
for partitions and
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{Z}...
for the elements of the partition.
I.e.,
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathsf{X}\subseteq\mathbb{X}
.

Definition 1.12

(screen)
.

Given a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
, a partition
\mathfrak{P}
of
\mathbb{X}
is a
screen
if the following are satisfied.

(1)

Elements of
\mathfrak{P}
are
\sim
-thin
: Consider
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
,
\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
,
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
. Then
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}
.

(2)

Elements of
\mathfrak{P}
are
\Gamma
-connected
: For any
x,y\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
there is a finite walk
\gamma_{0}\cdot\gamma_{1}^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{2n-1}^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{2n}
, with
\gamma_{0}(0)=x
,
\gamma_{2n}(1)=y
,
\forall i(\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma)
, and
\forall i(\mathrm{im}(\gamma_{i})\subset\mathsf{X})
, where
\gamma^{-1}
means to do the path backwards and any
\gamma_{i}
may be the constant path.

(3)

\mathfrak{P}
has an Ore condition
: Consider the square of paths in
\mathbb{X}
:

\rho
\rho^{\prime}
\gamma
\gamma^{\prime}

If
\rho,\gamma\in\Gamma
, and
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, then there exists
\rho^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
.
Similarly, if
\rho^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
then there exists
\rho,\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
\rho^{\prime}\cdot\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho
.

(4)

\mathfrak{P}
is discrete
:
For any path
\gamma\in\Gamma
, there is a finite partition
\{I_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
[0,1]
and a corresponding finite list
(\mathsf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{X}_{n})
of pixels in
\mathfrak{P}
satisfying the following conditions.
Each
I_{i}
is a subinterval and if
t\in I_{i}
then
\gamma(t)\in\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
We allow the possibility that
\mathsf{X}_{i}=\mathsf{X}_{j}
only if
|j-i|>1
.

(5)

\mathfrak{P}
maintains equivalences
:
Assume
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
with the same partitions
\{I_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}=\{I^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and lists
(\mathsf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{X}_{n})=(\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{n})
, from (
4
).
Assume also that
\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
,
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
, and if
t\in I_{i}=I_{i}^{\prime}
then
\gamma(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\mathsf{X}_{i}=\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{i}
.
Then
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
.

If
\mathfrak{P}
is a screen we call its elements
pixels
.

Example 1.13

(running example)
.

Let
\mathbb{R}
be the triple from Example
1.5
and let
\mathfrak{P}=\{[i,i+1)\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
Then
\mathfrak{P}
is a screen of
\mathbb{R}
.

In fact, a short consideration of Definition
1.12
reveals that, for any screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
\mathbb{R}
, every pixel
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
is an interval.
The discreteness requirement means that screens of
\mathbb{R}
are those partitions of
\mathbb{R}
(as a set) where, for any arbitrary bounded interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, there are finitely-many pixels
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
I\cap\mathsf{X}\neq\emptyset
.

The “one dimensional” version of a screen, like the one in Example
1.13
, comes from the study of thread quivers and can be found in
[
15
, Definition 2.6]
.

For the rest of this section, we fix a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
.

The following lemma is a useful reduction of Definition
1.12
(
2
) that we will use throughout the paper.

Lemma 1.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
and let
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
For any
x,y\in\mathsf{X}
, there is a walk of 2 paths from
x
to
y
in
\mathsf{X}
.

Proof.

By Definition
1.12
(
2
), let
\gamma_{0}\cdot\gamma_{1}^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{2n-1}^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{2n}
be finite walk where
\gamma_{0}(0)=x
,
\gamma_{2n}(1)=y
, and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma_{i})\subset\mathsf{X}
for each
0\leq i\leq 2n
.
Consider
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
.
We know
\gamma_{1}(0)=\gamma_{2}(0)
and so we can use Definition
1.12
(
3
) to obtain two new paths
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
, where
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime}_{1})\subset\mathsf{X}
.
This gives us the following picture in
\mathbb{X}
:

\gamma_{1}
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
\gamma_{2}
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}

And, we know
\gamma_{2}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\sim\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
.
So,
\mathrm{im}(\gamma_{2}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}_{1})\subset\mathsf{X}
.
By Definition
1.12
(
1
), we must have
\mathrm{im}(\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}_{2})\subset\mathsf{X}
.
Now we have the following paths in
\mathbb{X}
:

\ddots
\gamma_{0}
\gamma_{1}
\gamma_{2}
\gamma_{3}
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}

If we replace
\gamma_{0}\cdot(\gamma_{1})^{-1}\cdot\gamma_{2}\cdot(\gamma-3)^{-1}
with
(\gamma_{0}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}_{2})\cdot(\gamma_{3}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}_{1})^{-1}
, we have shortened our walk by two paths.
We can repeat this process to get a walk
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1})^{-1}
from
x
to
y
.
Similarly, we may construct a walk
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}(\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{1})^{-1}\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{2}
from
x
to
y
such that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{0}
is a constant path, which is effectively a walk of two paths.
∎

Definition 1.15

(
\mathscr{P}
)
.

Denote by
\mathscr{P}
the poset of all screens on
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.
We say
\mathfrak{P}\leq\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
if
\mathfrak{P}
refines
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 1.16
.

If
\mathscr{P}
is nonempty then it has at least one maximal element.

Proof.

We will use the Kuratowski–-Zorn lemma (commonly known as Zorn’s lemma).

Let
\mathscr{T}\subset\mathscr{P}
be a chain.
Define morphisms
\mathfrak{P}\to\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
whenever
\mathfrak{P}\leq\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{T}
by
\mathsf{X}\mapsto\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
if
\mathsf{X}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
In the category of sets, let
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
be the colimit of
\mathscr{T}
, where we identity the elements
\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
with
\displaystyle\bigcup_{\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T},\ \mathsf{X}\mapsto\overline{\mathsf{X}}}\mathsf{X}
.
Overloading notation, we set
\overline{\mathsf{X}}
equal to this big union.

First we show that
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
is a partition.
Let
x\in\mathbb{X}
. For each
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
, there exists a unique
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
x\in\mathsf{X}
.
Then, there is some
\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
such that
\mathsf{X}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{X}}
and so
x\in\overline{\mathsf{X}}
. Suppose
x\in\overline{\mathsf{X}}\cap\overline{\mathsf{Y}}
, for
\overline{\mathsf{X}},\overline{\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
.
Then for any
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
there are
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
x\in\mathsf{X}
,
x\in\mathsf{Y}
,
\mathsf{X}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{X}}
, and
\mathsf{Y}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{Y}}
.
Then
x\in\mathsf{X}\cap\mathsf{Y}
so
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Y}
which implies
\overline{\mathsf{X}}=\overline{\mathsf{Y}}
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
is a partition of
\mathbb{X}
.

Now we will show that
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
is a screen.
We start with Definition
1.12
(
4
).
Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
and let
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
.
We know there is a finite partition
\{I_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
[0,1]
and list
\mathsf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{X}_{n}
of pixels in
\mathfrak{P}
(possibly with repetition), such that, on each
I_{i}
,
\mathrm{im}(\gamma|_{I_{i}})
is contained in the pixel
\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\overline{\mathsf{X}}_{i}
be the pixel in
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
that contains
\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Then we see immediately that
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
is also discrete.

Trick:

We will reuse the following trick.
We now show that if
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
, there exists
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
where
\mathsf{X}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
.
Let
\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
and let
\gamma\in\Gamma
be some path such that
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{T}
and notice that, since
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime\prime}
is discrete, we have the finite list of pixels
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{n^{\prime\prime}}
and corresponding partition
\{I^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
[0,1]
from the previous paragraph.
Since
X^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{X}}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
, there is some
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}\subseteq\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.
Then
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathsf{X}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{X}}
.

Now we show Definition
1.12
(
1
).
Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
and
\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
such that
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}
,
\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
, and
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
.
By our trick there is a partition
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
and pixel
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}
.
Then, since
\mathfrak{P}
is a screen,
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}
.
Thus, if
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
then
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}
. If
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}
, then by our trick there is some
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
Either
\mathfrak{P}\leq\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
or
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\leq\mathfrak{P}
.
Let
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime\prime}
be the larger of the two and
\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime}
the pixel corresponding to
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
if
\mathfrak{P}
is larger or
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
if
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is larger.
Then
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\cup\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime}
and, since
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime\prime}
is a screen, we know
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
.

Now we show Definition
1.12
(
3
).
Let
\rho,\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\rho(0)=\gamma(0)
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
.
We only show this version as the other is similar.
Then by our trick there is some
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}
.
Then, since
\mathfrak{P}
is a screen, there exist paths
\gamma^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}
where
\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
Then there is some
\overline{\mathsf{Y}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
such that
\mathsf{Y}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{Y}}
.

Now we show Definition
1.12
(
2
).
Let
x,y\in\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{T}
.
Then
x\in\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
and
y\in\mathsf{Y}^{\prime}
, for
\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{Y}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\cup\mathsf{Y}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}
, there is some
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathsf{X}\cup\mathsf{Y}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
Then
x,y\in\mathsf{X}
so there is a finite walk in
\mathsf{X}\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{X}}
from
x
to
y
.

Finally, we show Definition
1.12
(
5
).
Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
.
Assume there is a partition
\{I_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of
[0,1]
where each
I_{i}
is an interval and if
t\in I_{i}
then
\gamma(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\overline{\mathsf{X}}_{i}
.
Choose
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq n
we have a partition
\{J_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}
of
I_{i}
such that each
J_{ij}
is an interval and
\gamma(t)\in\mathsf{X}_{ij}
if
t\in J_{ij}
.
We have a similar partition
\{J^{\prime}_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}_{i}}
for
\gamma^{\prime}
.

For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
(\bigcup_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\mathsf{X}_{ij})\cup(\bigcup_{j=1}^{m^{\prime}_{i}}\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{ij})\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}_{i}
.
Thus, there is some
\mathfrak{P}_{i}\in\mathscr{T}
with
\mathsf{X}_{i}
such that
\mathsf{X}_{ij}\subset\mathsf{X}_{i}
and
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{ij^{\prime}}\subset\mathsf{X}_{i}
for each
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq m^{\prime}_{i}
.
Since
\mathscr{T}
is a chain, one of the
\mathfrak{P}_{a}
is maximal in
\{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Thus, if
t\in I_{i}
then
\gamma(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\mathsf{X}_{i}\subset\overline{\mathsf{X}}_{i}
.
Since
\mathfrak{P}_{a}
is a screen we know
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
, satisfying Definition
1.12
(
5
).
This completes the proof.

We have now shown that
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
is a screen.
∎

In the present paper, for any triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
that we consider, we will assume
\mathscr{P}\neq\emptyset
.

Remark 1.17
.

Definition
1.12
(
1
) implies that if
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)
and
\gamma
is not a constant path, then there exists pixels
\mathsf{X}\neq\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
with
\gamma(a)\in\mathsf{X}
and
\gamma(b)\in\mathsf{Y}
, for some
a,b\in[0,1]
.

The following technical lemma is helpful to prove Lemma
1.19
.

Lemma 1.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
be screens of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
such that
\mathfrak{P}
refines
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.
Suppose
\mathsf{X}_{1},\mathsf{X}_{2}\in\mathfrak{P}
,
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{X}_{1}\cup\mathsf{X}_{2}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma(0)\in\mathsf{X}_{1}
,
\gamma(1)\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
, and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
Then there is no
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma^{\prime}(0)\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
,
\gamma^{\prime}(1)\in\mathsf{X}_{1}
, and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For contradiction, suppose such a
\gamma^{\prime}
exists.
The reader is encouraged to reference Figure
1.1
as a guide to the proof.

\mathsf{X}_{1}
\mathsf{X}_{2}
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
\tilde{\rho}
\tilde{\gamma}
\rho_{3}
\rho_{4}
\gamma^{\prime}
\gamma
\rho_{1}
\rho_{2}

Figure 1.1.
Schematic for the proof of Lemma
1.18
.

By Lemma
1.14
we have paths
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
with
\rho_{1}(0)=\rho_{2}(0)
,
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{1})\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho_{2})\subset\mathsf{X}_{2}
,
\rho_{1}(1)=\gamma(1)
, and
\rho_{2}(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
.
Since we have
\gamma(0)\in\mathsf{X}_{1}
,
\gamma(1)\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
, and
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{1})\subset\mathsf{X}_{2}
, there is some
\tilde{\gamma}
and
\tilde{\rho}
with
\tilde{\gamma}(0)=\tilde{\rho}(0)
,
\tilde{\rho}(1)=\gamma(0)
, and
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\rho_{1}(0)
such that
\tilde{\rho}\cdot\gamma=\tilde{\gamma}\cdot\rho_{1}
and
\mathrm{im}(\tilde{\rho})\subset\mathsf{X}_{1}
(Definition
1.12
(
3
)).
Since
\tilde{\gamma}\cdot\rho_{1}\sim\tilde{\rho}\cdot\gamma
, we know
\mathrm{im}(\tilde{\gamma})\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
by Definition
1.12
(
1
).
Using Lemma
1.14
again, we have paths
\rho_{3},\rho_{4}
with
\rho_{3}(1)=\rho_{4}(1)
,
\rho_{3}(0)=\tilde{\gamma}(0)
,
\rho_{4}(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
, and
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{3})\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho_{4})\subset\mathsf{X}_{1}
.

Let
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\tilde{\gamma}\cdot\rho_{2}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho_{4}
.
Now we have
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{3})\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
,
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(0)=\rho_{3}(0)
, and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(1)=\rho_{3}(1)
.
Therefore, by Definition
1.12
(
1
), we have
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\sim\rho_{3}
.
But, since
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{3})\subset\mathsf{X}_{1}
, we must have
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}_{1}
also by Definition
1.12
(
1
).
But
\gamma^{\prime}(0)\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
, a contradiction.
This compelets the proof.
∎

Now, Lemma
1.19
is useful in multiple places, especially in the proof of Proposition
2.45
.

Lemma 1.19
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
are screens of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
and
\mathfrak{P}
refines
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
and suppose there exist only finitely-many pixels
\{\mathsf{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
such that each
\mathsf{X}_{i}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
Then there is an initial
\mathsf{X}_{j}
in the sense that for any
x_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i}
there is an
x_{j}\in\mathsf{X}_{j}
and a path
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
\gamma(0)=x_{j}
and
\gamma(1)=x_{i}
.

Proof.

We start with
\{\mathsf{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathfrak{P}
and systematically remove pixels
\mathsf{X}_{i}
if there is a pixel
\mathsf{X}_{j}
with a path
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma(0)\in\mathsf{X}_{j}
and
\gamma(1)\in\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
The last pixel remaining must be the initial pixel among those that are subsets of
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, by Lemma
1.18
.

First consider
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
.
If there is a path
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
\gamma(0)\in\mathsf{X}_{i}
,
\gamma(1)\in\mathsf{X}_{3-i}
, and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, then we keep
\mathsf{X}_{i}
and remove
\mathsf{X}_{3-i}
.
By Lemma
1.18
, we know there cannot be a path from
\mathsf{X}_{3-i}
to
\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
We now have a subset of
\mathfrak{P}
with
n-1
pixels.

If instead there is no such
\gamma
, let
x_{1}\in\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
x_{2}\in\mathsf{X}_{2}
.
Since
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is a screen, there is some
x_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
and paths
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\Gamma
with
\rho_{1}(0)=\rho_{2}(0)=x_{i}
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{1})\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho_{2})\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
Then we remove both
\mathsf{X}_{1}
and
\mathsf{X}_{2}
.
We now have a subset of
\mathfrak{P}
with
n-2
pixels.

In both cases we now have fewer pixels and repeat the process at most
n-1
times.
The last remaining pixel is the initial pixel as desired.
∎

We have the following equivalence relation on paths, relative to a screen
\mathfrak{P}
.

Definition 1.20

(
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent paths)
.

We say two paths
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
are
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent
if there exists
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3},\rho_{4}\in\Gamma
where
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{1})\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho_{2})\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
,
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{3})\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho_{4})\subset\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
, and
\rho_{1}\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho_{3}\sim\rho_{2}\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho_{4}
.
Notice that if
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
(in particular if
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
) then
\gamma
is
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent to
\gamma^{\prime}
, by taking
\rho_{i}
to be the identity for each
i
.

Using Lemma
1.14
, it is straight forward to show that
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalence is indeed an equivalence relation.

Sometimes we will consider a partition
\mathfrak{P}
of a set
\mathbb{X}
to be the induced surjection
\mathbb{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
in the category of sets.
This point of view is useful to define the following operations, for example.

Definition 1.21
.

(meet and join of partitions)
Let
\mathbb{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathbb{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
be partitions of a set
\mathbb{X}
.

We define the meet of the partitions to be

\mathfrak{P}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}:=\{\mathsf{X}\cap\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\mid\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P},\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\}.

We define the join of the partitions to be the pushout in the following diagram (in the category of sets):

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}.}

The following proposition follows from straightforward set theory.

Proposition 1.22
.

The operations
\sqcap
and
\sqcup
make the set of partitions of a set
\mathbb{X}
into a lattice.

Remark 1.23
.

In general, it is
not
true that
\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
implies either
\mathfrak{P}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
or
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
.
This must be done on a case-by-case basis.

However, if it is true that
\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
implies
\mathfrak{P}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}^{\prime},\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
, then
\mathscr{P}
is a lattice and there is a unique maximal element in
\mathscr{P}
(using Proposition
1.16
).

Definition 1.24
.

(product of screens)
Let
\{(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a finite collection of triples in
\mathbf{X}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
be a screen of
(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
.
The
product of screens
,
\mathfrak{P}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}
is defined as

\left\{\left.\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}\right|\mathsf{X}_{i}\in\mathfrak{P}_{i}\right\}.

Proposition 1.25
.

Let
\{(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a finite collection of triples in
\mathbf{X}
and, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
be a screen of
(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
.

(1)

We have
\mathfrak{P}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}
is a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{Q}
is a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
then
\mathfrak{Q}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{Q}_{i}
where each
\mathfrak{Q}_{i}=\{\pi_{i}\mathsf{X}\mid\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{Q}\}
is a screen of
(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})
, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

We prove statement (
1
) in the proposition.
For each of the items in Definition
1.12
, we can reverse the arguments presented to show statement (
2
) in the proposition.
Thus, we suppress the proof of statement (
2
).

It follows immediately that
\mathfrak{P}
is a partition of
\mathbb{X}
.
We now show that
\mathfrak{P}
satisfies Definition
1.12
.

Defintiion
1.12
(
1
)
.
Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
,
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
, and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, where
\mathsf{X}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
and each
\mathsf{X}_{i}\in\mathfrak{P}_{i}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\pi_{i}\gamma\sim_{i}\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime},\ \ 1\leq i\leq n

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{im}(\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}_{i},\ \ 1\leq i\leq n

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\pi_{i}(\mathrm{im}\gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}_{i},\ \ 1\leq i\leq n

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}.

Defintiion
1.12
(
2
)
.
Let
x,y\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, where
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
.
Then, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
By Lemma
1.14
, we have a walk
(\rho_{i})^{-1}\gamma_{i}
where
\rho_{i}(1)=x_{i}
,
\gamma_{i}(1)=y_{i}
,
\rho_{i}(0)=\gamma_{i}(0)
, and
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{i})\cup\mathrm{im}(\gamma_{i})\subset\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Define
\rho,\gamma:[0,1]\rightrightarrows\mathbb{X}
to the unique function such that
\pi_{i}\rho=\rho_{i}
and
\pi_{i}\gamma=\gamma_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
.
Then
\rho^{-1}\gamma
is a finite walk from
x
to
y
in
\mathsf{X}
.

Defintiion
1.12
(
3
)
.
We prove the statement where we start with
\rho
and
\gamma
as the proof of the dual statement is similar.
Let
\rho,\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\rho(0)=\gamma(0)
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq n
, we have
\pi_{i}\rho(0)=\pi_{i}\gamma(0)
and
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{i}\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Then, we have
\rho^{\prime}_{i},\gamma^{\prime}_{i}\in\Gamma_{i}
such that
\pi_{i}\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}_{i}\sim_{i}\pi_{i}\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime}_{i})\subset\mathsf{Y}_{i}
, for some
\mathsf{Y}_{i}\in\mathfrak{P}_{i}
.
Let
\rho^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}:[0,1]\rightrightarrows\mathbb{X}
be the unique functions such that
\pi_{i}\rho^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}_{i}
and
\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
.
Then,
\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{Y}_{i}
.

Definition
1.12
(
4
)
.
Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
For each
1\leq i\leq n
, there is a partition
\mathfrak{I}_{i}=\{I_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}
of
[0,1]
where each
I_{ij}
is an interval and
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{i}\gamma)|_{I_{ij}}
is contained in
\mathsf{X}_{ij}\in\mathfrak{P}_{i}
.
Since we have finitely many partitions
\{I_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}
, we may use Proposition
1.22
and obtain
\mathfrak{I}=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{I}_{i}
.
Since each
\mathfrak{I}_{i}
is finite and we have a finite collection,
\mathfrak{I}
is also a finite partition.
Let
\bigcap_{i}^{n}I_{ij}
be nonempty, where
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
for each
1\leq i\leq n
.
Then
\mathrm{im}(\pi_{i}\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}_{ij}
for each
I_{ij}
.
So,
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{ij}
on
\bigcap_{i}^{n}I_{ij}
.

Definition
1.12
(
5
)
.
Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
and assume there is a partition
\{I_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
of
[0,1]
such that each
I_{j}
is an interval and if
t\in I_{j}
then
\gamma(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\mathsf{X}_{j}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{ij}
.
Then, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, and each
1\leq j\leq m
, if
t\in I_{j}
then
\pi_{i}\gamma(t),\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime}(t)\in\mathsf{X}_{ij}
.
Thus, since each
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
is a screen,
\pi_{i}\gamma\sim_{i}\pi_{i}\gamma^{\prime}
.
By definition, we have
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
.
∎

Notice that we needed
\{(\mathbb{X}_{i},\Gamma_{i}{/}{\sim_{i}})\}_{i=1}^{n}
to be a finite collection near the end of the above proof.
For those interested in screens without the dicrete requirement, one may drop the finite-ness requirement and instead work with
\{(\mathbb{X}_{\alpha},\Gamma_{\alpha}{/}{\sim_{\alpha}})\}_{\alpha}
and
\{\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha}\}
for some indexing set
\{\alpha\}
.
However, in the present paper, we use the finite-nes requirement.

Example 1.26

(running example)
.

Let
\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the triple from Example
1.11
.
Then any screen of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a product of screens of
\mathbb{R}
as in Example
1.13
.
Specifically, if
\mathfrak{P}
is a screen of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
then each pixel
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
is a product of intervals
I_{1}\times I_{2}\times\cdots\times I_{n}
.

Although we defined
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
to be a pushout in the category of sets, there is an alternate construction that is useful for computations.

Definition 1.27

(join complex)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
be partitions of a set
\mathbb{X}
.
We now construct a CW-complex
\mathbb{Y}
.
Let
\mathbb{Y}_{0}=\mathfrak{P}\amalg\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
(where we take the disjoint union in the category of sets).
For each
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{X}\cap\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, we add a 1-cell from
\mathsf{X}
to
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{Y}_{1}
.
The
join complex of
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is the CW-complex
\mathbb{Y}
whose 0-cells are
\mathbb{Y}_{0}
and whose 1-cells are
\mathbb{Y}_{1}
.

Remark 1.28
.

For each
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
there is at least one
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{X}\cap\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and vice verse.

It is possible to form a join complex from any finite collection
\{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of screens by taking
\mathbb{Y}_{0}
to be
\coprod_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}
and adding a 1-cell for each pairwise intersection of pixels
\mathsf{X}_{i}
and
\mathsf{X}_{j}
.

Denote by
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{0}(\mathbb{X})
the 0th homotopy group of a topological space
\mathbb{X}
, which is equivalently the set of connected components of
\mathbb{X}
.

The following proposition can be generalized to a finite join complex in the obvious way.

Proposition 1.29
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
be partitions of a set
\mathbb{X}
and let
\mathbb{Y}
be the join complex of
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{0}(\mathbb{Y})
is in bijection with the elements of
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
p:\mathbb{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{P}
and
p^{\prime}:\mathbb{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
be the surjections given by
x\mapsto\mathsf{X}\ni x
and
x\mapsto\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\ni x
, respectively.
Let
f:\mathfrak{P}\to\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}:\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
be the induced maps since
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is a pushout.

Notice that we have a surjection
h:\mathfrak{P}\twoheadrightarrow\boldsymbol{\pi}_{0}(\mathbb{Y})
where
\mathsf{X}
is sent to the connected component of
\mathbb{Y}
containing the point
\mathsf{X}\in\mathbb{Y}_{0}
and similarly we have
h^{\prime}:\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\boldsymbol{\pi}_{0}(\mathbb{Y})
(follows immediately from Remark
1.28
).
Let
x\in\mathbb{X}
,
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, and
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
such that
x\in\mathsf{X}\cap\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
Then there is a 1-cell in
\mathbb{Y}_{1}
from
\mathsf{X}
to
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
Thus,
hp=h^{\prime}p^{\prime}
and so, since
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is a colimit, there exists a unique map
g:\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\to\boldsymbol{\pi}_{0}(\mathbb{Y})
such that
h=gf
and
h^{\prime}=gf^{\prime}
:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{h}
-

-
