-
\crvi
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
\textstyle{\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!g}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{0}(\mathbb{Y}).}

The function
g
must be surjective since
h
and
h^{\prime}
are surjective.

Let
\mathsf{X}\sqcup\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{Y}\sqcup\mathsf{Y}^{\prime}
be elements of
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
and suppose
g(\mathsf{X}\sqcup\mathsf{X}^{\prime})=g(\mathsf{Y}\sqcup\mathsf{Y}^{\prime})
.
Then, since
h=gf
, there is a continuous path
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{Y}
such that
\gamma(0)=\mathsf{X}
and
\gamma(1)=\mathsf{Y}
.
Since
\mathbb{Y}
is a CW-complex,
\gamma
may only traverse finitely-many 1-cells.
Let
t_{0}=0
and let
s_{1}\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma|_{[t_{0},s_{1}]}
traverses a 1-cell from
\mathsf{X}_{0}=\mathsf{X}
to some
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{1}
.
Let
t_{1}\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma|_{[s_{1},t_{1}]}
traverses a 1-cell from
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{1}
to
\mathsf{X}_{1}
.
Proceed inductively until we arrive at
\gamma|_{[s_{n},t_{1}=1]}
traverses a 1-cell from
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{n}
to
\mathsf{X}_{n}=\mathsf{Y}
.

Now we have
f(\mathsf{X}_{i-1})=f^{\prime}(\mathsf{X}_{i})=f(\mathsf{X}_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq n
.
In particular,
f(\mathsf{X})=f^{\prime}(\mathsf{Y})
and so
\mathsf{X}\sqcup\mathsf{X}^{\prime}=\mathsf{Y}\sqcup\mathsf{Y}^{\prime}
.
Therefore
g
is injective and so bijective.
∎

Notice that if
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
are
0
-cells that are in the same connected component of
\mathbb{Y}
then
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
are subsets of the same pixel
\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.

2.
Path Categories

In this section we relate a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
to a path category
C
and
\Bbbk
-linear version
\mathcal{C}
, for a commutative ring
\Bbbk
(Definition
2.1
).
For the
\Bbbk
-linear version, we also allow a ideal
\mathcal{I}
generated by paths in
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
(Definition
2.8
) and consider
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{C}
.
We use the screens from Section
1.2
to construct special localizations of
C
and of
\mathcal{A}
called
pixelations
, the titular construction of the present paper.
After showing that pixelations are related to quotients of categories from quivers (Theorem
2.37
), we prove a few more useful properties about them.

Fix a commutative ring
\Bbbk
for the rest of Section
2
.

2.1.
Calculus of fractions and pixelation

In this section we will define path categories (Definition
2.1
) and show how to obtain a calculus of fractions from a screen (Propositions
2.19
and
2.19
).
The localization with respect to this special calculus of fractions is the titular
pixelation
.

Definition 2.1

(path category)
.

The
path category

C
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
is the category whose objects are
\mathbb{X}
and whose morphisms are given by

\Hom_{C}(x,y)=\{[\gamma]\in\Gamma\mid\gamma(0)=x,\gamma(1)=y\}.

The
\Bbbk
-linear path category

\mathcal{C}
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
is the category whose objects are
\mathbb{X}\coprod\{0\}
and whose morphisms are given by

\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)=\begin{cases}\Bbbk\langle\{[\gamma]\in\Gamma\mid\gamma(0)=x,\gamma(1)=y\}\rangle&x,y\in\mathbb{X}\\
0&x=0\text{ or }y=0.\end{cases}

That is,
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
is the
\Bbbk
-linearization of
C
with a
0
object.

Example 2.2

(running example)
.

Let
\mathbb{R}
be as in Example
1.5
.
We consider
C=\mathbb{R}
as a path category where the objects are the real numbers and

\Hom_{C}(x,y)=\begin{cases}\{*\}&x\leq y\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

This is also an example of a continuous quiver of type
A
as in
[
10
]
.

For the
\Bbbk
-linear version
\mathcal{C}
, we have the same objects.
The
\Hom
-modules are given by:

\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)=\begin{cases}\Bbbk&x\leq y\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

When
\Bbbk
is a field,
\mathcal{C}
is a spectroid if and only if
|\Hom_{C}(x,y)|<\infty
for all ordered pairs
(x,y)\in\mathbb{X}^{2}
.
This leads to the following conjecture, which falls outside the scope of the present paper.

Conjecture 2.3
.

For every spectroid
\mathcal{C}
, there exists a choice of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
such that
\mathcal{C}
is the
\Bbbk
-linear path category of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Given
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
, the
C,\mathcal{C}
in Definition
2.1
are indeed categories.

Proof.

We prove the result for
C
since
\mathcal{C}
is the
\Bbbk
-linearization of
C
with a
0
object.
By Definition
1.1
(
3
) we know that for each
x\in\mathbb{X}
the equivalence class of the constant path at
x
is the identity on
x
in
\mathcal{C}
.
Using Definition
1.1
(
1
) and Definition
1.2
(
3
) we have that if
\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}
then
[\gamma^{\prime}]\circ[\gamma]=[\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}]
.
By Definitions
1.1
(
2
) and
1.2
(
2
,
3
) we know that composition is associative.
∎

We will, of course, be interested in the category of path categories and functors between them.

Definition 2.5

(
\boldsymbol{PCat}
)
.

We define
\boldsymbol{PCat}
to be the subcategory of the category of small categories whose objects are path categories as in Definition
2.1
and whose morphisms are the functors between them.

One could also see
\boldsymbol{PCat}
as a 2-category but we will not need this in the present paper.

Proposition 2.6
.

There is a functor
\mathbf{X}\to\boldsymbol{PCat}
that takes a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
to its path category
C
and we have an injection from
\Hom_{\mathbf{X}}((\mathbb{X}_{1},\Gamma_{1}{/}{\sim_{1}}),(\mathbb{X}_{2},\Gamma_{2}{/}{\sim_{2}}))
into
\Hom_{\boldsymbol{PCat}}(C_{1},C_{2})
.

Proof.

Definition
2.1
and Proposition
2.4
show us that the functor is well-defined on objects.

We construct a functor
F:C_{1}\to C_{2}
from a morphism
f:(\mathbb{X}_{1},\Gamma_{1}{/}{\sim_{1}})\to(\mathbb{X}_{2},\Gamma_{2}{/}{\sim_{2}})
.
Suppose
f:(\mathbb{X}_{1},\Gamma_{1}{/}{\sim_{1}})\to(\mathbb{X}_{2},\Gamma_{2}{/}{\sim_{2}})
is a morphism.
For an object
x
in
C_{1}
, define
F(x)=f(x)
.
For a morphism
[\gamma]:x\to y
in
C_{1}
, take a representative
\gamma
and define
F([\gamma])=[f\circ\gamma]
.
Since
\gamma\sim_{1}\gamma^{\prime}
implies
f\circ\gamma\sim_{2}f\circ\gamma^{\prime}
, we see that our choice of representative does not matter.
Finally, since
[\gamma^{\prime}]\circ[\gamma]=[\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}]
, we see that
F
respects composition and is therefore a functor.

Suppose
f,f^{\prime}:(\mathbb{X}_{1},\Gamma_{1}{/}{\sim_{1}})\to(\mathbb{X}_{2},\Gamma_{2}{/}{\sim_{2}})
are morphisms in
\mathbf{X}
such that
f\neq f^{\prime}
.
Then there is either some
x\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
such that
f_{1}(x)\neq f_{2}(x)
or there is some
\gamma
such that
f\circ\gamma\neq f^{\prime}\circ\gamma
.
In the second case, there is some
x\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
such that
f\circ\gamma(x)\neq f^{\prime}\circ\gamma(x)
.
Then
F(x)\neq F^{\prime}(x)
from the above construction and so we have different functors, completing the proof.
∎

The injection on morphisms is sharp.
The functor in Proposition
2.45
does not come from a morphism of triples in
\mathbf{X}
.
It is also possible to generate a path category from two different triples (see Remark
2.23
).

Remark 2.7
.

Notice the functor in Proposition
2.6
takes products to products.

Definition 2.8

(path based ideal)
.

We say an ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{C}
is
path based
if
\mathcal{I}
is generated by elements of the form
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}[\gamma_{i}]
, where each
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma
and each
\lambda_{i}\in\Bbbk
is not a zero divisor.
We explicitly allow the
0
ideal also.

Notation 2.9

(
\mathcal{A}
)
.

For a path based ideal
\mathcal{I}
, we denote by
\mathcal{A}
the quotient category
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{I}
.

Example 2.10

(running example)
.

Let
\mathbb{R}
be as in Example
1.5
.
The ideal
\mathcal{I}
generated by
f:x\to y
where
f\neq 0
and
y-x\geq 2
is a path based ideal.

Fix a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
.

Definition 2.11

(pre-dead)
.

Given a screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
and path based ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{C}
, a pixel
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
is called
pre-dead
if there exists a path
\rho\in\Gamma
with
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}
and
[\rho]=0
in
\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 2.12

(
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent morphisms)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.
We say two morphisms
[\gamma]
and
[\gamma^{\prime}]
in
\Hom_{C}(x,y)
are
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent
if
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a path based ideal in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathfrak{P}
a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.
We say two nonzero morphisms
\lambda[\gamma]
and
\lambda^{\prime}[\gamma^{\prime}]
in
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(x,y)
are
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent
if
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent and
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}
.
We say direct sums
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}[\gamma_{i}]
and
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\lambda^{\prime}_{j}[\gamma^{\prime}_{j}]
are
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent if
m=n
and, up to permutation,
[\gamma_{i}]
is
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent to
[\gamma^{\prime}_{j}]
when
i=j
.

Definition 2.13

(
\mathfrak{P}
-complete ideal)
.

Given a screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
, we say an ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{C}
is
\mathfrak{P}
-complete
if, for every
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent pair
f,g
, we have
f\in\mathcal{I}
if and only if
g\in\mathcal{I}
.

The
\mathfrak{P}
-completion
of an ideal
\mathcal{I}
is the ideal

{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}:=\left\langle\left\{\left.f\,\right|\,\exists g\in\mathcal{I}\text{ that is }\mathfrak{P}\text{-equivalent to }f\right\}\right\rangle.

In particular,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.

Notice that if
\mathcal{I}=0
then
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}=0
.

Notation 2.14
.

Set
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}:=\mathcal{C}/{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.

Notice that
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
is also a quotient category of
\mathcal{A}
by looking at the image of
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
in
\mathcal{A}
and then quotienting by it.

Given a screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
we wish to construct a class of morphisms
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
C
that will induce a calculus of left and right fractions.
Overloading notation, we also consider a class of morphisms
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
\mathcal{A}
, given path based ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

First we recall a calculus of fractions.

Definition 2.15

(calculus of fractions)
.

A class of morphisms
\Sigma
in a category
\mathcal{D}
admits a
calculus of fractions
if it satisfies the following 5 conditions.

(1)

The class
\Sigma
contains all identity morphisms and is closed under composition.

(2)

Given morphisms
\sigma:x\to x^{\prime}
and
f:x\to y
, with
\sigma\in\Sigma
, there exists an object
y^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
with morphisms
\sigma^{\prime}:y\to y^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\to y^{\prime}
, with
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, such that
f^{\prime}\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}f
.

(3)

Given a morphism
\sigma:w\to x
in
\Sigma
and two morphisms
f,g:x\rightrightarrows y
such that
f\sigma=g\sigma
, there exists
\sigma^{\prime}:y\to z
in
\Sigma
such that
\sigma^{\prime}f=\sigma^{\prime}g
.

(4)

Given morphisms
\sigma:y^{\prime}\to y
and
f:x\to y
, with
\sigma\in\Sigma
, there exists an object
x^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
with morphisms
\sigma^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\to x
and
f^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\to y^{\prime}
, with
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, such that
\sigma f^{\prime}=f\sigma^{\prime}
.

(5)

Given a morphism
\sigma:y\to z
in
\Sigma
and two morphisms
f,g:x\rightrightarrows y
such that
\sigma f=\sigma g
, there exists
\sigma^{\prime}:w\to x
in
\Sigma
such that
f\sigma^{\prime}=g\sigma^{\prime}
.

Typically, a
calculus of left fractions
requires (
1
), (
2
), and (
3
) while a
calculus of right fractions
requires (
1
), (
4
), and (
5
), although the terminology of left versus right is not yet standardized.

Definition 2.16

(
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.

We say a morphism
[\rho]
in
C
is in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
if and only if
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}
for some
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.

We say a morphism
f
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
is in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
if and only if it satisfies one of the following.

•

f=[\rho]\neq 0
, for some
\rho\in\Gamma
such that
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}
for some
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.

•

f=0\in\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
where
x,y\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
for
\mathsf{X}
pre-dead.

First we show that the
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
C
admits a calculus of fractions.

Proposition 2.17
.

The class
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
C
admits a calculus of fractions.

Proof.

By the definition of
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
, we see that each identity morphism is in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Moreover, if
[\rho],[\rho^{\prime}]\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\rho\cdot\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, we must have
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
Thus, we have Definition
2.15
(
1
).

Since Definition
2.15
(
2
,
3
) are dual to Definition
2.15
(
4
,
5
), we only prove the first two.

Suppose we have
[\rho]:x\to x^{\prime}
in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
[\gamma]:x\to y
a morphism in
C
.
By Definition
1.12
(
3
), we know there exists
\rho^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\rho^{\prime}\cdot\gamma\sim\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
Then we have
[\gamma]\circ[\rho^{\prime}]=[\rho]\circ[\gamma^{\prime}]
in
C
, with
[\rho^{\prime}]\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
This satisfies Definition
2.15
(
2
).

Now suppose we have
[\rho]:x\to y
in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
[\gamma],[\gamma^{\prime}]:y\rightrightarrows z
in
C
such that
[\gamma]\circ[\rho]=[\gamma^{\prime}]\circ[\rho]
.
Then
\rho\cdot\gamma\sim\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
.
By Definition
1.2
(
3
) we know
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
and so
[\gamma]=[\gamma^{\prime}]
.
Then, choosing
[\rho^{\prime}]=\boldsymbol{1}_{z}
, we have
[\rho^{\prime}]\circ[\gamma]=[\rho^{\prime}]\circ[\gamma^{\prime}]
with
[\rho^{\prime}]\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
This satisfies Definition
2.15
(
3
).
∎

To prove that
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
induces a calculus of fractions, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
[\gamma]:x\to y
be a morphism in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.
If
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
is pre-dead and
x\in\mathsf{X}
, then
[\gamma]=0
.
Dually, if
\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
is pre-dead and
y\in\mathsf{Y}
, then
[\gamma]=0
.

Proof.

We will only prove the statement where
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
is pre-dead and
x\in\mathsf{X}
, as the other statement is similar.

Since
\mathsf{X}
is pre-dead, there is some
\omega\in\Gamma
with
\mathrm{im}(\omega)\subset\mathsf{X}
and
[\omega]=0
in
\mathcal{A}
.
Then
[\omega]=0
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
also.

x
\omega
\gamma
\tilde{\gamma}
\rho_{1}
\rho_{2}
\gamma^{\prime}
\delta_{0}
\delta_{1}
\delta_{2}
\delta_{3}
\delta_{4}
\delta_{5}
y

Figure 2.1.
In the proof of Lemma
2.18
: showing
\gamma
is
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent to some
\gamma^{\prime}
, where
[\gamma^{\prime}]=0
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.

We construct the diagram of paths in Figure
2.1
.
First we use Lemma
1.14
and the comment after its proof with
x
and
\omega(1)
to obtain
\delta_{2}
,
\delta_{3}
,
\delta_{4}
and
\delta_{5}
.
Then, starting with
\omega
and
\delta_{2}
, we use Definition
1.12
(
3
) to obtain
\delta_{0}
and
\delta_{1}
.
It is straight forward to show that any well-defined path composition of
\omega
and/or
\delta
’s has its image inside
\mathsf{X}
.
Finally, starting with
\delta_{5}
and
\gamma
, we use Definition
1.12
(
3
) to obtain
\tilde{\gamma}
and
\rho_{2}
(in blue).

Notice
\mathrm{im}(\rho_{2})\subset\mathsf{Y}\ni z
.
Define
\gamma^{\prime}:=\delta_{0}\cdot\omega\cdot\delta_{3}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}
(in red) and
\rho_{1}:=\delta_{1}\cdot\delta_{4}
(in blue).
By construction,
\gamma^{\prime}\sim\rho_{1}\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho_{2}
and so
\gamma^{\prime}
is
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent to
\gamma
(Definition
1.20
) which yields
[\gamma^{\prime}]
is
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent to
[\gamma]
(Definition
2.12
).
Moreover, since
[\omega]=0
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
, we must have
[\gamma^{\prime}]=0
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
and so
[\gamma^{\prime}]\in{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Then we must have
[\gamma]\in{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
by Definition
2.13
.
Therefore, we must have
[\gamma]=0
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.
∎

Proposition 2.19
.

The class
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
admits a calculus of fractions.

Proof.

First we check Definition
2.15
(
1
).
We note that every identity morphism in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
is in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
, by Definition
2.16
.
For
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, if
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
,
\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
, and
\rho\cdot\rho^{\prime}
is defined, then
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Y}
.
Then it is clear that
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
is closed under composition.

Next we prove Definition
2.15
(
2
). This is sufficient also for Definition
2.15
(
4
) as the statements and their proofs are dual.
Let
\sigma:x\to x^{\prime}
and
f:x\to y
be morphisms in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
with
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Note
f=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}[\gamma_{i}]
.
If
f
is
0
, then we may pick
\sigma^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{1}_{y}
and
f^{\prime}=0
(with target
y
).
If
\mathsf{X}
or
\mathsf{Y}
is pre-dead, then, by Lemma
2.18
,
[\gamma_{i}]=0
for each
1\leq i\leq m
.
Thus,
f=0
and we choose
\sigma^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{1}_{y}
,
f^{\prime}=0
again.

Now suppose both
\sigma
and
f
are nonzero.
In particular,
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
are not pre-dead.
By Definition
2.16
we know
\sigma=[\rho]
, for
\rho\in\Gamma
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume
f=\lambda[\gamma]
for some
\lambda\in\Bbbk
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
(This is because, for each summand
f_{i}
of
f
, we would find the corresponding
f^{\prime}_{i}
to complete the square and use the same
\rho^{\prime}
for each
f^{\prime}_{i}
. The result is that we take
f^{\prime}
to be the direct sum of the
f^{\prime}_{i}
’s and we obtain the desired commutative square.)
Then, by Definition
1.12
(
3
), there are paths
\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma
from
y
to
y^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
from
x^{\prime}
to
y^{\prime}
such that
\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}
.
Then,
[\rho^{\prime}]\circ[\gamma]=[\gamma^{\prime}]\circ[\rho]
(even if they are both
0
).
Thus, the axiom holds by multiplying by the appropriate scalars.

Now we prove Definition
2.15
(
3
).
Again, we do not write the proof of the dual statement, Definition
2.15
(
5
).
Suppose we have
\sigma:x\to y
and
\bar{f},\bar{g}:y\rightrightarrows z
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\bar{f}\sigma=\bar{g}\sigma
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.
If
\mathsf{X}\ni x
or
\mathsf{Y}\ni y
is pre-dead, then, by Lemma
2.18
, we see that
\bar{f}=\bar{g}=0
.
Then we may choose
\sigma^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{1}_{z}
and we are done.

Now assume
\sigma\neq 0
and at least one of
\bar{f}
or
\bar{g}
is nonzero.
In particular,
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
are not pre-dead and
\sigma=[\omega]
with
\mathrm{im}(\omega)\subset\mathsf{X}
.
Since Hom modules in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
are additive quotients of Hom modules in
\mathcal{C}
, let
f,g:y\rightrightarrows z
be morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
such that the quotient maps
f
to
\bar{f}
and
g
to
\bar{g}
.
Thus
f-g\mapsto\bar{f}-\bar{g}
.

Since
\bar{f}\sigma-\bar{g}\sigma=0
, we have
(f\sigma-g\sigma)\in{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Since
f\sigma-g\sigma=(f-g)\sigma=(f-g)[\omega]
is
\mathfrak{P}
-equivalent to
f-g
, we have
f-g\in{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
and so
\bar{f}=\bar{g}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Then take
\sigma^{\prime}
to be the identity and so
\sigma^{\prime}\bar{f}=\sigma^{\prime}\bar{g}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Definition 2.20

(pixelation)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.

The
pixelation of
C
with respect to
\mathfrak{P}
is the localization
C[\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}^{-1}]
, denoted
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

The
pixelation of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
\mathfrak{P}
is the localization
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}[\Sigma^{-1}_{\mathfrak{P}}]
, denoted
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

Notation 2.21

(
p,\pi
)
.

We denote by
p:C\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
the canonical localization functor.

We denote by
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
the canonical composition functor that factors as the quotient followed by the localization
\mathcal{A}\to{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

Notice that
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
still has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
and
C
, although some of them might be isomorphic to each other or to
0
now.

Example 2.22

(running example)
.

Let
\mathbb{R}
be as in Example
1.5
and
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
as in Example
1.26
.
Let
C
be the path category of
\mathbb{R}
as in Example
2.2
.
Then, in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
,
x\cong y
if and only if
i\leq x,y<i+1
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
also be as in Example
2.2
and let
\mathcal{I}
be as in Example
2.10
.
Set
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{I}
as in Notation
2.9
.
As in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, we have
x\cong y
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
if and only if
i\leq x,y<i+1
, for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For morphisms,
f:x\to y
is nonzero in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
if and only if there is
i\in\mathbb{Z}
such that either (i)
i\leq x,y<i+1
or (ii)
i\leq x<i+1\leq y<i+2
.

Remark 2.23
.

Note that it is possible to have the same path category from two different triples
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
and
({\mathbb{X}^{\prime}},{\Gamma^{\prime}}{/}{\sim^{\prime}})
.
The difference in path structure changes the screens and thus the pixelations.
An example for
\mathbb{R}
can be see in Example
4.11
.

This is partly explains why the injection on
\Hom
-sets in Proposition
2.6
is not a bijection.
There are morphisms in one
\Hom
-set of
\boldsymbol{PCat}
that may come from different
\Hom
-sets in
\mathbf{X}
.

Question 2.24
.

Following Remark
2.23
, how does one tell if an arbitrary localization of a path category
C
via a calculus of fractions is a pixelation with respect to some triple
({\mathbb{X}^{\prime}},{\Gamma^{\prime}}{/}{\sim^{\prime}})
and screen
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
?

Definition 2.25

(trivial morphism)
.

We say a morphism
f
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is
trivial
if
f=[\rho]^{-1}[\rho]
, where
[\rho],[\rho^{\prime}]\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.

We say a nonzero morphism
f
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is
trivial
if
f=\sigma^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}
, where
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.

Lemma 2.26
.

(1)

If
x,y\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
then
x\cong y
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and there are trivial morphisms
x\to y
and
y\to x
in
C
.

(2)

If
x,y\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, then
x\cong y
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Moreover, if
\mathsf{X}
is not pre-dead, then there are trivial isomorphisms
x\to y
and
y\to x
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

Proof.

We only prove (2) as the proof of (1) is essentially the same as the first part of (2). Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is not pre-dead and let
x,y\in\mathsf{X}
.
Then, by Lemma
1.14
, there is a walk
\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\gamma^{-1}
with
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=x
,
\gamma(0)=y
, and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma^{\prime})\cup\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}
.
Set
\sigma=[\gamma]
and
\sigma^{\prime}=[\gamma^{\prime}]
.
Then
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\sigma^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}:x\to y
is an isomorphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
This is the desired isomorphism.
Reverse the rolls of
x
and
y
to obtain the other isomorphism.
If
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
is pre-dead then
x\cong 0\cong y
for all
x,y\in\mathsf{X}
since, by Definition
2.16
, we include the 0 morphisms between objects in a pre-dead pixel.
∎

Definition 2.27

(pseudo arrow)
.

We say a morphism
[\rho]^{-1}[\gamma]
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is a
pseudo arrow
if
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)
intersects exactly two pixels
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}
of
\mathfrak{P}
, including multiplicity.
That is, there is a partition of
[0,1]
for
\gamma
, as in Definition
1.12
(
4
), with exactly two elements.

We say a morphism
\sigma^{-1}f\neq 0
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is a
pseudo arrow
if
f=\lambda[\gamma]
and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)
intersects exactly two pixels
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}
of
\mathfrak{P}
, including multiplicity.
In particular,
\sigma\neq 0
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
,
f=[\gamma]\neq 0
in
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
, and there is a partition of
[0,1]
for
\gamma
, as in Definition
1.12
(
4
), with exactly two elements.

Notice, by Lemma
2.18
, if
\sigma^{-1}f
is a pseudo arrow in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, then the pixels
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}
must not be pre-dead.

Lemma 2.28
.

(1)

Each morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is either a trivial morphism or a finite composition of pseudo arrows.

(2)

Each non-zero morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is a finite sum of morphisms, each of which is a multiple of a trivial morphism or a composition of finitely-many pseudo arrows.

Proof.

We first prove (2).
Let
f=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\sigma^{-1}_{i}f_{i}
be some non-zero morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Each
\sigma_{i}^{-1}f_{i}
can be seen as a floor
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{i}}}{{\rightarrow}}y^{\prime}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{i}}}{{\leftarrow}}y
.
Since
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is a localization with respect to a calculus of fractions, there is a
y^{\prime\prime}
, morphism
\sigma:y\to y^{\prime\prime}
, and morphisms
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}:y^{\prime}_{i}\to y^{\prime\prime}
such that
f=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\sigma^{-1}(\sigma^{\prime}_{i}f_{i})
.
Choose some
i
and consider
\sigma^{-1}(\sigma^{\prime}_{i}f)
.
We know
f_{i}=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\lambda_{ij}[\gamma_{ij}]
and so
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}f_{i}
is equal to
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\lambda_{ij}[\gamma_{ij}]
.
Since our localization is with respect to a calculus of fractions, we have
\displaystyle\sigma^{-1}(\sigma_{i}f_{i})=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sigma^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\lambda_{ij}[\gamma_{ij}]
.
Thus
f=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\sigma^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\lambda_{ij}[\gamma_{ij}]
. It remains to show that each
\sigma^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\lambda_{ij}[\gamma_{ij}]
is a composition of finitely-many pseudo arrows or is a trivial morphism.

We may now finish proving (2) and prove (1) along the way.
We simplify our notation and consider
f=\sigma^{-1}\lambda[\gamma]
.
If
f
is trivial, or a scalar multiple of a trivial morphism, we are done.
Suppose not.
We will show that
f
is a composition of pseudo arrows.
Since we have assumed
f
is not trivial,
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)
must intersect at least two pixels of
\mathfrak{P}
.
Since
\mathfrak{P}
is a screen, there is a partition
\{I_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
of
[0,1]
such that each
I_{i}
is an interval and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma|_{I_{i}})\subset\mathsf{X}_{i}\in\mathfrak{P}
(Definition
1.12
(
4
)).
Without loss of generality we assume that
\mathsf{X}_{i-1}\neq\mathsf{X}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

For each
0<i<n
, choose some
a_{i}\in I_{i}
.
Set
a_{0}=\gamma(0)
and
a_{n}=\gamma(1)
.
For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\gamma_{i}=\gamma\phi_{i}
where
\phi_{i}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
is given by
\phi_{i}(t)=(a_{i}-a_{i-1})t+a_{i}
.
Then we have

\sigma^{-1}\lambda[\gamma]=\sigma^{-1}\lambda[\gamma_{n}]\circ\left(\bigcirc_{i=n-1}^{1}(\boldsymbol{1}_{\gamma_{i}(1)})^{-1}[\gamma_{i}]\right).

All
(\boldsymbol{1}_{\gamma_{i}(1)})^{-1}[\gamma_{i}]
as well as
\sigma^{-1}\lambda[\gamma_{n}]
are pseudo arrows.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.29
.

Using Lemma
2.28
, for an arbitrary nonzero morphism
f:x\to y
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, we have the following description of
f
:

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{m}f_{i},

\displaystyle\qquad f_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}\bigcirc_{j=n_{i}}^{1}f_{ij}&1\leq i\leq m\\
\lambda_{0}\sigma^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}&i=0\text{ and }x\cong y\\
0&i=0,\ x\in\mathsf{X},\ y\in\mathsf{Y},\text{ and }\mathsf{X}\neq\mathsf{Y},\end{cases}

where each
f_{ij}
is a pseudo arrow and
\sigma^{-1}\sigma^{\prime}
is trivial.
To describe all morphisms in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, we allow
m=0
and/or
\lambda_{0}=0
.

Definition 2.30

(dead pixel)
.

Let
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
We say
\mathsf{X}
is
dead
if there exists
x\in\mathsf{X}
such that
x\cong 0
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

Remark 2.31

(pre-dead pixels are dead)
.

If
\mathfrak{P}
is a screen, every pre-dead
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
is also a dead pixel.
Furthermore, if
y\cong x
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
x\in\mathsf{X}
, where
\mathsf{X}
is a dead pixel, then
\mathsf{Y}\ni y
is also a dead pixel.

2.2.
The categories from quivers

We now show that pixelations are related to quotients of categories obtained from quivers.

We keep our fixed triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
.
We also fix a screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
\mathbb{X}
and a path based ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 2.32

(sample)
.

A
sample
of
\mathfrak{P}
is a pair
(S,\{\gamma_{x}\cdot\rho^{-1}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{X}})
where
|S\cap\mathsf{X}|=1
for each
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, every
\gamma_{x},\rho_{x}\in\Gamma
, and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma_{x})\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho_{x})\subset\mathsf{X}\ni x
.
We denote the unique element of each
S\cap\mathsf{X}
by
s_{\mathsf{X}}
.
Moreover, we have
\gamma_{x}(0)
,
\gamma_{x}(1)=\rho_{x}(1)
, and
\rho_{x}(0)=s_{\mathsf{X}}
where
x\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
Each of the
\gamma_{x}\cdot\rho^{-1}_{x}
’s exist by Lemma
1.14
.

•

In
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, we denote by
\varphi_{x}
the morphism
[\rho]^{-1}\circ[\gamma]:x\to s_{\mathsf{X}}
.

•

In
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, if
\mathsf{X}
is not pre-dead we denote by
\varphi_{x}
the morphism
[\rho]^{-1}\circ[\gamma]:x\to s_{\mathsf{X}}
.
Otherwise, we denote by
\varphi_{x}
the
0
morphism
x\to s_{\mathsf{X}}
.

Notice that the
\varphi_{x}
’s are unique, up to equivalence.

Notice also that the full subcategory of
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
whose objects are
S
is a skeleton.
The full subcategory of
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
whose objects the nonzero elements of
S
also forms a skeleton.

By Definition
1.12
(
5
), we see that any two pseudo arrows
f,f^{\prime}:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
must be equivalent.
The similar statement is true for pseudo arrows in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

Definition 2.33

(
Q(C,\mathfrak{P}),Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
)
.

We now define two quivers.

•

We define a quiver
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
based on
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Let
Q_{0}(C,\mathfrak{P})=\mathfrak{P}
and let
\mathrm{Arr}_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})
be the set of pseudo arrows
s_{\mathsf{X}}\to s_{\mathsf{Y}}
, which has 1 or 0 elements.
We set

Q_{1}(C,\mathfrak{P})=\bigcup_{(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})\in\mathfrak{P}^{2}}\mathrm{Arr}_{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}).

The source of an arrow
\alpha\in\mathrm{Arr}_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})
is
\mathsf{X}
and the target is
\mathsf{Y}
.

•

We define a quiver
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
based on
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Let
\displaystyle Q_{0}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})=\mathfrak{P}\setminus\mathrm{dead}(\mathfrak{P})
, where
\mathrm{dead}(\mathfrak{P})
is the set of dead pixels in
\mathfrak{P}
.
For each
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
and let
\mathrm{Arr}_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})
be equivalence classes of pseudo arrows in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
from
s_{\mathsf{X}}
to
s_{\mathsf{Y}}
modulo nonzero scalar multiplication.
Then
\mathrm{Arr}_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})
has 1 or 0 elements.
I2f
\Hom_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(x,y)=0
for any
x\in\mathsf{X}
and
y\in\mathsf{Y}
then
\mathrm{Arr}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})=\emptyset
.
Thus, let

Q_{1}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P}):=\displaystyle\bigcup_{(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})\in(Q_{0}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P}))^{2}}\mathrm{Arr}_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}).

For any
\alpha\in\mathrm{Arr}_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})
, the source of
\alpha
is
\mathsf{X}
and the target of
\alpha
is
\mathsf{Y}
.

Remark 2.34
.

Notice that, by Definition
2.27
, it is not possible for
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
or
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
to have loops.
It is still possible to have 2-cycles.

We can immediately consider
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
as a category whose objects are
Q_{0}(C,\mathfrak{P})
and whose morphisms are paths in
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
.
However, for
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
we want to consider
\Bbbk
-linearization.

Definition 2.35

(
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
)
.

Let
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
be as in Definition
2.33
.
We define
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
to be the
\Bbbk
-linear category of
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
.
That is, the objects of
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
are the vertices of
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
.
For morphisms,
\Hom_{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})
is the free
\Bbbk
-module whose basis is the paths from
\mathsf{X}
to
\mathsf{Y}
.
That is,

\Hom_{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})=\Bbbk\langle\{\text{paths from }\mathsf{X}\text{ to }\mathsf{Y}\text{ in }Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})\}\rangle.

We also include a
0
object in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
.

Notice that, in general, neither
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
nor
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
has the same relations as in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
or
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, respectively.
For example, if the composition of pseudo arrows
(\sigma^{\prime})^{-1}[\gamma^{\prime}]\circ\sigma^{-1}[\gamma]=0
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, the corresponding composition of arrows in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
do
not
compose to
0
.
We may have two morphisms
[\rho]^{-1}[\gamma]
and
[\rho^{\prime}]^{-1}[\gamma^{\prime}]
are identified in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
but the corresponding composition of arrows may not be same in
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
.
A similar statement holds true for sums of morphisms in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

Because of this, we define quotient categories
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
of
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
and
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
of
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
that we wish to use in the main theorem of this section.
To do this, we define a functor
\Psi:Q(C,\mathfrak{P})\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and a
\Bbbk
-linear functor
\Phi:\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

Since the (nonzero)
isomorphism classes
of objects in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
are in bijection with the objects in
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
and
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
, respectively, we need to pick out a particular object in each isomorphism class in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

We fix a sample
(S,\{\gamma_{x}\cdot\rho_{x}^{-1}\}_{x\in\mathbb{X}})
of
\mathfrak{P}
for the rest of this section.

To define
\Psi
and
\Phi
on objects, let
\Psi(\mathsf{X}):=s_{\mathsf{X}}
and
\Phi(\mathsf{X}):=s_{\mathsf{X}}
.

Let
\mathsf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\to}}\mathsf{Y}
be an arrow in
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
.
Then there is a corresponding pseudo arrow
[\rho]^{-1}[\gamma]
in
\Hom_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(s_{\mathsf{X}},s_{\mathsf{Y}})
, by construction.
Define
\Psi(\alpha)=[\rho]^{-1}[\gamma]
.

For each arrow
\mathsf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\to}}\mathsf{Y}
in
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
there is a corresponding
\Bbbk\langle[\rho]^{-1}[\gamma]\rangle
in
\Hom_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(s_{\mathsf{X}},s_{\mathsf{Y}})
, where
[\rho]^{-1}[\gamma]
is a pseudo arrow.
Define
\Phi(\lambda\alpha):=\lambda[\rho]^{-1}[\gamma]
.

We know
\Psi
and
\Phi
are well-defined on objects.
Since every non-identity morphism in
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
is a composition of arrows, we extend
\Psi
to a functor on all of
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
by using this composition.
Since every morphism in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
is direct sum of compositions of arrows (and possibly the identity), we can extend
\Phi
to all morphisms in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})

\Bbbk
-linearly to obtain a functor.

Definition 2.36

(
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})},{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
)
.

We now define the quotients
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
.

•

Let
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
be the category whose objects are
Q_{0}(C,\mathfrak{P})
and whose morphisms are given by

\Hom_{\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})=\Hom_{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})/\{\Psi(f)=\Psi(g)\}.

•

Let
\mathcal{J}
be the ideal in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
defined simply as

\mathcal{J}:=\{f\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P}))\mid\Phi(f)=0\}.

Let
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}:=\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})/\mathcal{J}
and
(/\mathcal{J}):\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})\to{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
be the quotient functor.

Notice that
(/\mathcal{J})
is
\Bbbk
-linear.

Essentially,
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
are equivalent to the images of
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
and
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, respectively.
The following theorem states that these images are actually equivalent to their respective target categories.

Theorem 2.37
.

Fix a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
, a screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
\mathbb{X}
, and a path based ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then the following hold.

(1)

The pixelation
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is equivalent to
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
.

(2)

The pixelation
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is equivalent to
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
.

Before the proofs of the two parts of Theorem
2.37
, we return to our running example to guide our intuition.

Example 2.38

(running example)
.

Let
\mathbb{R}
be as in Examples
1.5
with
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
as in Example
2.22
.
In the case of
C
, the category
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is equivalent to the categorification of the quiver
A_{\mathbb{Z}}
:

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{{-}3}}
\textstyle{{-}2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{{-}2}}
\textstyle{{-}1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{{-}1}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{0}}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots.}

In the case of
\mathcal{A}
, the category
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is equivalent starting the same quiver
A_{\mathbb{Z}}
, taking the
\Bbbk
-linearization, and adding the relation that
\alpha_{i+1}\alpha_{i}=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The proofs of Theorem
2.37
(
1
) and Theorem
2.37
(
2
) are different and so are treated separately.

Proof of Theorem
2.37
(
1
).

We will define a functor
H:{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and show that it has a quasi inverse.
For each
x\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, set
H(x)=\mathsf{X}\in Q_{0}(C,\mathfrak{P})
.

Let
f=[\rho]^{-1}\circ[\gamma]:x\to y
be a morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
If
f
is trivial then
x\cong y
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and so define
H(f)=\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}
, where
x,y\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
If
f
is a pseudo arrow, there is an arrow
\alpha:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
in
Q(C,\mathfrak{P})
that corresponds to
f
, where
x\in\mathsf{X}
,
y\in\mathsf{Y}
, and
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
So, define
H(f)=\alpha
.

By Lemma
2.28
(1), every nontrivial morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is a fintie composition of arrows.
Thus, if
f
is neither trivial nor a psudeo arrow,
f=f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}
where each
f_{i}
is a pseudo arrow.
So, define
H(f)=H(f_{n})\circ\cdots\circ H(f_{1})
.
We know that
\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{1}=\beta_{n}\cdots\beta_{1}
in
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
if
\Psi(\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{1})=\Psi(\beta_{n}\cdots\beta_{1})
.
This means the corresponding compositions of pseudo arrows in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
are the same.
Thus,
H
is a functor.

Now, we define
H^{-1}:\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Let
H^{-1}(\mathsf{X})=s_{\mathsf{X}}
.
For each arrow
\alpha:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
, define
H^{-1}(\alpha)
to be the corresponding pseudo arrow in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Then, for any path
\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{1}
in
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
, we define
H^{-1}(\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{1})=H^{-1}(\alpha_{n})\circ\cdots\circ H^{-1}(\alpha_{1})
.
Again, by construction of
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
, if
\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{1}=\beta_{n}\cdots\beta_{1}
in
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
then the compositions of the corresponding pseudo arrows in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
are the same.
Therefore,
H^{-1}
is also a functor.

It is clear by construction that
HH^{-1}
is the identity on
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
.
We see
H^{-1}H
is bijective on
\Hom
sets and
H^{-1}H(x)=s_{\mathsf{X}}\cong x
, for any
x\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
∎

In order to prove (
2
) in Theorem
2.37
, we will define
\Bbbk
-linear functors
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
that are quasi inverses of each other.
These are Definitions
2.39
and
2.41
, respectively.

Definition 2.39

(
F:{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
)
.

Given
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, we define a
\Bbbk
-linear functor
F:{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
For each
\mathsf{X}
in
\mathrm{Ob}({\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}})=Q_{0}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
, let
F(\mathsf{X}):=s_{\mathsf{X}}
.
For each nonzero
f\in\mathrm{Mor}({\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}})
there is an
\tilde{f}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P}))
such that
(/\mathcal{J})\tilde{f}=f
.
Let
F(f):=\Phi(\tilde{f})
.

Lemma 2.40
.

The
F
in Definition
2.39
is a well-defined functor and
\Phi=F(/\mathcal{J})
.

Proof.

First we show
F
is well-defined.
Let
f
be a nonzero morphism in
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
and suppose
(/\mathcal{J})\tilde{f}=f
,
(/\mathcal{J})\tilde{f}^{\prime}=f
, and
\tilde{f}^{\prime}\neq\tilde{f}
.
Then
(/\mathcal{J})(\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{\prime})=0
which means
\Phi(\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{\prime})=0
and so
\Phi(\tilde{f})=\Phi(\tilde{f}^{\prime})
.
Thus, any choice of
\tilde{f}
such that
(/\mathcal{J})\tilde{f}=f
yields the same
F(f)
, showing that
F
is indeed well-defined and thus a functor.

Now we show
\Phi=F(/\mathcal{J})
.
We see immediately that
F(/\mathcal{J})(\mathsf{X})=F(\mathsf{X})=s_{\mathsf{X}}=\Phi(\mathsf{X})
.
Now consider an arbitrary morphism
f:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
.
We know

f=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\left(\bigcirc_{j=n_{i}}^{1}\alpha_{ij}\right),

where each
\alpha_{ij}
is an arrow in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
and we may have an additional summand
\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}
if
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Y}
.

Since all of
\Phi
,
F
, and
(/\mathcal{J})
are
\Bbbk
-linear functors it suffices to show
\Phi(\alpha)=F(/\mathcal{J})(\alpha)
for any arrow
\alpha
in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
.
Notice that
\Phi(\alpha)\neq 0
, for each arrow
\alpha
, since
\mathrm{Arr}_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})
contains an element precisely when there is a pseudo arrow
s_{\mathsf{X}}\to s_{\mathsf{Y}}
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Then
(/\mathcal{J})(\alpha)\neq 0
for any arrow
\alpha
in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
.
By Definition
2.39
,
F(/\mathcal{J})(\alpha)
is defined to be
\Phi(\alpha)
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Definition 2.41

(
G:{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
)
.

Given
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, we define a functor
G:{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
.
For each object
s_{\mathsf{X}}\not\cong 0
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, let
G(s_{\mathsf{X}}):=\mathsf{X}\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})=\mathrm{Ob}({\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}})
.
For each non-dead
\mathsf{X}\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
and object
x\in\mathsf{X}
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, let
G(x):=G(s_{\mathsf{X}})
and
G(\varphi_{x}):=\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}
.
For all
x\in\mathbb{X}
such that
x\cong 0
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, define
G(x)=0
.

Let
f:s_{\mathsf{X}}\to s_{\mathsf{Y}}
be a morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
If
f=0
then set
G(f):=0
.
If
f=\lambda\boldsymbol{1}_{s_{\mathsf{X}}}
, for some
\lambda\in\Bbbk
, then set
G(f):=\lambda\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}
.
For other morphisms, we may assume, using Lemma
2.28
and Remark
2.29
, and without loss of generality, that
f
is a pseudo arrow.
I.e.,
f=[\rho]^{-1}\lambda[\gamma]
.

Then there is an arrow
\alpha:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
that corresponds to the the copy of
\Bbbk
given by
\Bbbk\langle f\rangle
in
\Hom_{{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}}(s_{\mathsf{X}},s_{\mathsf{Y}})
.
Let
G(f):=(/\mathcal{J})(\lambda\alpha)
.

For an arbitrary morphism
f:x\to y
we again assume that either
f=0
,
f
is trivial, or
f
is a pseudo arrow.
In the first case,
G(f)=0
.
In the second case,
x\cong y\cong s_{\mathsf{X}}
for some
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
By Definition
1.12
(
1
) and our definition of the
\varphi
’s, we have that
f=\varphi^{-1}_{y}\lambda\varphi_{x}
.
Then let
G(f):=\lambda\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}
.

Suppose
f:x\to y
is a pseudo arrow.
We know
x\cong s_{\mathsf{X}}
,
y\cong s_{\mathsf{Y}}
, for
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
Then by Definition
1.12
(
3
) there exists an pseudo arrow
\hat{f}:s_{\mathsf{X}}\to s_{\mathsf{Y}}
such that
f=\varphi_{y}^{-1}\hat{f}\varphi_{x}
.
Let
G(f):=G(\hat{f})
.

Then extend the definition of
G
by composition and
\Bbbk
-linearity.

Lemma 2.42
.

The
G
in Definition
2.41
is a well-defined functor.

Proof.

By Definition
2.41
directly,
G
is well-defined on objects.
By Lemma
2.28
, we know any morphism
f\neq 0
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is a finite direct sum of compositions of pseudo arrows, with one summand possibly a trivial morphism.
We will show that
G
is well-defined on trivial morphisms and pseudo arrows, then show that sums and compositions must be respected.

If
f
is a morphism between elements of the sample
S
, we know
G
is well-defined by the definition.
Thus, we consider
f:x\to y
with two cases: either
f
is trivial or
f
is a pseudo arrow.
In the case
f
is trivial there is no ambiguity in the definition of
G
since pixels of
\mathfrak{P}
are
\sim
-thin (Definition
1.12
(
1
)).
So we consider
f
to be an pseudo arrow.

We use the fact that an
\hat{f}
exists such that
f=\varphi_{y}^{-1}\hat{f}\varphi_{x}
(Definition
2.41
).
Suppose some
\hat{f}^{\prime}
exists such that
f=\varphi_{y}^{-1}\hat{f}^{\prime}\varphi_{x}
is also true.
Then, since
\varphi_{y}^{-1}
and
\varphi_{x}
are isomorphisms, we have
\hat{f}^{\prime}=\varphi_{y}f\varphi_{x}^{-1}=\hat{f}
.
Thus, the
\hat{f}
is unique and so
G
is indeed well-defined.

Let
f:x\to y
be a morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Using a description of
f
as in Remark
2.29
, we may identify
f
with a morphism in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
given by

\displaystyle\tilde{f}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{m}\tilde{f}_{i},

\displaystyle\qquad\tilde{f}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}\bigcirc_{j=n_{i}}^{1}\alpha_{ij}&1\leq i\leq m\\
\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}&i=0\text{ and }\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Y}\\
0&i=0\text{ and }\mathsf{X}\neq\mathsf{Y},\end{cases}

where each
\alpha_{ij}
is an arrow in
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
.
If there is a different description of
f
we also have a morphism
\tilde{f}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
, which may be different from
\tilde{f}
.

However, if both
\tilde{f}
and
\tilde{f}^{\prime}
are morphisms in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
defined by a description of
f
as in Remark
2.29
, then
\Phi(\tilde{f})=\Phi(\tilde{f}^{\prime})
.
Thus,
(/\mathcal{J})\tilde{f}^{\prime}=(/\mathcal{J})\tilde{f}=G(f)
.
The
\Bbbk
-linearity is then also apparent and the proof is complete.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
2.37
(
2
).

Proof of Theorem
2.37
(
2
).

We will show that
F
and
G
from Definitions
2.39
and
2.41
, respectively, are quasi-inverses of each other.
It follows from the definitions that
FG(s_{\mathsf{X}})=s_{\mathsf{X}}
and
GF(\mathsf{X})=\mathsf{X}
.
For an arbitrary nonzero object
x
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, there is some
s_{\mathsf{X}}
such that
x\cong s_{\mathsf{X}}
and so
FG(x)=s_{\mathsf{X}}\cong x
.
It remains to show that
F
and
G
are both fully faithful.

Let
f
be a morphism in
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
such that
F(f)=0
.
Let
\tilde{f}
be any morphism in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
such that
(/\mathcal{J})\tilde{f}=f
.
Since
\Phi=F(/\mathcal{J})
and
F(f)=0
, we know
\Phi(\tilde{f})=0
.
But then
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{J}
and so
(/\mathcal{J})(\tilde{f})=0=f
.
Thus,
F
is faithful.

Now let
f:s_{\mathsf{X}}\to s_{\mathsf{Y}}
be a nonzero morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
By Lemma
2.28
, we know
f
is the direct sum of finitely-many summands, each of which is finite composition of arrows, except maybe one summand is trivial.
If
\mathsf{X}\neq\mathsf{Y}
then there are no trivial summands of
f
.
If
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Y}
then the trivial summand of
f
must be scalar multiple of the identity, i.e.
\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{1}_{s_{\mathsf{X}}}
for some
\lambda_{0}\in\Bbbk
.
Let
\bar{f}_{0}\in\End_{{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}}(\mathsf{X})
be
\lambda_{0}\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}
if
f
has a scalar multiple of the identity as a summand and let
\bar{f}_{0}=0
in
\Hom_{{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})
otherwise.

Index the non-trivial summands of
f
from
1
to
m
.
Let
f_{i}
be a non-trivial summand of
f
.
Again by Lemma
2.28
,
f_{i}=\lambda_{i}f_{in_{i}}f_{i(n_{i}-1)}\cdots f_{i2}f_{i1}
, where each
f_{ij}
is a pseudo arrow.
Without loss of generality, we collect all the scalars on the left in
\lambda_{i}
so that each
f_{ij}=[\rho_{ij}]^{-1}\circ[\gamma_{ij}]
.
Each
f_{ij}
corresponds to some
\alpha_{ij}
in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
.
So, for each
1\leq i\leq m
, let

\bar{f}_{i}=\lambda_{i}(/\mathcal{J})\left(\alpha_{i,n_{i}}\circ\alpha_{i,(n_{i}-1)}\circ\cdots\circ\alpha_{i,2}\circ\alpha_{i,1}\right)\in\Hom_{{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}).

Then we let
\bar{f}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{m}\bar{f}_{i}
.
By construction,
F(\bar{f})=f
and so
F
is full and thus fully faithful.

Let
g:x\to y
be a morphism in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
such that
G(g)=0
.
Construct a morphism
\tilde{g}
in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
as in the proof of Lemma
2.42
.
Then
G(g)=(/\mathcal{J})(\tilde{g})
.
If
(/\mathcal{J})(\tilde{g})=0
then
g=0
, by construction.
Thus,
G
is faithful.

Let
f:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
be a morphism in
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
.
Then there is
\tilde{f}
in
\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
such that
(/\mathcal{J})(\tilde{f})=f
.
By Definition
2.41
,
GF(\tilde{f})=f
.
Thus,
G
is full.

We have shown that
F
and
G
are both fully faithful,
GF(\mathsf{X})=\mathsf{X}
, and
FG(x)=s_{\mathsf{X}}\cong x
.
Thus,
F
and
G
are quasi-inverses of each other.
∎

Theorem
2.37
allows us to work directly with a skeleton of a
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
or
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.

Recall
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
from Definition
2.36
.

Theorem 2.43
.

The category
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
isomorphic to a category in
\boldsymbol{PCat}
.

Proof.

We will construct a triple
({\mathbb{X}^{\prime}},{\Gamma^{\prime}}{/}{\sim^{\prime}})
from
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and show that its path category is isomorphic to
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
.
Recall that
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
is a small category; in particular,
\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
is a set.
From Definition
2.33
, every nonidentity
f\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
is a composition of arrows
\alpha_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\alpha_{1}
, where
\alpha_{i}
is an arrow from
\mathsf{X}_{i-1}
to
\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Notice there may be more than one such composition for
f
.
However, it is not possible to compose arrows and obtain an identity map.

Let
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}=Q_{0}=\mathfrak{P}
and define, for each nonidentity
f\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
,

\Gamma^{\prime}_{f}:=\left\{\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\left|\begin{array}[]{l}\exists\bigcirc_{i=n}^{1}\alpha_{i}=f\\
(s\leq t\in[0,1])\Leftrightarrow(\gamma(s)=\mathsf{X}_{i},\ \gamma(t)=\mathsf{X}_{j},\text{ and }i\leq j)\end{array}\right.\right\}.

For each
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, we define
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}}
to contain only the constant path at
\mathsf{X}
.
Now define

\Gamma^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{f\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})}\Gamma^{\prime}_{f}.

We now show that
\Gamma^{\prime}
satisfies Definition
1.1
.
By construction, Definition
1.1
(
3
) is satisfied.
Let
f,f^{\prime}\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
, let
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{f}
, and let
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}
.
Then
\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}\circ f}
, by construction.
This satisfies Definition
1.1
(
1
).
By Definition
2.33
, we know that, for each
f\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
, we have
f=\alpha_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\alpha_{1}
where each
\alpha_{i}
is an arrows from
\mathsf{X}_{i-1}
to
\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Thus,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is immediately closed under subpaths (Definition
1.1
(
2
)).

We say
\gamma\sim^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}
if and only if
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{f}
, for some
f\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
.
Now we check Definition
1.2
.
If
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathsf{X}}}
, for some
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, then
\gamma
must be the constant path at
\mathsf{X}
and so Definition
1.2
(
1
) is satisfied.
By our construction of each
\Gamma^{\prime}_{f}
, we see that equivalence classes are indeed closed under reparameterization (Definition
1.2
(
2
)).

Next we let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\rho\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
are in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Let
g,g^{\prime}\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
such that
\rho\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{g}
and
\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{g^{\prime}}
.
Assume
\gamma\sim^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime}
.
Then
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{f}
, for some
f\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
.
So,
\rho\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
are both in
\Gamma^{\prime}_{g^{\prime}\circ f\circ g}
.

Now assume
\rho\cdot\gamma\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim^{\prime}\rho\cdot\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
.
Let
f,f^{\prime}\in\Mor(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
such that
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{f}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}
.
We see
H^{-1}(g^{\prime}\circ f\circ g)
is some
[\sigma]^{-1}[\delta]
and
H^{-1}(g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}\circ g)
is some
[\sigma^{\prime}]^{-1}[\delta^{\prime}]
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume
[\sigma]=[\sigma^{\prime}]
since both
[\delta]
and
[\delta^{\prime}]
coincide for the part associated to
H^{-1}(g^{\prime})
.
By assumption
[\sigma]^{-1}[\delta]=[\sigma]^{-1}[\delta^{\prime}]
so
[\delta]=[\delta^{\prime}]
.
We know
\delta=\tilde{\rho}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}\cdot\tilde{\rho^{\prime}}
and
\delta^{\prime}=\tilde{\rho}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}\cdot\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}
, for
\tilde{\rho},\tilde{\rho^{\prime}},\tilde{\gamma},\tilde{\gamma^{\prime}}\in\Gamma
, such that
\delta\sim\delta^{\prime}
.
Specifically:
Hp(\tilde{\gamma})=f
,
Hp(\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime})=f^{\prime}
,
Hp(\tilde{\rho})=g
, and
Hp(\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})=g^{\prime}
.
Since
\sim
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
satisfies Definition
1.2
(
3
) we have
\tilde{\gamma}=\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
.
Thus
g=g^{\prime}
, satisfying Definition
1.2
(
3
).
Therefore,
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
is (isomorphic to) the path category of
({\mathbb{X}^{\prime}},{\Gamma^{\prime}}{/}{\sim^{\prime}})
.
∎

Definition 2.44

(finitary refinement)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
such that
\mathfrak{P}
refines
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.
We say
\mathfrak{P}
is a
finitary refinement
of
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
if, for each
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
, there are at most finitely-many
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
\mathsf{X}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 2.45
.

Let
\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
such that
\mathfrak{P}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
.
There there is a functor
\Init:\overline{Q(C,{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}})}\to\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
.

Proof.

1.19
, for each
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
there is an initial
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
\mathsf{X}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
.
On objects, define
\Init(\mathsf{X}^{\prime})
to be this initial
\mathsf{X}
.

Let
f
be a morphism in

.
Then there is some morphism
[\rho]^{-1}[\gamma]
in

such that

.

.

also.

.
Now we have a path

.

Suppose
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
H^{\prime}p^{\prime}([\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}])=f
,
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(0)\in\mathsf{X}
, and
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(1)\in\mathsf{Y}
.

, we have
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3},\rho_{4}\in\Gamma

.

,

,

, and

.
Then

.

) we have
\rho_{1}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}\cdot\rho_{3}\sim\rho_{2}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}\cdot\rho_{4}
.
Thus,
Hp[\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}]=Hp[\tilde{\gamma}]
and so
\Init
is well-defined on morphisms.

and

are morphisms in
\overline{Q(C,{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}})}

is defined.

.

Again by Lemma

, we have
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\Gamma

), there is a

.

and, moreover,
