.
Therefore,
\Init(g)\circ\Init(f)=\Init(g\circ f)
and so
\Init
is a functor.
∎

One can make the dual lemma to Lemma
1.19
that instead picks out a terminal pixel and define a functor
\mathrm{Term}:\overline{Q(C,{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}})}\to\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
.
However, in the present paper, the initial pixel serves us better, especially in Section
4.3
.

Remark 2.46
.

Notice that if
f\neq f^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\overline{Q(C,{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}})}}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{Y}^{\prime})
, then, using techniques in the proof of Proposition
2.45
, we see that any pair
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
such that
H^{\prime}p^{\prime}(\gamma)=f
and
H^{\prime}p^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime})=f^{\prime}
, we must have
\gamma\not\sim\gamma^{\prime}
and in particular
Hp(\gamma)\neq Hp(\gamma^{\prime})
.
That is,
\Init
is injective on
\Hom
-spaces.

3.
Representations

This section is dedicated to representations of
\mathcal{A}
(Notation
2.9
).
In Section
3.1
, we recall the definition of a representation of a category and prove some results about how screens and representations interact.
We say a representation is pixelated if there is a screen that is compatible with it in a particular way (Definition
3.4
).
In Section
3.2
we discuss abelian categories of pixelated representations and exact structures on these categories.

For all of Section
3
, we fix the following.

•

a triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
(Definitions
1.1
,
1.2
, and
1.7
) and

•

a path based ideal
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{C}
. (Definitions
2.1
and
2.8
).

We also assume that
\mathscr{P}
is nonempty (that a screen
\mathfrak{P}
of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
exists, Definition
1.12
).
Recall
\mathcal{A}
(Notation
2.14
),
{\overline{\mathcal{I}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
(Definition
2.13
),
{\overline{\mathcal{A}}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
(Notation
2.14
), and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
(Definition
2.16
and Propostion
2.19
).

Recall that
\Bbbk
is a commutive ring.
We fix a
\Bbbk
-linear abelian category
\mathcal{K}
for this section.
The reader may choose
\mathcal{K}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
to guide their intuition.

3.1.
Representations and screens

Definition 3.1

(representation)
.

Let
D
be a category and let
\mathcal{D}
be a
\Bbbk
-linear category.
A
representation
M
of
D
with values in
\mathcal{K}
is a functor
M:D\to\mathcal{K}
.
A
representation
M
of
\mathcal{D}
with values in
\mathcal{K}
is a
\Bbbk
-linear functor
M:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{K}
.

In both cases, the representation
M
is
pointwise finite-length
(
pwf
) if
M
factors through finite-length objects in
\mathcal{K}
.
Also in both cases, the support of
M
, denoted
\supp M
, is the class of objects in
D
or
\mathcal{D}
defined by
x\in\supp M
if and only if
M(x)\neq 0
.

If
\Bbbk
is a field and
\mathcal{K}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
, then finite-length
\Bbbk
-modules are finite-dimensional vector spaces.
In the literature, a pwf representation in this case is called
pointwise finite-dimensional
.
See, for example,
[
5
,
9
]
.

Notation 3.2
.

We denote by
\RepK(\mathcal{D})
(respectively,
\RepK(D)
) the category of representations of
\mathcal{D}
(respectively, of
D
) with values in
\mathcal{K}
.
We denote by
\rpwfK(\mathcal{D})
(respectively,
\rpwfK(D)
) the full subcategory of
\Rep(\mathcal{D})
(respectively, of
\RepK(D)
) whose objects are functors that factor through finite-length objects in
\mathcal{K}
.

Recall the functors
H:{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
and
H^{-1}:\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
(proof of Theorem
2.37
(
1
)).
Recall also the functors
F:{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, and
G:{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
(Definitions
2.39
and
2.41
, respectively).

Remark 3.3
.

Since
F
and
G
are equivalences of categories, the induced functors

\displaystyle(H^{-1})^{*}:\RepK\left({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\right)

\displaystyle\to\RepK\left(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}\right)

\displaystyle(H^{-1})^{*}(M)

\displaystyle=M\circ H^{-1}

\displaystyle H^{*}:\RepK\left(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}\right)

\displaystyle\to\RepK\left({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})\right)

\displaystyle H(M)

\displaystyle=M\circ H

\displaystyle F^{*}:\RepK\left({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\right)

\displaystyle\to\RepK\left({\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}\right)

\displaystyle F(M)

\displaystyle=M\circ F

\displaystyle G^{*}:\RepK\left({\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}\right)

\displaystyle\to\RepK\left({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\right)

\displaystyle G(M)

\displaystyle=M\circ G

are also equivalences of categories.
In particular, they are exact.

If the category
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
is idempotent complete and has enough compact objects, then it has the Krull–Remak–Schmidt–Azumaya property
[
6
, Theorem 4.1]
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
is small, if
\Bbbk
is a field and
\mathcal{K}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Vec}
then
\rpwfK(\mathcal{A})
has the Krull–Remak–Schmidt–Azumaya property by
[
5
, Theorem 1.1]
.
The same statements are true for
\RepK(C)
and
\rpwfK(C)
, respectively.

Since, for each
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
, we have that
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
are equivalent to path categories, we wish to study representations that “play nice” with screens.

Definition 3.4

(pixelated)
.

Let
M
be a representation in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
(respectively,
\RepK(C)
) and
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
.
We say
\mathfrak{P}

pixelates

M
if
M(\sigma)
is an isomorphism, for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
(respectively,
M([\rho])
is an isomorphism for all
[\rho]\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
).
If such a
\mathfrak{P}
exists we say
M
is
pixelated
or
pixelated by
\mathfrak{P}
.

We denote by
\mathscr{P}_{M}
the screens of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
that pixelate
M
.

The set
\mathscr{P}_{M}
inherits its partial order from
\mathscr{P}
(Definition
1.15
).

Remark 3.5
.

We make two statements regarding partitions that pixelate a representation
M
.

•

Let
M
be a representation in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.
If
x,y
are in a dead pixel of
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}_{M}
, then
M(0:x\to y)
is an isomorphism.
So,
M(x)=0
for all dead pixels
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
and all
x\in\mathsf{X}
.

•

Let
M
be a representation in either
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
or
\RepK(C)
.
Notice that if
\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
,
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
pixelates
M
, and
\mathfrak{P}
refines
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
, then
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
M
.
Thus,
\mathscr{P}_{M}
is closed under refinements.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
M
be pixelated in
\Rep(\mathcal{A})
or in
\RepK(C)
.
Then there exists a screen
\mathfrak{P}_{M}\in\mathscr{P}_{M}
such that
\mathfrak{P}_{M}
is maximal in
\mathscr{P}_{M}
.

Proof.

We prove the case with
\Rep(\mathcal{A})
as the proofs of both cases are nearly identical.
Again, we use the Kuratowski–Zorn lemma as we did in the proof of Proposition
1.16
.
Let
\mathscr{T}
be a chain in
\mathscr{P}_{M}
.
Compute
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
as in the proof of Proposition
1.16
.
We need to show that
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
pixelates
M
.

Let
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}}
be nonzero in
\mathcal{A}
.
Then
\sigma=\lambda[\gamma]
for some
[\gamma]\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
.
Then there is some
\overline{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}
such that
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subseteq\overline{\mathsf{X}}
.
By our trick from the proof of Proposition
1.16
(page
1.2
), there is some
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{T}
such that
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}
.
We see then that
\sigma=\lambda[\gamma]\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Thus, since
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
M
, we have that
M(\sigma)
is an isomorphism.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathscr{T}}\in\mathscr{P}_{M}
and so, by the Kuratowski–Zorn lemma,
\mathscr{P}_{M}
has a maximal element.
∎

Recall the functors
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
(Notation
2.21
) and
G:{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
(Definition
2.41
).

Theorem 3.7
.

Given a pixelated representation
M
in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
or in
\RepK(C)
and
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}_{M}
, there exists a representation
\overline{M}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
such that
\pi^{*}(G^{*}(\overline{M}))\cong M
.

Proof.

As in Proposition
3.6
, we only prove the case with
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
as the other proof is similar.

Let
M
be pixelated in
\Rep(\mathcal{A})
and let
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}_{M}
.
Let
(S,\ \{\gamma_{x}\cdot\rho^{-1}_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{X}})
be a sample of
\mathfrak{P}
(Definition
2.32
).
For each
x\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
, write
\varphi_{x}
as
[\rho_{x}]^{-1}[\gamma_{x}]
.
Recall we have the equivalence
G:{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
(Theorem
2.37
), which induces an equivalence
G^{*}:\Rep({\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}})\to\Rep({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

We construct a representation
\overline{M}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
directly and then show that
M\cong\pi^{*}(G^{*}(\overline{M}))
.
For each vertex
\mathsf{X}
of
Q(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})
, set
\overline{M}(\mathsf{X}):=M(s_{\mathsf{X}})
.

Given
s_{\mathsf{X}}
and
s_{\mathsf{Y}}
, we have the set
\mathrm{Arr}(s_{\mathsf{X}},s_{\mathsf{Y}})
of pseudo arrows from
s_{\mathsf{X}}
to
s_{\mathsf{Y}}
, modulo scalar multiplication, which contains 1 or 0 elements.
If
\mathrm{Arr}(s_{\mathsf{X}},s_{\mathsf{Y}})
is nonempty,
\alpha\in\mathrm{Arr}(s_{\mathsf{X}},s_{\mathsf{Y}})
.
Choose a representative
\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}[\gamma_{\alpha}]:s_{\mathsf{X}}\to s_{\mathsf{Y}}
and let
y_{\alpha}=\gamma_{\alpha}(1)
.
Then,
\sigma_{\alpha}^{-1}[\gamma_{\alpha}]
is equivalent to
\varphi_{y_{\alpha}}^{-1}[\gamma_{\alpha}]
, which we can write as
([\rho_{y_{\alpha}}]^{-1}[\gamma_{y_{\alpha}}])^{-1}[\gamma_{\alpha}]
.
Recall that
M([\rho_{y_{\alpha}}])
and
M([\gamma_{y_{\alpha}}])
are isomorphisms since
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
M
.
Then, define

\overline{M}(\alpha):=(M([\gamma_{y_{\alpha}}]))^{-1}\circ M([\rho_{y_{\alpha}}])\circ M([\gamma_{\alpha}]).

Since every morphism in
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
is a finite sum of idempotents and compositions of arrows, this defines a representation
\overline{M}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
.
For a pseudo arrow
[\gamma]
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, we have, by Definition
2.41
,

G^{*}(\overline{M})([\gamma])=M([\gamma_{y}])^{-1}\circ M([\rho_{y}])\circ M([\gamma])\circ M([\rho_{x}])^{-1}\circ M([\gamma_{x}]).

Let
\widehat{M}:=\pi^{*}(G^{*}(\overline{M}))
.
By construction,
\widehat{M}(x)\cong M(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
.
We define an isomorphism
f:\widehat{M}\to M
in the following way.
For each
s_{\mathsf{X}}\in S
,
\widehat{M}(s_{\mathsf{X}})=M(s_{\mathsf{X}})
.
So, let
f(s_{\mathsf{X}})
be the identity.

Now, let
x\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
.
In
\mathcal{K}
we have the following commutative diagram of isomorphisms:

\textstyle{\widehat{M}(s_{\mathsf{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}(x^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M(\rho_{x})^{-1}\circ M(\gamma_{x})}
\textstyle{M(s_{\mathsf{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M(\gamma_{x})}
\textstyle{M(x^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M(\rho_{x})^{-1}}
\textstyle{M(x).}

So, define
f(x):=M(\rho_{x})^{-1}\circ M(\gamma_{x})
.

To show that
f
is a morphism of representations, we need to show that, for any morphism
g:x\to y
in
\mathcal{A}
, we have
f(y)\widehat{M}(g)=M(g)f(x)
. Since every morphism in
\mathcal{A}
is a sum of elements of the form
\lambda[\gamma]
, where one
[\gamma]
may be the constant path, it suffices to show that
f
is a morphism of representations by restricting our attention to
[\gamma]
’s.

Let
[\gamma]:x\to y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{A}
.
If the partition of
[0,1]
from Definition
1.12
(
4
) has one or two pixels, then we know

\displaystyle f(y)\circ\widehat{M}([\gamma])

\displaystyle=f(y)\circ\left(M([\gamma_{y}])^{-1}\circ M([\rho_{y}])\right)\circ M([\gamma])\circ\left(M([\rho_{x}])^{-1}\circ M([\gamma_{x}])\right)

\displaystyle=f(y)\circ f(y)^{-1}\circ M[\gamma]\circ f(x)

\displaystyle=M[\gamma]\circ f(x).

Now suppose the partition of
[0,1]
from Definition
1.12
(
4
) has more than 2 pixels.
Then,
[\gamma]=[\gamma_{n}]\circ\cdots\circ[\gamma_{1}]
, where each
[\gamma_{i}]
has a partition of
[0,1]
from Definition
1.12
(
4
) with exactly two pixels.
For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
x_{i-1}=\gamma_{i}(0)
and
x_{i+1}=\gamma_{i}(1)
.
Notice
x_{0}=x
and
x_{n}=y
.
Then we have the following diagram where each square commutes by the argument in the previous paragraph:

\textstyle{\widehat{M}(x=x_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{M}([\gamma_{1}])}
\scriptstyle{f(x=x_{0})}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}(x_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{M}([\gamma_{2}])}
\scriptstyle{f(x_{1})}
\textstyle{{\cdots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{M}([\gamma_{n}])}
\textstyle{\widehat{M}(x_{n}=y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f(x_{n}=y)}
\textstyle{M(x=x_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M([\gamma_{1}])}
\textstyle{M(x_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M([\gamma_{2}])}
\textstyle{{\cdots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M([\gamma_{n}])}
\textstyle{M(x_{n}=y).}

Then entire diagram commutes and so
f
is indeed a morphism of representations.
Thus,
f
is an isomorphism and
\pi^{*}(G^{*}(\overline{M}))\cong M
as desired.
∎

Given Theorem
3.7
, we want to study all representations for which we can leverage the theorem.

The abelian category of quasi-noise free representations of a thread quiver from
[
15
]
is the category of all pixelated representations in the sense of the present paper.
However, it is not currently known to the author whether or not the category of “all” pixelated representations is abelian in full generality.
The author suspects not.
Nevertheless, progress can be made to understand abelian categories of pixelated representations.

Recall that if
\cdots\to A_{-1}\to A_{0}\to A_{1}\to\cdots
is exact in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
then
\cdots\to A_{-1}(x)\to A_{0}(x)\to A_{1}(x)\to\cdots
is exact in
\mathcal{K}
for every
x\in\mathbb{X}
.
This is true because representations of
\mathcal{A}
are only the
\Bbbk
-linear
functors.
The similar statement is true for
\RepK(C)
because we can consider every functor
C\to\mathcal{K}
as a unique
\Bbbk
-linear functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{K}
and vice versa.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
A
,
B
, and
M
be representations in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
and let
\mathfrak{P}
be a screen that pixelates both
A
and
B
.
If (1), (2), or (3) hold, then
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
M
:

(1)

A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}M\to 0
is exact in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
,

(2)

0\to M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}B
is exact in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
, or

(3)

0\to A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}B\to 0
is exact in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.

The same is true if all the representations are in
\RepK(C)
.

Proof.

We only prove (
1
) and (
3
) since the proofs of (
1
) and (
2
) are similar.
Moreover, the proofs of the cases for
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
and
\RepK(C)
are nearly identical so we prove the case with
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.

First we prove (
1
).
Let
x,y\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
with
\sigma:x\to y
in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Since
f
and
g
are maps of representations and the sequence is exact we have the following commutative diagram,

\textstyle{A(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{x}}
\scriptstyle{A(\sigma)}
\textstyle{B(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{x}}
\scriptstyle{B(\sigma)}
\textstyle{M(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M(\sigma)}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{y}}
\textstyle{B(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{y}}
\textstyle{M(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the rows are exact.
Since
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates both
A
and
B
, we know
A(\sigma)
and
B(\sigma)
are isomorphisms.
Then
A(x)\cong A(y)
and
B(x)\cong B(y)
and thus
M(x)\cong M(y)
.
Then, by the four lemma,
M(\sigma)
is mono.
But
M(\sigma)\circ g_{x}=g_{y}\circ B(\sigma)
is epic and so
M(\sigma)
must be epic.
Since
\mathcal{K}
is abelian, this means
M(\sigma)
is an isomorphism.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
M
.

Now we prove (
3
).
Again let
x,y\in\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
with
\sigma:x\to y
in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Again
f
and
g
are maps of reprsentations so we have the commutative diagram in
\mathcal{K}
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{x}}
\scriptstyle{A(\sigma)}
\textstyle{M(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{g_{x}}
\textstyle{B(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B(\sigma)}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{y}}
\textstyle{M(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{y}}
\textstyle{B(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the rows are exact.
Since
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates both
A
and
B
we know that
A(\sigma)
and
B(\sigma)
are isomorphisms.
Then, by the five lemma,
M(\sigma)
is an isomorphism.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
M
.
∎

Recall the functors
p:C\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
(Notation
2.21
).
Using Lemma
3.8
, we have the following statement about
p^{*}:\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})\to\RepK(C)
and
\pi^{*}\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})\to\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.

Proposition 3.9
.

For any
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
, the functors
p^{*}:\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})\to\RepK(C)
and
\pi^{*}:\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})\to\RepK(\mathcal{A})
are exact embeddings that restrict, respectively, to exact embeddings
\rpwfK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})\to\rpwfK(C)
and
\rpwfK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})\to\rpwfK(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

As before, we only prove the versions with
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
as the other case has similar proofs.

Recall the functor
\pi^{*}
is defined on objects by taking a representation
M:{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
and precomposing with
\pi
to obtain
M\circ\pi:\mathcal{A}\to\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
.
Let
0\to\bar{A}\to\bar{B}\to\bar{C}\to 0
be exact in
\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
and let
A=\pi^{*}\bar{A}
,
B=\pi^{*}\bar{B}
, and
C=\pi^{*}\bar{C}
.

We consider the sequence
0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
in
\rpwfK(\mathcal{A})
.
Choose any
x\in\mathbb{X}
and let
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
x\in\mathsf{X}
.
We then have the following commutative diagram in
\mathcal{K}
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(s_{\mathsf{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{B(s_{\mathsf{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{C(s_{\mathsf{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the top row is exact.
Then, the bottom row is also exact.

Thus, since
0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
is exact at every
x\in\mathbb{X}
, we know
0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
is exact in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.
Therefore,
\pi^{*}
is exact.

Notice that if
\bar{A}\not\cong\bar{A}^{\prime}
in
\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
then there is some
s_{\mathsf{X}}
such that
\bar{A}(s_{\mathsf{X}})\not\cong\bar{A}^{\prime}(s_{\mathsf{X}})
.
Then
\pi^{*}\bar{A}(s_{\mathsf{X}})\not\cong\pi^{*}\bar{A}^{\prime}(s_{\mathsf{X}})
.
Therefore,
\pi^{*}
is an embedding.

Since the usual exact structure on
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
restricts to
\rpwfK(\mathcal{A})
, so does the exact embedding
\pi^{*}
.
∎

3.2.
Abelian subcategories and exact structures

Choose some representations
A
and
B
in
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
or in
\RepK(C)
.
Notice that if
\mathfrak{P}_{A}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{B}
are screens that pixelate
A
and
B
, respectively, then any screen
\mathfrak{P}
that refines both
\mathfrak{P}_{A}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{B}
pixelates both
A
and
B
.
Thus, we want to consider some subcategory of
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
where, for any finite collection of screens, each of which pixelates some representation in the subcategory, there is a screen that refines all of them.
Notice we are not necessarily assuming that this set of screens is closed under
\sqcap
(Defnition
1.21
).

Recall we are assuming
\mathscr{P}\neq\emptyset
.
Define a subset
\mathbf{S}\subset\boldsymbol{2}^{\mathscr{P}}
where
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
if and only if, for any finite collection
\{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathscr{L}
, there is a
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{L}
that refines each
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
.
(Since
\mathscr{P}
is nonempty,
\mathbf{S}
is also nonempty.)
By a routine argument leveraging the Kuratowski–Zorn lemma,
\mathbf{S}
has at least one maximal element.

Given
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
, we denote by
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
and
\rpLK(\mathcal{A})
the respective full subcategories of
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
and
\rpwfK(\mathcal{A})
whose objects are representations
M
such that
\mathscr{P}_{M}\cap\mathscr{L}\neq\emptyset
.

Then we have the following theorem describing some abelian categories of pixelated representations.

Theorem 3.10
.

For each
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
, the categories
\RpLK(C)
,
\rpLK(C)
,
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
, and
\rpLK(\mathcal{A})
are abelian.
The embeddings into
\RepK(C)
,
\rpwf(C)
,
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
, and
\rpwf(\mathcal{A})
, respectively, are exact.

Proof.

As before, we prove the version with
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
and
\rpLK(\mathcal{A})
as the version with
\RpLK(C)
and
\rpLK(C)
is similar.

First, let
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
.
Let
A
and
B
be objects in
\RpL(\mathcal{A})
.
Then there are screens
\mathfrak{P}_{A}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{B}
in
\mathscr{L}
that pixelate
A
and
B
, respectively.
Since
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
, there is a
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{L}
that refines both
\mathfrak{P}_{A}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{B}
.
Thus,
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates both
A
and
B
.

We have shown that for any two objects in
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
there is a screen in
\mathscr{L}
that pixelates both.
Thus, we may apply Lemma
3.8
.
Specifically, Lemma
3.8
(
1
) tells us
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
is closed under cokernels.
Lemma
3.8
(
2
) tells us
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
is closed under kernels.
Finally, Lemma
3.8
(
3
) tells us
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
is closed under extensions.

Therefore,
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
is abelian.
By restricting our attention to representations in
\rpwfK(\mathcal{A})
, we see that
\rpLK(\mathcal{A})
is also abelian by combining properties of finite-length modules with Lemma
3.8
.

The exactness of the embeddings follows the same argument presented in Proposition
3.9
.
∎

If
\mathscr{L}=\{\mathfrak{P}\}
, for some screen
\mathfrak{P}
, then
\RpLK(C)\simeq p^{*}(\RepK(C))
and
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})\simeq\pi^{*}(\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}))
.

Remark 3.11
.

Since the embeddings in Theorem
3.10
are all exact, we may do the following.
Consider a pixelated
M
in, for example,
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
and a
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
that pixelates
M
.
Then
M
comes from some
\overline{M}
in
\RepK({\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}})
(Theorem
3.7
).
If
\overline{M}
is isomorphic to a direct sum
\bigoplus_{\alpha}\overline{M}_{\alpha}
, then
M
is isomorphic to a direct sum
\bigoplus_{\alpha}M_{\alpha}
, where each
M_{\alpha}
comes from
\overline{M}_{\alpha}
.
This means that if we understand the decomposition of representations of
{\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A},\mathfrak{P})}}
, then we understand the decomposition of representations in
\pi^{*}(\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}))\subset\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.
This is perspective and technique applied in
[
9
,
15
]
.

We only have “the” catgory of pixelated (pwf) representations if
\mathbf{S}
has a unique maximal element.
For example, this happens when
\mathscr{P}
is closed under
\sqcup
(Definition
1.21
).
Then, the maximal element of
\mathbf{S}
is
\mathscr{P}
.
If
\mathbf{S}
has multiple maximal elements, then some choices must be made.

Recall that for
\mathcal{D}
an abelian category, we have the additive xfunctor
\Ext^{1}:\mathcal{D}^{op}\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathrm{Ab}
, which takes a pair
(A,B)
to group of extensions of the form
0\to B\to E\to A\to 0
.
Then we may consider any additive subfunctor
\mathbb{E}\subset\Ext^{1}
as an exact structure on
\mathcal{D}
.

Since
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
is an abelian subcategory of
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
whose embedding is exact, for each
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
, we may restrict any exact structure
\mathbb{E}\subset\Ext^{1}
on
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
to
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
.

Notation 3.12

(
\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{L}}
)
.

Let
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
and let
\mathbb{E}
be an exact structure on
\RepK(C)
,
\rpwfK(C)
,
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
, or
\rpwfK(\mathcal{A})
.
We denote by
\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{L}}
the restriction of
\mathbb{E}
to
\RpLK(C)
,
\rpLK(C)
,
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
, or
\rpLK(\mathcal{A})
, respectively.

Remark 3.13
.

Let
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
and let
\mathbb{E}
be an exact structure on
\RepK(\mathcal{A})
.

•

Then we have the exact structure
\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{L}}
on
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
.
Choose
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{L}
.
Then,
\pi^{*}
factors through
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
as an exact embedding.
One can see this by noting that
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates
\pi^{*}(\overline{M})
, for each
\overline{M}
in
\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

Similar statements are true for
\mathbb{E}|_{\mathscr{L}}
on
\rpwfK(\mathcal{A})
,
\RpLK(C)
, and
\rpwfK(C)
.

•

Moreover, notice that, for each
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
, we have
\{\mathfrak{P}\}\in\mathbf{S}
.
If
\mathscr{L}=\{\mathfrak{P}\}
then
\mathbb{E}|_{\{\mathfrak{P}\}}
is an exact structure on
\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
,
\rpwfK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
,
\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
, or
\rpwfK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

Corollary 3.14
.

Let
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
and let
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{L}
.
Then, any exact structure
\mathbb{E}
on
\RpLK(\mathcal{A})
,
\rpLK(\mathcal{A})
,
\RpLK(C)
, or
\rpLK(C)
restricts to an exact structure on
\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
,
\rpwfK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
,
\RepK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
, or
\rpwfK({\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}})
, respectively.

Proof.

Combine Proposition
3.9
with Remark
3.13
.
∎

4.
Sites and pathed sites

In this section we prove results about how (small) sites and pixelation interact.
In Section
4.1
, we consider general small sites that are also path categories as in Definition
2.1
.
We show that any collection of screens
\mathscr{L}\subset\mathscr{P}
with products is itself a site.
We also show that the canonical quotient functor
C\to\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
is both continuous and cocontinuous when
C
is a site, which means sheaves of
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
lift to sheaves of
C
and sheaves of
C
may be pushed down to sheaves of
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
(Theorem
4.8
).

In Section
4.2
, we turn our attention to distributive lattices and show that a distributive lattice is both a site and a path category.
In particular, we consider a distributive lattice
C
that is sublattice of larger distributive lattice
L
.
We provide a general type of screen
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
for each
Y\in L
(Definition
4.9
) and prove that
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
is equivalent to the lattice
C_{Y}=\{U\wedge Y\mid U\in C\}
(Theorem
4.12
).
In Corollary
4.13
, we put the theorem into the context of topological spaces and, more specifically,
\mathrm{Spec}(R)
for a commutative ring
R
.

In Section
4.3
we tell a parallel story to ringed spaces and their modules in the form of pathed sites
(D,\mathcal{O}_{D})
(Definition
4.14
) and
\mathcal{O}_{D}
-representations (Definition
4.20
).
In particular, we provide the “standard” example of a pathed site.
Let
C
be a path category.
We use a particular subcategory
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
of
\mathscr{P}
, with the same objects, and define a sheaf of pathed sites
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
that sends a screen
\mathfrak{P}
to a path category isomorphic to
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
(Definition
4.17
).
We end the section with an example of an
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
-representation (Example
4.23
).

4.1.
General results

We first recall some basic definitions and known facts and then recall the defnition of a (small) site (Definition
4.2
).

Recall that a
pullback
or
fibre product
of the diagram
x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\leftarrow}}y
in a category
\mathcal{D}
is an object
x\times_{z}y
with morphisms
f^{\prime}:x\times_{z}y\to y
and
g^{\prime}:x\times_{z}y\to x
such that
gf^{\prime}=fg^{\prime}
.
Moreover, for any
w
and morphisms
f^{\prime\prime}:w\to y
and
g^{\prime\prime}:w\to x
such that
gf^{\prime\prime}=fg^{\prime\prime}
there is a unique
h:w^{\prime}\to x\times_{z}y
such that
f^{\prime\prime}=f^{\prime}h
and
g^{\prime\prime}=g^{\prime}h
:

\textstyle{\forall w\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime\prime}}
-
\crvi

-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi
-
\crvi

-
\crvi

\crvi

\scriptstyle{f^{\prime\prime}}
-

\crvi

\scriptstyle{\exists!h}
\textstyle{x\times_{z}y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We state the following well-known lemma without proof.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a small category and
\Sigma
in
\mathcal{D}
a class of morphisms that admits a calculus of left and right fractions.
Then the canonical quotient functor
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}[\Sigma^{-1}]
preserves finite limits and finite colimits.

We now put the lemma into our context.
Recall the definition of our triples
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
(Definition
1.1
and
1.2
), screens
\mathfrak{P}
(Definition
1.12
), and path categories
C
(Definition
2.1
).
Recall also
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
(Definition
2.16
) and
p:C\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
(Notation
2.21
).

Let
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
be such a triple and let
C
be its path category.
Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.
Since
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
(Definition
2.16
) admits a (left and right) calculus of fractions (Proposition
2.17
), the quotient map
p:C\to{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
preserves all finite limits and colimits.
In particular, if the left diagram below is a pullback diagram in
C
, then the right diagram below is a pullback diagram in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
:

\textstyle{x\times_{z}y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{p(x\times_{z}y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p(f^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{p(g^{\prime})}
\textstyle{p(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p(g)}
\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{z}
\textstyle{p(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p(f)}
\textstyle{p(z).}

That is, given the diagram
p(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p(f)}}{{\rightarrow}}p(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p(g)}}{{\leftarrow}}p(y)
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
, the object
p(x\times_{z}y)
is canonically isomorphic to
p(x)\times_{p(z)}p(y)
.

A
covering
is a set
\{f_{i}:x_{i}\to x\}_{i\in I}
of morphisms in
C
which all have the same target.
The empty set with chosen target
x
is also considered a covering.
A
coverage

\Cov(C)
of a small category
C
is a set of coverings.

Definition 4.2

(site)
.

A small category
C
is a
(small) site
if there exists a coverage
\Cov(C)
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

If
f:x\to y
is an isomorphism then
\{f:x\to y\}\in\Cov(C)
.

(2)

If
\{f_{i}:x_{i}\to x\}_{i\in I}\in\Cov(C)
and for each
i\in I
we have
\{g_{ij}:y_{ij}\to x_{i}\}_{j\in J_{i}}\in\Cov(C)
then
\{f_{i}g_{ij}:y_{ij}\to x\}_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}\in\Cov(C)
.

(3)

If
\{f_{i}:x_{i}\to x\}_{i\in I}\in\Cov(C)
and
g:y\to x
is a morphism in
C
then the pullback
x_{i}\times_{x}y
exists for each
i\in I
and
\{x_{i}\times_{x}y\to y\}_{i\in I}\in\Cov(C)
, where the maps
x_{i}\times_{x}y\to y
are the induced maps by taking the pullback.

From now on we omit the word ‘small’ as all our sites are small.

Let
P
be a poset.
Consider
P
as a category whose objects are
P
and Hom sets are given by

\Hom_{P}(x,y)=\begin{cases}\{*\}&x\leq y\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
\Hom_{P}(x,y)
is nonempty we write the unique morphism as
x\to y
.

The coverage
\Cov(P)
is given by the following.

•

The empty covering of each
x\in P
is in
\Cov(P)
.

•

Each collection
\{x_{i}\to x\}_{i\in I}
is in
\Cov(P)
.

If the reader is unfamiliar, we have the following result.

Proposition 4.3
.

If a poset
P
has finite products, then
P
is a site with coverage
\Cov(P)
defined above.

Proof.

Since
P
has finite products, for all
x,y\in P
there is a unique
x\times y\in P
such that
x\times y\leq x
and
x\times y\leq y
and if
z\leq x
and
z\leq y
then
z\leq x\times y
.

The only isomorphisms in
P
are the identity morphisms.
By definition,
\{x\to x\}
is in
\Cov(P)
.
Thus, Definition
4.2
(
1
) is satisfied.

Suppose
\{x_{i}\to x\}_{i\in I}
is a covering in
\Cov(P)
and, for each
i\in I
, there is a covering
\{y_{ij}\to x_{i}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
in
\Cov(P)
.
We know
y_{ij}\leq x
for each
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
.
Thus,
\{y_{ij}\to x\}_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}
is also a covering in
\Cov(P)
and so Definition
4.2
(
2
) is satisfied.

Finally, suppose
\{x_{i}\to x\}_{i\in I}
is a covering in
\Cov(P)
and
y\to x
is a morphism in
P
.
For each
i\in I
, by assumption, we have the product
y\times x_{i}
.
Because Hom sets in
P
are either singletons or empty, we see that
y\times x_{i}
is also the pullback
y^{\prime}\times_{x}x_{i}
.
Moreover, for each
i\in I
, we have
y\times x_{i}\leq y
.
Thus,
\{y\times x_{i}\to y\}_{i\in I}
is also in
\Cov(P)
and so Definition
4.2
(
3
) is satisfied.
Therefore,
P
with coverage
\Cov(P)
is a site.
∎

In particular, if a subset
\mathscr{L}\subset\mathscr{P}
is a poset with finite products then Proposition
4.3
applies.

In Definition
4.4
and Proposition
4.5
, we generalize the functors
p
and
H
, from Notation
2.21
and the proof of Theorem
2.37
(1) on page
2.2
, respectively.
In particular, we consider the composition
Hp
.

First, our set up.
Let
C
a site with coverage
\Cov(C)
such that the only isomorphisms in
C
are the identity maps.
Let
\Sigma
be a class of morphisms in
C
such that
\Sigma
induces a calculus of left and right fractions and let
p:C\to C[\Sigma^{-1}]
be the canonical localization functor.
Moreover, assume
\mathcal{S}
is a skeleton of
C[\Sigma^{-1}]
and there is a quotient functor
H:C[\Sigma^{-1}]\to\mathcal{S}
such that the canonical inclusion
H^{-1}:\mathcal{S}\to C[\Sigma^{-1}]
is a left quasi-inverse and a right inverse.
I.e.,
HH^{-1}
is the identity on
\mathcal{S}
and
H^{-1}H
is an auto-equivalence on
C[\Sigma^{-1}]
.

Definition 4.4
.

With the setup above, we define the coverage
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
to be sets
\{Hp(f_{i}):Hp(x_{i})\to Hp(x)\}
, for each covering
\{f_{i}:x_{i}\to x\}
in
\Cov(C)
, including the empty covering.

The author was unable to find a proof of the following proposition in the literature.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
C
with
\Cov(C)
,
\Sigma
, and
\mathcal{S}
with
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
be as in the setup above.
Then
\mathcal{S}
is a site with coverage
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
.

Proof.

First we check Definition
4.2
(
1
).
Since
\mathcal{S}
is a skeleton, the only isomorphisms in
\mathcal{S}
are the identity morphisms.
And, since
Hp(1_{x})=1_{Hp(x)}
, we have
\{1_{x}:x\to x\}
in
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
for each object
x
in
\mathcal{S}
.
Thus, Definition
4.2
(
1
) is satisfied.

Next we check Definition
4.2
(
2
).
Suppose we have the covering
\{Hp(f_{i}):Hp(x_{i})\to Hp(x)\}_{i\in I}
in
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
.
And, for each
i\in I
, suppose we have
\{Hp(g_{ij}):Hp(y_{ij})\to Hp(x_{i})\}_{j\in J_{i}}
in
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
.
We know there exists
\{f_{i}g_{ij}:y_{ij}\to x\}_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}
in
\Cov(C)
since
C
is a site.
Then
\{Hp(f_{i}g_{ij}):Hp(y_{ij})\to Hp(x)\}_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}
is in
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
by definition.
Therefore, Definition
4.2
(
2
) is satisfied.

Finally we check Definition
4.2
(
3
).
Let
\{Hp(f_{i}):Hp(x_{i})\to Hp(x)\}_{i\in I}
be in
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
and let
g:y\to Hp(x)
be a morphism in
\mathcal{S}
.
Then there is a
\bar{g}:y\to x
in
C[\Sigma^{-1}]
such that
H(\bar{g})=g
, where
\bar{g}=\sigma^{-1}\tilde{g}
for some morphism
\tilde{g}
in
C
.
Let
\tilde{y}
be the source of
\tilde{g}
, which is also an object of
C
.
Then, since
\sigma\in\Sigma
, we know that
H(\sigma^{-1})=1_{Hp(x)}
.
So,
Hp(\tilde{y})=y
and
Hp(\tilde{g})=g
.

Since
C
is a site, and by Lemma
4.1
, the pullback diagram in
C
on the left becomes a pullback diagram in
\mathcal{S}
on the right, for each
i\in I
:

\textstyle{x_{i}\times_{x}\tilde{y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}_{i}}
\textstyle{\tilde{y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{g}}
\textstyle{Hp(x_{i}\times_{x}\tilde{y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Hp(f^{\prime}_{i})}
\scriptstyle{Hp(g^{\prime}_{i})}
\textstyle{Hp(\tilde{y})=y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g=Hp(\tilde{g})}
\textstyle{x_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{Hp(x_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Hp(f_{i})}
\textstyle{Hp(x),}

where
Hp(x_{i}\times_{x}\tilde{y})
is canonically isomorphic to
Hp(x_{i})\times_{Hp(x)}y
.
Moreover, we know that
\{f^{\prime}_{i}:x_{i}\times_{x}y\to y\}_{i\in I}\in\Cov(C)
since
C
is a site.
Thus, in
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
we have
\{Hp(f^{\prime}_{i}):Hp(x_{i})\times_{Hp(x)}y\to y\}_{i\in I}
.
Therefore, Definition
4.2
(
3
) is satisfied and so
\mathcal{S}
is a site with coverage
\Cov(\mathcal{S})
.
∎

Definition 4.6

(continuous functor)
.

Let
C
and
D
be sites and let
F:C\to D
be a functor.
We say
F
is
continuous
if for every
\{f_{i}:x_{i}\to x\}
in
\Cov(C)
the following hold.

(1)

The set
\{F(f_{i}):F(x_{i})\to F(x)\}
is in
\Cov(D)
.

(2)

For any morphism
g:y\to x
, the canonical morphism
F(y\times_{x}x_{i})\to F(y)\times_{F(x)}F(x_{i})
is an isomorphism.

Proposition 4.7
.

Given the setup in Proposition
4.5
, the quotient functor
Hp:C\to\mathcal{S}
is continuous.

Proof.

Definition
4.6
(1) follows from Definition
4.4
.
Definition
4.6
(2) follows from Definition
4.1
.
∎

We now put Propositions
4.5
and
4.7
into our context.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
C
be a path category from
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
and also a site with coverage
\Cov(C)
.
Let
\mathfrak{P}
be a screen of
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
and give
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
the coverage
\Cov(\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})})
as in Proposition
4.5
.
Then any sheaf on
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
lifts to a sheaf on
C
.

Proof.

In the setup from Propositions
4.5
and
4.7
, any sheaf on
\mathcal{S}
lifts to a sheaf on
C
and any sheaf on
C
can be pushed down to a sheaf on
\mathcal{S}
. We know that if a functor
F:C\to D
of sites is continuous then any sheaf on
D
lifts to a sheaf on
C
(see, for example,
[
18
, 00WU]
).

To see the result: the class of morphisms
\Sigma
is
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
for a screen
\mathfrak{P}
.
The skeleton
\mathcal{S}
of
C[\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}^{-1}]={\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
is
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
.
The functors
p
and
H
have the same name and are from Notation
2.21
and the proof of Theorem
2.37
(1) on page
2.2
, respectively. ∎

4.2.
Distributive lattices

We now consider
C
to be a distributive lattice and a category where
\Hom_{C}(U,V)
has a unique element if
U\leq V
and is empty otherwise.
Since
C
has finite products as a category (the joins as a lattice), we can reuse the proof of Proposition
4.3
to see that
C
is indeed a site

For example, classically, we could consider
\mathrm{Top}(X)
, for a topological space
X
.
Finite joins and finite products correspond to finite intersections; meets and coproducts correspond to unions.

We define
\Gamma
(Definition
1.1
) as follows.
Denote by
s(f)
the source of a morphism
f
and by
t(f)
the target of a morphism
f
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
we consider a finite partition
\{I_{0},\ldots,I_{n}\}
of
[0,1]
, where each
I_{i}
is a subinterval, such that if
s\in I_{i}
,
t\in I_{j}
, and
i<j
, then
s<t
.
For any such partition and a finite composition
f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}
of morphisms, define
\gamma:[0,1]\to\Ob(C)
by

\gamma(t)=\begin{cases}s(f_{1})&t\in I_{0}\\
t(f_{i})&t\in I_{i},1\leq i\leq n.\end{cases}

Let
\Gamma
be the set of all possible
\gamma
constructed in this way.
It is straightforward to check that our
\Gamma
satisfies Definition
1.1
(
1
,
2
,
3
).

Now, we define
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
if and only if
\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
and
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
.
Then,
\sim
satisfies Definition
1.2
(
1
).
By construction,
\sim
also satisfies Definition
1.2
(
2
,
3
).

From now on, we assume
C
is a sublattice of
L
, for some distributive lattice
L
.
In the
\mathrm{Top}(X)
example,
C
is the open subsets of
X
and
L=\boldsymbol{2}^{X}
, ordered by inclusion.

Definition 4.9

(
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
)
.

Let
Y\in L
.
Define

\mathfrak{P}_{Y}:=\left(\{A_{Z}:=\{U\in C\mid U\wedge Y=Z\}\}_{Z\leq Y\in L}\right)\setminus\{\emptyset\}.

It follows immediately that
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
partitions
C
.

Proposition 4.10
.

The partition
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
is a screen of
(C,\Gamma{/}{\sim})
.

Proof.

We begin with Definition
1.12
(
1
).
Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma(i)=\gamma^{\prime}(i)
for
i\in\{0,1\}
.
Suppose
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset A_{Z}
for some
Z\leq Y
in
L
.
Then,
\gamma(1)\wedge Y=\gamma(0)\wedge Y=Z
.
So, for all
t\in[0,1]
we must have
\gamma(t)\wedge Y=Z
.
But the same must also be true for
\gamma^{\prime}
.
Therefore, each nonempty
A_{Z}
is
\sim
-thin.

Now we show Definition
1.12
(
2
).
Since
C
and
L
are distributive, for any
U,V\in A_{Z}
, we have both
U\vee V
and
U\wedge V
in
A_{Z}
.
Thus the following commutative square exists in
C
:

\textstyle{U\wedge V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U\vee V.}

Thus, each
A_{Z}
is
\Gamma
-connected.

Next, we show Definition
1.12
(
3
).
Let
\gamma
and
\rho
be paths in
\Gamma
such that
\gamma(0)=\rho(0)
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset A_{Z}
for some
Z\leq Y
.
Let
U=\gamma(0)=\rho(0)
,
U^{\prime}=\rho(1)
,
V=\gamma(1)
and
W=V\wedge Y
.
Notice that since
U\leq V
we have
Z=U\wedge Y\leq V\wedge Y=W
and
Z\vee W=W
.
Set
V^{\prime}=U^{\prime}\vee V
.
Since
C
and
L
are distributive, we have
(U^{\prime}\vee V)\wedge Y=(U^{\prime}\wedge Y)\vee(V\wedge Y)=Z\vee W=W
.
Thus we have the desired square.

Let
\gamma
and
\rho
be paths in
\Gamma
such that
\gamma(1)=\rho(1)
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset A_{Z}
for some
Z\leq Y
.
Let
U=\gamma(1)=\rho(1)
,
U^{\prime}=\rho(0
,
V=\gamma(0)
, and
W=V\wedge Y
.
Now
V\leq U
and so we have
W\leq Z
and
W\wedge Z=W
.
Set
V^{\prime}=U^{\prime}\wedge V
.
Then
(U^{\prime}\wedge V)\wedge Y=(U^{\prime}\wedge Y)\wedge(V\wedge Y)=Z\wedge W=W
.
Thus, we have the desired square.

Since, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
|\mathrm{im}(\gamma)|<\infty
, we see that Definition
1.12
(
4
) is automatically satisfied.
Finally, we know that if
\gamma(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
and
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
, for
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, then
\gamma\sim\gamma^{\prime}
.
Thus, Definition
1.12
(
5
) is also satisfied.
This completes the proof.
∎

Example 4.11
.

Here we show an explicit example of Remark
2.23
.
Consider
\mathbb{R}
as a (somewhat trivial) lattice.
Then the construction of the path category
C
from this perspective produces a different collection of screens.
Here, we are taking the opposite order on
\mathbb{R}

as a lattice
so that morphisms in the path category still move “up” with respect to the standard order of
\mathbb{R}
.

We now show an explicit example of a screen of
\mathbb{R}
in the lattice perspective that is not a screen of
\mathbb{R}
as in Example
1.5
.
Set
L=C=\mathbb{R}
, as lattices, and consider the element
Y=0\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then, for any
Z\in\mathbb{R}
, we have the set
A_{Z}
given by

A_{Z}=\begin{cases}\emptyset&Z<0=Y\\
(-\infty,0]&Z=0=Y\\
\{Z\}&Z>0=Y,\end{cases}

where the order in our case statements is the standard order in
\mathbb{R}
.
The pixels in
\mathfrak{P}_{0}=\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
are in bijection with the set
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, where
0
comes from the pixel
(\infty,0]
.
Any path
0\to 1
in the lattice interpretation of
\mathbb{R}
only passes through finitely-many pixels.
However, in the structure of
\mathbb{R}
from Example
1.5
, any path from
0
to
1
would pass through infinitely-many pixels and so
\mathfrak{P}_{0}
is not a screen in that perspective.

If
L
contains an element
Y
such that
Y\leq U
for all
U\in\Ob(C)
, then
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
has exactly one pixel.
If
L
contains an element
Y
such that
U\leq Y
for all
U\in\Ob(C)
, then the pixels of
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
are singletons containing precisely the elements of
\Ob(C)
.

In the
\mathrm{Top}(X)
example,
\mathfrak{P}_{\emptyset}
has exactly one pixel and
\mathfrak{P}_{X}
has one pixel per open subset of
X
.

In general, if
Y\leq Y^{\prime}
in
L
then
\mathfrak{P}_{Y^{\prime}}
refines
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
.
Given
Y,Y^{\prime}\in L
, we see that
\mathfrak{P}_{Y\vee Y^{\prime}}
is the product of
\mathfrak{P}_{Y}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{Y^{\prime}}
in
\{\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}\mid\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{Y},Y\in L\}\subset\mathscr{P}
.
Thus, by Proposition
4.3
, we see
\{\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}\mid\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{Y},Y\in L\}
is a site.

Recall that if
C
is a sublattice of
L
then the meets and joins of
C
coincide with those in
L
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
C
and
L
be distributive lattices such that
C
is a sublattice of
L
.
For
Y\in L
, denote by
C_{Y}\subset L
the distributive lattice
\{U\wedge Y\mid U\in C\}
.
Then
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
is canonically isomorphic to
C_{Y}
as categories.

Proof.

Notice that the condition
A_{Z}=\emptyset
is equivalent to
Z\notin C_{Y}
.
I.e., there does not exist
U\in C
such that
Z=U\wedge Y
.
Thus, we have a canonical bijection of sets
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}C_{Y}
given by
A_{U}\mapsto U
.

Let
U\leq V
in
C_{Y}
.
Then there are
U^{\prime},V^{\prime}\in C
such that,
U=U^{\prime}\wedge Y
and
V=V^{\prime}\wedge Y
.
Moreover,
U^{\prime}\leq V^{\prime}
.
Then there is a morphism
f:U^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
in
C
and so a morphism
1^{-1}f:U^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
and thus a morphism
A_{U}\to A_{V}
in
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
.

Suppose there is a morphism
A_{U}\to A_{V}
in
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
.
Then there is a morphism
\sigma^{-1}f:U^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
where
U^{\prime}\in A_{U}
and
V^{\prime}\in A_{V}
.
Let
V^{\prime\prime}
be the target of
f
.
Then there is a morphism
f:U^{\prime}\to V^{\prime\prime}
in
C
and so
U^{\prime}\leq V^{\prime\prime}
in
C
.
We know
U^{\prime}\wedge Y=U
.
Since
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
, we have
V^{\prime\prime}\in A_{V}
and so
V^{\prime\prime}\wedge Y=V
.
Thus
U\leq V
and so there is a morphism
U\to V
in
C_{Y}
.

We have shown a morphism exists
U\to V
in
C_{Y}
if and only if there is a morphism
A_{U}\to A_{V}
in
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
.
We will now show that there can be at most one morphism
A_{U}\to A_{V}
.
Let
\sigma_{1}^{-1}f_{1},\sigma_{2}^{-1}f_{2}:U\rightrightarrows V
be morphisms in
{\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle C$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}}
.
Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be the targets of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, respectively, in
C
and let
V^{\prime}=V\wedge Y
.
Then
V_{1}\wedge Y=V_{2}\wedge Y=V^{\prime}
and so
(V_{1}\vee V_{2})\wedge Y=V^{\prime}
.
Thus,
V_{1}\vee V_{2}\in A_{V^{\prime}}
and
V\leq V_{1}\vee V_{2}
.
We also have
U\leq V_{1}\vee V_{2}
.
Let
\sigma^{\prime}:V\to V_{1}\vee V_{2}
and
f^{\prime}:U\to V_{1}\vee V_{2}
be the unique maps that exist in their respective
\Hom
sets.
This yields a morphism
(\sigma^{\prime})^{-1}f^{\prime}:U\to V
which is equivalent to
\sigma_{1}^{-1}f_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}^{-1}f_{2}
.
Therefore, there is at most one morphism in
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
between any pair
A_{U}
and
A_{V}
.

We now have a bijection between the sets
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
and
C_{Y}
and we have shown that
\Hom_{\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}}(A_{U},A_{V})\cong\Hom_{C_{Y}}(U,V)
for all
U,V\in C_{Y}
.
Therefore, the two categories are canonically isomorphic.
Since the only choice was the canonical map of sets
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}\to C_{Y}
, we see that the isomorphism is itself canonical.
∎

To make the following corollary easier to write down, we write
\TS(R)
to mean
\mathrm{Top}(\mathrm{Spec}(R))
, for a commutative ring
R
.

Corollary 4.13

(to Theorem
4.12
)
.

In increasing specificity:

(1)

Let
X
be a topological space and
Y
a subset of
X
.
Then
\mathrm{Top}(Y)
, where
Y
has the subspace topology, is canonically isomorphic to
\overline{Q(\mathrm{Top}(X),\mathfrak{P}_{Y})}
.

(2)

Let
R
be a commutative ring, let
S\subset R
be a multiplicative set, and let
Y(S)=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathrm{Spec}(R)\mid S\cap\mathfrak{p}=\emptyset\}
.
Then
\TS(S^{-1}R)
is canonically isomorphic to
\overline{Q(\TS(R),\mathfrak{P}_{Y(S)})}
.

(3)

Let
R
be a commutative ring, let
\mathfrak{p}
be a prime ideal in
R
, and let
S=R\setminus\mathfrak{p}
.
Let
Y(\mathfrak{p})=\{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathrm{Spec}(R)\mid\mathfrak{q}\subset\mathfrak{p}\}
.
Then
\TS(R_{\mathfrak{p}})
is canonically isomorphic to
\overline{Q(\TS(R),\mathfrak{P}_{Y(\mathfrak{p})})}
.

Proof.

Item 1 follows directly from Theorem
4.12
.
Item 2 follows from item 1 and the fact that the induced map
\mathrm{Spec}(S^{-1}R)\to\{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathrm{Spec}(R)\mid\mathfrak{p}\cap S=\emptyset\}
is a homeomorphism.
Item 3 follows directly from item 2.
∎

4.3.
Pathed sites and sheaf of representations

The goal of this section is to provide the “standard” example of a parallel story to sheaves of rings and their modules.

Recall
\boldsymbol{PCat}
, the category of path categories, and recall that it is a full subcategory of
\boldsymbol{Cat}
, the category of small categories.
Recall also that all our sites are small categories.

Definition 4.14

(pathed site)
.

Let
E
be a site and let
\mathcal{O}_{E}:E^{op}\to\boldsymbol{PCat}
be a sheaf of path categories.
Then we say
(D,\mathcal{O}_{E})
is a
pathed site
.

The standard example of a ringed space
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is where
X
is a scheme and
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is its structure sheaf.
We suggest that the standard example of a pathed site come from a path category
C
and its pixelations, if
\mathscr{P}
is closed under finite
\sqcap
operations (Definition
1.21
).

Suppose
\mathscr{P}
always has the operation
\sqcap
over finitely-many screens.
Notice that
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{1}\mathfrak{P}_{i}=\mathfrak{P}_{1}
for all
\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{1}\in\mathscr{P}
.
Recall that the set of partitions of
\mathbb{X}
form a distributive lattice.
So, for any
\mathfrak{Q}\in\mathscr{P}
, if the operation
\sqcup
exists over some finite collection
\{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathscr{P}
, then
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}(\mathfrak{Q}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{i})=\mathfrak{Q}\sqcap(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i})\in\mathscr{P}
.

Definition 4.15

(
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
)
.

Let
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
be the subcategory of
\mathscr{P}
with the same objects and whose morphisms
\mathfrak{P}\to\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
only exist for finitary refinements (Definition
2.44
).
Let
\Cov(\overline{\mathscr{P}})
have the empty coverings of each
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
and
\{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\to\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}\}_{i=1}^{n}
if each
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
is a finitary refinement of
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

The category
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
with coverage
\Cov(\overline{\mathscr{P}})
is a site.

Proof.

We check each of the items in Definition
4.2
.

Definition
4.2
(
1
)
. The only isomorphisms in
\mathscr{P}
are the identity maps, and
\mathscr{P}
is trivially a finitary refinement of itself.

Definition
4.2
(
2
)
. If
\mathfrak{P}_{ij}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{P}_{i}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\mathfrak{P}_{ij}
, for each
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq n_{i}
, then
\mathfrak{P}_{ij}
is also a finitary refinement of
\sqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}
.

Definition
4.2
(
3
)
. If
\mathfrak{Q}
is a finitary refinement of
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}
, then each
\mathfrak{Q}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{i}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
.
Since the partitions of
\mathbb{X}
form a lattice, we know
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}(\mathfrak{Q}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{i})=\mathfrak{Q}\sqcap(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}P_{i})=\mathfrak{Q}
.
∎

Notice that if
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{j}
are both finitary refinements of
\mathfrak{P}
then
\mathfrak{P}_{i}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{j}
is a finitary refinement
\mathfrak{P}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
, and
\mathfrak{P}_{j}
.

Recall the
\Init
functor (Proposition
2.45
) that uses Lemma
1.19
to pick out an initial subpixel of a refinement screen.
For a screen
\mathfrak{P}
, denote by
C_{\mathfrak{P}}
the path category constructed in Proposition
2.43
that is isomorphic to
\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
.

Definition 4.17

(
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
)
.

We define
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}:\overline{\mathscr{P}}^{op}\to\boldsymbol{PCat}
.
For each
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
, let
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}(\mathfrak{P})=C_{\mathfrak{P}}
.
For each finitary refinement
\mathfrak{P}\leq\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
, let
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}(\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{P})
be the functor
\Init:C_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}\to C_{\mathfrak{P}}
that comes from
\Init:\overline{Q(C,{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}})}\to\overline{Q(C,\mathfrak{P})}
.

It is straightforward to show that the composition of two
\Init
functors is itself an
\Init
functor and so
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
is a functor (presheaf).

Let
\mathfrak{P}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}
such that each
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{P}
.
Let
\Init_{i}:C_{\mathfrak{P}}\to C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}}
be the
\Init
functor for each
1\leq i\leq n
.
For each
1\leq j,k,\leq n
let
\Init_{jk0}:C_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}}\to C_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k}}
and
\Init_{jk1}:C_{\mathfrak{P}_{k}}\to C_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k}}
be the respective
\Init
functors.

Define
pr_{0}
be the functor
\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}}\to\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{n}C_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k}}
where, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, the functor
C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}}\to\prod_{k=1}^{n}C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k}}
is
\prod_{k=1}^{n}\Init_{ik0}
.
Define
pr_{1}
to be the functor where, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, the functor
C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}}\to\prod_{j=1}^{n}C_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{i}}
is
\prod_{j=1}^{n}\Init_{ji1}
.

Recall the join complex of
\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}
(Definition
1.27
).

Theorem 4.18
.

The functor
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
, defined above, makes
(\overline{\mathscr{P}},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}})
a pathed site.
That is, if
\{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\}\subset\mathscr{P}
such that
\mathfrak{P}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}_{i}\in\mathscr{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{P}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
, then the following diagram is an equalizer diagram in
\boldsymbol{PCat}
:

\textstyle{C_{\mathfrak{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Init_{i}}
\textstyle{\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{pr_{1}}
\textstyle{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{n}C_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k}}.}

Proof.

If
pr_{j}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{X}_{n})=pr_{k}(\mathsf{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{X}_{n})
then, for each
1\leq j,k\leq n
, we have
\Init_{jk0}(\mathsf{X}_{j})=\Init_{jk1}(\mathsf{X}_{k})
.
That is,
\mathsf{X}_{j}\cap\mathsf{Y}_{k}=\mathsf{Y}^{\prime}_{j}\cap\mathsf{X}_{k}=\mathsf{X}_{j}\cap\mathsf{X}_{k}
.
Considering
\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k}
as a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{P}_{j}
and of
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
, this means
\mathsf{X}_{j}\cap\mathsf{X}_{k}
is initial in both
\mathsf{X}_{j}
and
\mathsf{X}_{k}
.

Since there are only finitely-many
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
, we see that
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\neq\emptyset
and so there is some
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
\mathsf{X}\supset\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Let
z\in\mathsf{X}
and suppose
z\notin\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}
.
Then there is
1\leq j\leq n
such that
z\notin\mathsf{X}_{j}
.
However,
z\in Y_{k}\in\mathfrak{P}_{k}
for some
1\leq k\leq n
.
Then there is some finite sequence
(\mathsf{X}_{j}=\mathsf{Y}_{i_{0},0},\mathsf{Y}_{i_{1},1},\ldots,\mathsf{Y}_{i_{m},m}=\mathsf{Y}_{k})
, where
1\leq i_{\ell}\leq n
for each
0\leq\ell\leq m
,
Y_{i_{\ell},\ell}\in\mathfrak{P}_{i_{\ell}}
for each
0\leq\ell\leq m
, and
\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell-1},\ell-1}\cap\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell},\ell}\neq\emptyset
for
1\leq\ell\leq m
.
Moreover, we may assume the sequence is minimal in the following sense: if
\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell},\ell}\cap\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell^{\prime}},\ell^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
then
\ell=\ell^{\prime}\pm 1
.

Let
x_{0}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Since
\mathsf{Y}_{i_{1},1}\neq\mathsf{X}_{i_{1}}
and
\mathsf{X}_{i_{1}}\cap\mathsf{X}_{j}
is initial in
\mathsf{X}_{j}
, there is some path
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\in\Gamma
with
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(0)\in\mathsf{X}_{i_{1}}\cap\mathsf{X}_{j}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(1)\in\mathsf{Y}_{i_{1},1}\cap\mathsf{X}_{j}
.
By Lemma
1.14
, we may find some
x_{1}
and two paths in
\Gamma
that start at
x_{1}
whose image is contained in
\mathsf{X}_{j}
, one of which ends at
x_{0}
and the other ends at
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(0)
.
Now we have a path
\gamma_{1}
from
x_{1}
to
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(0)=\gamma_{1}(0)\in\mathsf{Y}_{i_{1},1}\cap\mathsf{X}_{j}
.
Since
x_{0}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
, which is initial in
\mathsf{X}_{j}
, we know
x_{1}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
as well.

Suppose we have a path
\gamma_{\ell}\in\Gamma
with
\gamma_{\ell}(0)=x_{\ell}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
and
\gamma_{\ell}(1)\in\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell-1},\ell-1}\cap\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell},\ell}
.
Choose
x\in\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell},\ell}\cap\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell+1},\ell+1}
.
By Lemma
1.14
, we have
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma
with
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell},\ell}
,
\rho(1)=\rho^{\prime}(1)=:x^{\prime}
,
\rho(0)=x
, and
\rho^{\prime}(0)=\gamma_{\ell}(1)
.
Then we have paths
\gamma_{\ell}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
and
\rho
with
\rho(1)=\gamma_{\ell}\cdot\rho^{\prime}(1)
and
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\subset\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell},\ell}
.
By Definition
1.12
(
3
) there are paths
\tilde{\rho},\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma
such that
\tilde{\rho}(0)=\tilde{\gamma}(0)
,
\tilde{\rho}(1)=\gamma_{\ell}(0)
,
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\rho(0)
, and
\tilde{\rho}\cdot\gamma_{\ell}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim\tilde{\gamma}\cdot\rho
.

Again, since
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
is initial in
\mathsf{X}_{i_{\ell}}
, we have
x_{\ell+1}=\tilde{\rho}(0)\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Since
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell+1},\ell+1}
, we have a path
\gamma_{\ell+1}=\tilde{\gamma}\cdot\rho
from
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
to
\mathsf{Y}_{i_{\ell+1},\ell+1}
.
By induction, we have a path
\gamma_{n}
from some
x_{n}\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{0}
to
\mathsf{Y}_{n}=\mathsf{Y}_{i_{m},m}
.

Now we again use Lemma
1.14
to create paths
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\Gamma
with
\mathrm{im}(\rho)\cup\mathrm{im}(\rho^{\prime})\subset\mathsf{Y}_{n}
,
\rho(0)=z
,
\rho^{\prime}(0)=\gamma_{n}(1)
, and
\rho(1)=\rho^{\prime}(1)
.
By Definition
1.12
(
3
), we have paths
\tilde{\rho},\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma
with
\tilde{\rho}(0)=\tilde{\gamma}(0)
,
\tilde{\rho}(1)=\gamma_{n}(1)
,
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\rho(0)=z
, and
\tilde{\rho}\cdot\gamma_{n}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\sim\tilde{\gamma}\cdot\rho
.

For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathfrak{P}_{i}
be the pixel containing
z
.
We know that
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{j}\neq\mathsf{X}_{j}
.
Now we have a path
\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma
with
\tilde{\gamma}(0)\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
and
\tilde{\gamma}(1)=z\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{i}
.
Thus, by Lemma
1.18
, there are no paths
\gamma\in\Gamma
with
\gamma(0)\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\gamma(1)\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
.
Thus,
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i}
is initial in
\mathsf{X}
, with respect to
\mathfrak{P}_{1}\sqcap\cdots\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{n}
, and so each
\mathsf{X}_{i}
is initial in
\mathsf{X}
, with resepct to
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
.
Therefore, if
pr_{0}(\mathsf{X}_{i})_{i}=pr_{1}(\mathsf{X}_{i})_{i}
then there is
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
such that
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Init_{i}(\mathsf{X})=(\mathsf{X}_{i})_{i}
.
Since
\Init
is injective on objects by construction, we see that the
\mathsf{X}
is unique.

Suppose
(f_{i})_{i}:(\mathsf{X}_{i})_{i}\to(\mathsf{Y}_{i})_{i}
is a morphism in
\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}}
such that
pr_{0}((f_{i})_{i})=pr_{1}((f_{i})_{i})
.
So,
\Init_{jk0}(f_{j})=\Init_{jk1}(f_{k})
in
C_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k}}
, for
1\leq j,k\leq n
.
Denote by
\Init_{i\mathfrak{Q}}
the
\Init
functor
C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}}\to C_{\mathfrak{Q}}
, where
\mathfrak{Q}=\mathfrak{P}_{1}\sqcap\cdots\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{n}
.
Then we have
\bar{f}=\Init_{j\mathfrak{Q}}(f_{j})=\Init_{k\mathfrak{Q}}(f_{k})
for
1\leq j,k\leq n
.
So, there exists a path
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
H_{\mathfrak{Q}}p_{\mathfrak{Q}}([\gamma])=\bar{f}
.
We now have
H_{i}p_{i}([\gamma])=f_{i}
and
Hp([\gamma])=\Init_{i}(f_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
Since
\Init
is injective on
\Hom
-sets (Remark
2.46
), this
f
must be unique.

Therefore, the image of
C_{\mathfrak{P}}
in
\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\mathfrak{P}_{i}}
is precisely the subcategory on which
pr_{0}
and
pr_{1}
agree.
∎

Example 4.19

(running example)
.

Let
C
be
\mathbb{R}
from Example
1.5
.

Consider
\mathfrak{P}=\{[i,i+1)\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
from Example
1.13
and let

\mathfrak{Q}=\{[i,i+1/2],(i+1/2,i+2)\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ even}\}.

The reader is encouraged to verify that
\mathfrak{Q}
is also a screen (Definition
1.12
).
Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{Q}

\displaystyle=\{[i,i+2)\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ even}\}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}\sqcap\mathfrak{Q}

\displaystyle=\{[i,i+1/2],(i+1/2,i+1),[i+1,i+2)\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ even}\}.

The pixel
[i,i+1/2]\in\mathfrak{P}\sqcap\mathfrak{Q}
, for an even integer
i
, is initial in both
[i,i+1)
(for
\mathfrak{P}
) and itself (for
\mathfrak{Q}
).
It is also initial in
[i,i+2)
(for
\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{Q}
).
The pixel
[i,i+1)
is also initial in
[i,i+2)
when
i
is even.
There are no other pixels that work this way.
The reader is encouraged to verify this.

Thus,
\Init_{\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}0}(\mathsf{X}_{\mathfrak{P}})=\Init_{\mathfrak{P}\mathfrak{Q}1}(\mathsf{X}_{\mathfrak{Q}})
precisely when
\mathsf{X}_{\mathfrak{P}}=[i,i+1)
and
\mathsf{X}_{\mathfrak{Q}}=[i,i+2]
, for an even integer
i
.
Moreover,
\Init_{\mathfrak{P}}([i,i+2))=[i,i+1)
and
\Init_{\mathfrak{Q}}([i,i+2))=[i,i+1/2]
.
This yields the equalizer diagram below (with the names of maps suppressed):

\textstyle{C_{\mathfrak{P}\sqcup\mathfrak{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{\mathfrak{P}}\times C_{\mathfrak{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(C_{\mathfrak{P}}\times C_{\mathfrak{P}\sqcap\mathfrak{Q}})\times(C_{\mathfrak{P}\sqcap\mathfrak{Q}}\times C_{\mathfrak{Q}}).}

We compare our story to that for ringed sites.
In Definition
4.14
, we have a sheaf from a site
E
into
\boldsymbol{PCat}
, which is parallel to a sheaf into the category of rings.
Given a ringed site
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
, an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module is a sheaf
F:X^{op}\to\boldsymbol{Ab}
such that each
F(x)
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}(x)
-module.
Moreover, for each
f:x\leftarrow y
in
X
we have the following commutative diagram in
\boldsymbol{Ab}
:

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X}(x)\times F(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X}(f)\times F(f)}
\textstyle{F(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X}(y)\times F(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(y),}

where the horizontal arrows are the rings’ actions.
Definition
4.20
(
1
) is parallel to requiring that
F(x)
is an
\mathcal{O}_{X}(x)
-module.
Definition
4.20
(
2
) is parallel to requiring that rings’ actions are compatible with the sheaf.

We first give a simple
2
2

2

As simple as sheaves get, anyway.
example of an
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-representation.

Example 4.21

(“classical” example)
.

Let
E
be
\mathrm{Top}(\{1,2\})
, where
\{1,2\}
has the discrete topology.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{E}(\emptyset)=*
, where
*
is the path category with one object and only the identity morphism.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{E}(\{1\})=\mathcal{O}_{E}(\{2\})=C
, for a path category
C
different from
*
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{E}(\{1,2\})=C\times C
.
Since
*
is terminal in both
\boldsymbol{PCat}
and
cats
, we have no choice for functors
C\to*
and
C\times C\to*
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{E}(\{1,2\}\to\{1\})
be the projection
C\times C\to C
on the first coordinate and similarly for
\mathcal{O}_{E}(\{1,2\}\to\{2\})
on the second coordinate.
Then
\mathcal{O}_{E}
is a sheaf of path categories.

Let
{}_{\emptyset}M
be the
0
-representation.
Choose a representation
{}_{\{1\}}M={}_{\{2\}}M
of
C
.
Let
{}_{\{1,2\}}M(x_{1},x_{2})={}_{\{1\}}M(x_{1})\oplus{}_{\{2\}}M(x_{2})
and similarly for morphisms.

Let
M:E\to\mathcal{K}
be a functor where
M(X)={}_{X}M
, for each
X\subset\{1,2\}
.
For
X=*
, we have
M(Y\leftarrow X)=0
for all
Y\subset\{1,2\}
.
The only possible nonzero morphisms are
M(\{1,2\}\leftarrow\{1\})
and
M(\{1,2\}\leftarrow\{2\})
.
Define

\displaystyle M(\{1,2\}\leftarrow\{1\})

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}:M(\{1,2\})\to M(\{1\})

\displaystyle M(\{1,2\}\leftarrow\{2\})

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}:M(\{1,2\})\to M(\{2\}).

Thus,
M
is a functor and indeed a sheaf.
It is left to the reader as an exercise to verify that
M
is indeed an
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-representation.
