3

3

Hint: this is essentially a constant sheaf of modules.

We use the following definition for Example
4.23
.

Definition 4.22
.

Let
C
be a path category,
x
an object in
C
, and
K
an object in
\mathcal{K}
.
The
representation at
K
concentrated at
x
is the functor
M:C\to\mathcal{K}
where
M(x)=K
and
M(y)=0
for all objects
y\neq x
in
C
.
On morphisms,
M([\gamma])=0
, for any morphism
[\gamma]\neq\boldsymbol{1}_{x}
in
C
.
As required,
M(\boldsymbol{1}_{x})=\boldsymbol{1}_{K}
.

In the literature, when
\mathcal{K}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Vec}
and
K=\Bbbk
, for some field
\Bbbk
, the representation at
\Bbbk
concentrated at
x
is referred to as the
simple representation at
x
.

Example 4.23

(running example)
.

Notice that, for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as in Example
1.11
, the set
\mathscr{P}
has unique maximal element
\{\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
.
Let
\mathfrak{Q}=\{\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
and let
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
The following construction works for any triple
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
in
\mathbf{X}
for which
\mathscr{P}
has a unique maximal element and
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
exists.

Let
F:\overline{\mathscr{P}}^{op}\to\mathcal{K}
be a sheaf.
For each
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
such that
\mathfrak{P}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
, let
M(\mathfrak{P})=F(\mathfrak{P})
and let
{}_{\mathfrak{P}}M
be the representation at
F(\mathfrak{P})
concentrated at
\Init(\mathsf{Z})\in\mathfrak{P}
.
If
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}
is not a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
, the let
M(\mathfrak{P})=0
and
{}_{\mathfrak{P}}M
be the
0
representation.
If
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
, define
M(\mathfrak{P}\leftarrow\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})=F(\mathfrak{P}\leftarrow\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})
.
Set all other
M(\mathfrak{P}\leftarrow\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})=0
.
It is clear that
M
is a functor.

We now show
M
is a sheaf.
Suppose
\{\mathfrak{P}_{i}\to\mathfrak{P}\}
is in
\Cov(\overline{\mathscr{P}})
(Definition
4.15
).
Then every
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
, is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
if and only if
\mathfrak{P}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
.
Moreover, this means each
\mathfrak{P}_{i}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{j}
is also a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
if and only if
\mathfrak{P}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
.
If
\mathfrak{P}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
, we have the following, where the top row is an equalizer diagram:

\textstyle{F(\mathfrak{P})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}F(\mathfrak{P}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{pr_{1}}
\textstyle{{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}F(\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\mathfrak{P})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}M(\mathfrak{P}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{pr_{0}}
\scriptstyle{pr_{1}}
\textstyle{{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}M(\mathfrak{P}_{j}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{k})}.}

Therefore, the bottom row is an equalizer diagram as well.
If
\mathfrak{P}
is not a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
, then the bottom row of the diagram above is all
0
’s and is thus also an equalizer diagram.
Thus,
M
is a sheaf.

Finally, we show that
M
is an
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
-representation.
By construction,
M
and
\{{}_{\mathfrak{P}}M\}_{\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{P}}
satisfy Definition
4.20
(
1
).
Let
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
be a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{P}
.
If
\mathfrak{P}
is not a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
, then Definition
4.20
(
2
) is satisfied since it will be a square of
0
’s.
Now suppose
\mathfrak{P}
is a finitary refinement of
\mathfrak{Q}
.
All
{}_{\mathfrak{P}}M([\gamma])=0
unless
[\gamma]=\boldsymbol{1}_{\Init(\mathsf{Z})}
.
Similarly,
{}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}M([\gamma])=0
unless
[\gamma]=\boldsymbol{1}_{\Init(\mathsf{Z})}
.
Moreover, if
\mathsf{X}\in\mathfrak{P}
is not
\Init(\mathsf{Z})
, then let
\overline{\mathsf{X}}=\Init(\mathsf{X})
in
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
and notice
{}_{\mathfrak{P}}M(\mathsf{X})=0={}_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}M(\overline{\mathsf{X}})
.
So, we only need to check that
M(\mathfrak{P}\leftarrow\mathfrak{P}^{\prime})=f_{\Init(\mathsf{Z})}
.
This is also true by construction and so
M
is an
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
-representation.

5.
Higher Auslander Categories

For this section, fix
\Bbbk
to be a field and
\mathcal{K}
to be
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}=\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Vec}
.
Thus, we will suppress the
\mathcal{K}
in
\RepK
and
\rpwfK
to write just
\Rep
and
\rpwf
.
Recall our triples
(\mathbb{X},\Gamma{/}{\sim})
(Definitions
1.1
and
1.2
) and that
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
may be considered as triples (Examples
1.5
and
1.11
).
In the later case, we are using the product of triples (Definition
1.9
) that is indeed the product in the category of triples (Propostion
1.10
).
We will also use the fact that any screen on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a product of screens on
\mathbb{R}
and vice verse (Proposition
1.25
).

In this section we will apply Sections
2
and
3
to construct a continuous version of higher Auslander algebras, which we call higher Auslander categories.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and let
\mathbb{R}^{n}
be as in Example
1.11
.
We construct a path based
\mathcal{I}^{(n)}
in
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
, where
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
is the
\Bbbk
-linear path category from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
seen as product of triples (Definition
1.9
and Proposition
1.10
).
Our construction is based on those in
[
14
,
12
]
.
We emphasize that while essentially the same model appear in both papers, it originated in
[
14
]
and was modified in
[
12
]
.

A nonzero morphism
f:\bar{x}\to\bar{y}
is in
\mathcal{I}^{(n)}
if and only if at least one of the following are satisfied.

(1)

x_{1}\leq 0
or
y_{1}\leq 0
,

(2)

x_{n}\geq 1
or
y_{n}\geq 1
, or

(3)

x_{i}\geq x_{i+1}
or
y_{i}\geq y_{i+1}
for
1\leq i<n
.

Equivalently, we can say that a nonzero
f:\bar{x}\to\bar{y}
is
not
in
\mathcal{I}^{(n)}
if and only if
0<x_{1}\leq y_{1}<x_{2}\leq y_{2}<\cdots<x_{n}\leq y_{n}<1
(see Proposition
5.3
).

Recall the definition of a path based ideal (Definition
2.8
).

The following follows immediately from the construction.

Proposition 5.1
.

The ideal
\mathcal{I}^{(n)}
is a path based ideal.

Notation 5.2
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
the quotient
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}/\mathcal{I}^{(n)}
.

When
n=2
, the set of nonzero objects in
\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the shaded region below, without its boundary:

w_{1}
w_{2}

The morphisms in
\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{\mathbb{R}}
move up and/or to the right: the positive
w_{1}
direction and/or the positive
w_{2}
direction.

When
n=3
, the set of nonzero objects in
\mathcal{A}^{(3)}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the interior of the polygon below (without its faces).
We show two different perspectives as this paper is 2D and only has static images:

w_{1}
w_{2}
w_{3}

w_{1}
w_{2}
w_{3}

Here, morphisms move in at least one of: the positive
w_{1}
direction, positive
w_{2}
direction, and/or positive
w_{3}
direction.

Let
\bar{x}
be an point in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
1\leq i\leq n
be an integer, and let
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
We define
g_{\bar{x}i\delta}
to be the path in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
given by

g_{\bar{x}i\delta}(t)=(x_{1},x_{2}\ldots,x_{i-1},\,x_{i}+t\delta,\,x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n-1},x_{n}).

Thus, any path
[\gamma]
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a finite composition of
g_{\bar{x}i\delta}
’s by traveling along the first coordinate, then the second, and so on.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\bar{x},\bar{y}
be objects in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\neq 0
if and only if
0<x_{1}\leq y_{1}<x_{2}\leq y_{2}<\cdots<x_{n}\leq y_{n}<1
.

Proof.

(
\Leftarrow
).
Assume the inequality in the statement of the proposition.
Then, certainly,
\bar{x}\not\cong 0
and
\bar{y}\not\cong 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Moreover,
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\cong\Bbbk
, by construction.
We need to show that the nonzero morphism
[\gamma]:\bar{x}\to\bar{y}
in
\mathcal{C}
does not factor through some
\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\bar{z}\cong 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Let
\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that the nonzero
[\gamma]:\bar{x}\to\bar{y}
in
\mathcal{C}
factors through
\bar{z}
.
Then we can rewrite
[\gamma]
as
[\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{2}]=[\gamma_{2}]\circ[\gamma_{1}]
.
Since
[\gamma_{1}]\neq 0
, we know
x_{k}\leq z_{k}
for each
1\leq k\leq n
.
Since
[\gamma_{2}]\neq 0
, we know
z_{k}\leq y_{k}
for each
1\leq k\leq n
.
This forces
0<z_{1}<z_{2}<\ldots<z_{n}<1
, and so
\bar{z}\not\cong 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Therefore,
[\gamma]
is not 0 in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(
\Rightarrow
).
Now suppose
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\neq 0
.
Then we must have
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\cong\Bbbk
.
Let
[\gamma]:\bar{x}\to\bar{y}
be nonzero in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We immediately know
0<x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{n}<1
,
0<y_{1}<y_{2}<\ldots<y_{n}<1
, and
x_{k}\leq y_{k}
for each
1\leq k\leq n
.

For contradiction, suppose there is some
1\leq j<n
such that
x_{j+1}\leq y_{j}
.
For each
1\leq k\leq n
, let
\delta_{k}=y_{k}-x_{k}
.
Then
[\gamma]
has a representative of the form

g_{\bar{x}j\delta_{j}}\cdot g_{(\bar{x}+\delta_{j}e_{j})1\delta_{1}}\cdots g_{(\bar{x}+\delta_{1}e_{1}+\cdots+\delta_{n-1}e_{n-1})n\delta_{n}}.

However, the target of
[g_{\bar{x}j\delta_{j}}]
is
(x_{1},\ldots x_{j-1},y_{j},x_{j+1},\ldots,x_{n})
.
Since
x_{j+1}\leq y_{j}
, the target of
[g_{\bar{x}j\delta_{j}}]
is
0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
. This is a contradiction since
[\gamma]
is nonzero in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Therefore, the inequality in the statement of the proposition holds.
∎

We say a sequence of objects
\{\bar{x}_{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
is
projective
if the following are satisfied.

(1)

We have
\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\bar{x}_{(i)}=:\bar{x}
is an object in
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
such that
0=x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{n}<1
, where the limit is computed in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the usual metric.

(2)

We have
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i+1)},\bar{x}_{(i)})\cong\Bbbk
for all
i\geq 1
.

Definition 5.4

(
P_{\bar{x}}
)
.

For each object
\bar{x}
in
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
such that
0\leq x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{n}<1
, we define a representation
P_{\bar{x}}
of
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
as follows.
4
4

4

Notice the difference in categories!

First, if
\bar{x}\neq 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
then
P_{\bar{x}}:=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x},-)
.
If
x_{1}=0
we define
P_{\bar{x}}
on objects as

P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y})=\begin{cases}\Bbbk&\exists\text{ projective }\{\bar{x}_{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\text{ such that }{\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{y})\cong\Bbbk\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where the limit of
\Hom
’s is taken by choosing the element in
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i+1)},\bar{x}_{(i)})
corresponding to
1\in\Bbbk
, for each
i\geq 1
.

If
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{y},\bar{y^{\prime}})\cong\Bbbk
,
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y})\neq 0
, and
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y}^{\prime})\neq 0
, we define
P_{\bar{x}}([\gamma]):=\boldsymbol{1}_{\Bbbk}
.
Otherwise, we say
P_{\bar{x}}(f)=0
for any
f:\bar{y}\to\bar{y}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Notice that each
P_{\bar{x}}
is indecomposable, even if
x_{1}=0
.

While it follows almost immediately that
P_{\bar{x}}
is a functor even if
x_{1}=0
, perhaps the reader would benefit from some intuitive reasoning as to why
P_{\bar{x}}
makes sense to include among the
\Hom
functors.

Suppose
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{y},\bar{y^{\prime}})\cong\Bbbk
,
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y})\neq 0
, and
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y}^{\prime})\neq 0
.
Then there are
N,N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
i>\max{N,N^{\prime}}
we have
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{y})\cong\Bbbk
and similarly for
\bar{y}^{\prime}
.
For the unique class
[\gamma]:\bar{y}\to\bar{y}^{\prime}
, and when
i>\max{N,N^{\prime}}
, we have the diagram below, where each node is isomorphic to
\Bbbk
in
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{vec}
and each arrow is an isomorphism:

\textstyle{\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\circ(\bar{x}_{(i+1)}\to\bar{x}_{(i)})}
\scriptstyle{[\gamma]\circ-}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i+1)},\bar{y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\gamma]\circ-}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{y}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\circ(\bar{x}_{(i+1)}\to\bar{x}_{(i)})}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i+1)},\bar{y}^{\prime}).}

Thus, the induced map

\left(\lim_{i\to\infty}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{y})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\gamma]\circ-}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left(\lim_{i\to\infty}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{y}^{\prime})\right)

is an isomorphism.
However, when defining
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y})
, we can choose any sequence so long as the limit is isomorphic to
\Bbbk
.
Thus, it makes sense to simply define
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y})=\Bbbk
and to define
P_{\bar{x}}([\gamma])=\boldsymbol{1}_{\Bbbk}
in the above context.

Proposition 5.5
.

The
P_{\bar{x}}
functors in Definition
5.4
are projective in
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.

Proof.

If
\bar{x}\neq 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
then the statement follows from the fact that
\Hom(\bar{x},-)
is a projective object in
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.
So, we assume
x_{1}=0
.

Consider the diagram
P_{\bar{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\twoheadleftarrow}}M
in
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.
We will construct a lift
h:P_{\bar{x}}\to M
such that
g=fh
.

Since the result follows immediately if
g=0
, we assume
g\neq 0
.
Then
f\neq 0
and so let
K=\ker(f)
.
Choose some
\bar{z}
such that
g_{\bar{z}}:P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{z})\to N(\bar{z})
is nonzero.
Then
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{z})\neq 0
and so there is a projecive sequence
\{\bar{x}_{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
such that
{\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{z})\cong\Bbbk
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that for sufficiently large
i
, we have
x_{k}=x_{(i),k}
for
1<k\leq n
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
i>N
then
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{z})\cong\Bbbk
and
x_{k}=x_{(i),k}
for
1<k\leq n
.
Remove the elements of
\{\bar{x}_{(i)}\}
where
i\leq N
and reindex the remaining sequence by
i\mapsto i-N
.
Thus,
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{z})\cong\Bbbk
and
x_{k}=x_{(i),k}
when
1<k\leq n
, for all indices
i
.

Now we have a system of short exact sequences

\textstyle{{\vdots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\vdots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\vdots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K(\bar{x}_{(i+1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\bar{x}_{(i+1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N(\bar{x}_{(i+1)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K(\bar{x}_{(i)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\bar{x}_{(i)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N(\bar{x}_{(i)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}
\textstyle{{\vdots}}

In
\Bbbk\text{-}\mathrm{Vec}
, let

\displaystyle K(\bar{x})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\leftarrow}K(\bar{x}_{(i)})

\displaystyle M(\bar{x})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\leftarrow}M(\bar{x}_{(i)})

\displaystyle N(\bar{x})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\leftarrow}N(\bar{x}_{(i)}).

Since
K
is pointwise finite-dimensional, the inverse system
\{K(\bar{x}_{(i)})\}
is Mittag-Leffler and so
0\to K(\bar{x})\to M(\bar{x})\to N(\bar{x})\to 0
is exact.
In particular,
M(\bar{x})
surjects onto
N(\bar{x})
; denote this epimorphism by
f_{\bar{x}}
.

We also have the inverse system
\{P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x}_{(i)})\}
and we denote its limit by
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x})
.
Since, for each
i
, we have
g_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}:P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x}_{(i)})\to N(\bar{x}_{(i)})
we have an induced map between the limits:
g_{\bar{x}}:P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x})\to N(\bar{x})
.
Since
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x}_{(i)})=\Bbbk
for each
i
, we know
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{x})=\Bbbk
.

Now, let
n=g_{\bar{x}}(1)
and choose
m\in M(\bar{x})
such that
f_{\bar{x}}(m)=n
.
For each
i
, let
m_{i}\in M(\bar{x}_{(i)})
be the image of
m
under the limit map
M(\bar{x})\to M(\bar{x}_{(i)})
.
Now, for any
\bar{y}
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
g_{\bar{y}}\neq 0
, we know there is some
\bar{x}_{(i)}
such that
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{y})\cong\Bbbk
.
Then we know
N(\bar{x}_{(i)}\to\bar{y})\circ f_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}(m_{i})=f_{\bar{y}}\circ M(\bar{x}_{(i)}\to\bar{y})(m_{i})
.
So, define
m_{\bar{y}}=M(\bar{x}_{(i)}\to\bar{y})(m_{i})
.
In general, define
m_{\bar{y}}=M(\bar{x}_{(i)}\to y)(m_{i})
, for some
\bar{x}_{(i)}
such that
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},\bar{y})\neq 0
, whenever such a
\bar{x}_{(i)}
exists, and define
m_{\bar{y}}=0
otherwise.
Notice that if
P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y})=0
then we must have
m_{\bar{y}}=0
also.
It is straightforward to check that
h:P_{\bar{x}}\to M
determined

h_{\bar{y}}=\begin{cases}\lambda\mapsto\lambda m_{\bar{y}}&P_{\bar{x}}(\bar{y})\neq 0\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

is a morphism such that
g=fh
, completing the proof.
∎

Using Proposition
5.5
as a justification, we have the following definition.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\bar{x}
be an object in
\mathcal{C}^{(n)}
.
We say
\bar{x}
is a
projective source
if either
\bar{x}\neq 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
or
0=x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{n}<1
.

For a suitable screen
\mathfrak{P}
, we want to relate
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
to the category from a higher Auslander algebra of type
\mathbf{A}^{(n)}_{m}
.
To do this, we will construct a suitible
\mathfrak{P}
as follows.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, let
\bar{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m+n-2})
be a finite list of real numbers such that
0<a_{1}<a_{2},\ldots,<a_{m+n-2}<1
, and let

\mathfrak{P}=\{(-\infty,0],(0,a_{1}),[a_{1},a_{2}),\ldots,[a_{m+n-2},1),[1,+\infty)\}

Recall
m\geq 2
and
n\geq 1
so
m+n-2\geq 1
and
\mathfrak{P}
has at least 2 cells contained in
(0,1)
.

We define
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{P}
, which is a screen on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(Proposition
1.25
).
We set
I=\{-1,0,1,\ldots,m+n-1,m+n\}
,
a_{-1}=-\infty
,
a_{0}=0
,
a_{m+n-1}=1
, and
a_{m+n}=+\infty
.

Let
\mathsf{X}_{-1}=(-\infty,0]
,
\mathsf{X}_{0}=(0,a_{1})
, and
\mathsf{X}_{i}=[a_{i},a_{i+1})
if
0<i\leq m+n-1
.
For
\bar{\imath}=(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
, we define
\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{X}_{i_{j}}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\bar{\imath}\in I^{n}
.
The pixel
\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
is not a dead pixel if and only if

0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{n-1}<i_{n}<m+n-1.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
).
We assume
i_{1}>0
as the proof when
i_{1}=0
is nearly the same.
Let
\bar{x}\in\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}
and assume the inequality.
Then we have

0<a_{i_{1}}\leq x_{1}<a_{i_{1}+1}\leq a_{i_{2}}\leq x_{2}<\cdots<a_{i_{n-1}+1}\leq a_{i_{n}}\leq x_{n}<a_{i_{n}+1}\leq 1.

Restricting our attention to the
x_{k}
’s, we have
0<x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{n}<1
.
By definition, this means
\bar{x}\not\cong 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Thus, every
[\gamma]:\bar{x}\to\bar{y}
with
\bar{x},\bar{y}\in\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}
is nonzero in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
if and only if it is nonzero in
\mathcal{C}
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}
is not a dead pixel.

(
\Leftarrow
).
Suppose the inequality is false.
Then either (i) there is some
1\leq j<k\leq n
such that
i_{j}\geq i_{k}
or (ii) there is some
1\leq k\leq n
such that
i_{k}<0
or
i_{k}\geq m+n-1
.

Suppose (i).
So there exists some
\bar{x}\in\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}
such that
x_{j}\geq x_{k}
.
By definition this means
\bar{x}\cong 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
and so
\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}
is a dead pixel since
\bar{x}\cong 0
in
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.

Suppose (ii).
Then there is some
\bar{x}\in\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}
such that
x_{k}=0
or
x_{k}\geq 1
, respectively.
In both cases, by definition,
\bar{x}\cong 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Thus, again,
\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}
is dead.
This concludes the proof.
∎

When
n=1
, we also define
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{()}}=\{(-\infty,0],(0,1),[1,+\infty)\}
for the empty sequence
\bar{a}=()
.

Choose a projective source
\bar{x}
.

•

If
x_{1}=0
and
n=1
, set
\bar{a}_{\bar{x}}=()
.

•

If
x_{1}=0
and
n>1
, set
\bar{a}_{\bar{x}}=(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})
.

•

If
x_{1}>0
, set
\bar{a}_{\bar{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

We define
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}}
to be
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{\bar{x}}}
as before.
We define a specific pixel in
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}}
:

\mathsf{X}_{\bar{x}}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{X}_{(0,1,\ldots,n-1)}&x_{1}=0\\
\mathsf{X}_{(1,2,\ldots,n)}&x_{1}>0.\end{cases}

Recall that a screen
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates a representation
M
when all of the morphisms in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}}
are sent to isomorphisms by
M
(Definition
3.4
).

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
\bar{x}_{(1)},\ldots,\bar{x}_{(m)}
be a finite collection of projective sources, for
1\leq i\leq m
.
For each
1\leq i\leq m
, the partition
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}
pixelates
P_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}_{(i)},-)
.
Moreover,
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}\sqcap\cdots\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(n)}}
pixelates each
P_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}
.

Proof.

We first consider just one
\bar{x}
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\bar{x}\not\cong 0
.
The following proof may be adjusted when
x_{1}=0
by replacing
\bar{x}
with a projective sequence and selecting an appropriate
\bar{x}_{(i)}
.
We will show that
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\cong\Bbbk
if and only if
\bar{y}\in\mathsf{X}_{\bar{x}}
.
By Proposition
5.3
, we know that
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x},\bar{y})\cong\Bbbk
if and only if

0<x_{1}\leq y_{1}<x_{2}\leq y_{2}<\cdots<x_{n}\leq y_{n}<1.

This is precisely the condition for
\bar{y}\in\mathsf{X}_{\bar{x}}
and so each nonzero morphism
[\gamma]
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
with source
\bar{x}
is in
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}}}
.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}}
pixelates
P_{\bar{x}}
.

Now consider our collection
\bar{x}_{(1)},\ldots,\bar{x}_{(m)}
.
Since
\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(1)}}\sqcap\cdots\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(m)}}
is a (finitary) refinement of each
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}
, we see that
\mathfrak{P}
pixelates each
P_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}
.
∎

Definition 5.9

(finitely
\mathcal{P}
-presented)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{\bar{x}}\}
, where
\bar{x}
runs over all projective sources.
We say
M
in
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
is
finitely
\mathcal{P}
-presented
if
M
is finitely-presented in
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
by projective objects in
\mathcal{P}
.
Denote by
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
the full category of
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
whose objects are finitely
\mathcal{P}
-presented representations.

Higher Auslander algebras were originally defined by Iyama in
[
11
]
and a combinatorial approach was introduced in
[
14
]
that we use here. This model also appears in
[
12
]
.

Definition 5.10

(higher Auslander algebra/category)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}_{>1}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
.
The
n
th higher Auslander algebra of
A_{m}
is the path algebra of the quiver
Q^{(n)}_{m}
obtained in the following way.
The vertices of
Q^{(n)}
are labeled in
n
-tuples
\bar{\imath}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
where
1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\ldots\leq i_{n}\leq m
.
There is an arrow
\bar{\imath}\to\bar{\jmath}
when
\bar{\imath}_{k}=\bar{\jmath}_{k}
for all but one
k=\ell
, where
\bar{\jmath}_{\ell}=\bar{\imath}_{\ell}+1
.

There are two imposed relations in the path algebra that generate an admissible ideal
I^{(n)}
.

(1)

Any two compositions of
arrows

\bar{\imath}\to\bar{\jmath}\to\bar{k}
and
\bar{\imath}\to\bar{\jmath}^{\prime}\to\bar{k}
are the same.

(2)

Any path from a constant sequence
(i,i,\ldots,i)
to another
(j,j,\ldots,j)
is
0
.

The
n
th higher Auslander algebra of type
A_{m}
is the algebra
\Lambda=\Bbbk Q^{(n)}_{m}/I^{(n)}
.
The immediate consequence of (1) is that
e_{\bar{\jmath}}\Lambda e_{\bar{\imath}}
is either isomorphic to
\Bbbk
or is 0.

The
category from the
n
th higher Auslander algebra of type
A_{m}
is the
\Bbbk
-linearized category constructed from
Q^{(n)}_{m}
modulo the ideal induced by
I^{(n)}
.
We denote it by
\boldsymbol{A}^{(n)}_{m}
.

Proposition 5.11
.

The
\Bbbk
-linear category
\boldsymbol{A}^{(n)}_{m}
from the
n
th higher Auslander algebra of type
A_{m}
is isomorphic to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}})}
.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{Q}
the category
\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}})}
.
Using the models in
[
14
,
12
]
, we have a bijection
\Phi
from the isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathcal{Q}
to the vertices in the quivers of the models.
The bijection is given by

\Phi:\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}\mapsto(i_{1}+1,i_{2},i_{3}-1,i_{4}-2,\ldots,i_{n}-(n-2)).

It is straightforward, but tedious, to check that if
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
are not dead in
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
, then
\Hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y})\cong e_{\Phi(\mathsf{Y})}\Lambda e_{\Phi(\mathsf{X})}
, where
\Lambda
is the
n
th Auslander algebra of type
A_{m}
as in Definition
5.10
.
∎

Example 5.12

(
A^{(2)}_{5}
)
.

The quiver
Q^{(2)}_{5}
for the
2
nd higher Auslander algebra of type
A_{5}
is given by

\textstyle{(1,5)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(2,5)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(3,5)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(4,5)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(5,5)}
\textstyle{(1,4)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(2,4)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(3,4)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(4,4)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(1,3)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(2,3)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(3,3)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(1,2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(2,2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(1,1).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The category
\boldsymbol{A}_{5}^{(2)}
has objects the vertices of
Q^{(2)}_{5}
and has the relations induced by
I^{(2)}
.
For some
\bar{a}
of length
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{5+2-2})
, we see that
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{\mathbb{R}}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}}
is equivalent to
\boldsymbol{A}_{5}^{(2)}
(Proposition
5.11
).
This can be seen graphically in Figure
5.1
.

\cdots
\cdots
\vdots
\vdots

Figure 5.1.
On the left, example of a screen that pixelates a finite sum of projective indecomposables.
Let
\bar{x}_{(1)}=(0,\frac{1}{6})
,
\bar{x}_{(2)}=(\frac{1}{6},\frac{1}{3})
,
\bar{x}_{(3)}=(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{2})
,
\bar{x}_{(4)}=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{2}{3})
, and
\bar{x}_{(5)}=(\frac{2}{3},\frac{5}{6})
.
In the figure, on the left, is the screen
\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(1)}}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(2)}}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(3)}}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(4)}}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(5)}}
.
All the pixels that are not
completely
shaded are dead pixels.
Thus, only the blue pixels are not dead pixels.
This means
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{\mathbb{R}}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
is equivalent to the path algebra from the quiver on the right, with the usual mesh relations (the diagonal pseudo arrows exit but are superfluous).

Recall that
\mathbf{S}
is the subset of
\boldsymbol{2}^{\mathscr{P}}
such that, for each
\mathscr{L}\in\mathbf{S}
, if
\mathfrak{P}_{1},\mathfrak{P}_{2}\in\mathscr{L}
then there is some
\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{L}
such that
\mathfrak{P}
refines both
\mathfrak{P}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{2}
.

We define a specific
\mathscr{A}^{(n)}\subset\mathbf{S}
to be
\{\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}\}
, where each
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
is defined as before with
\bar{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m+n-2})
where
0<a_{1}<\ldots<a_{k}<1
and
k\geq n-1
(where if
n=1
we also include
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{()}}
in
\mathscr{A}^{(n)}
).
For any
\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}\in\mathfrak{P}\in\mathscr{A}^{(n)}
, if
\mathsf{X}_{\bar{\imath}}
contains any nonzero objects of
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
i_{1}>0
, then there is some initial
s_{\mathsf{X}}\in\mathsf{X}
. That is, for any other
\bar{x}\in\mathsf{X}
there is a path
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma(0)=s_{\mathsf{X}}
,
\gamma(1)=\bar{x}
, and
\mathrm{im}(\gamma)\subset\mathsf{X}
.

The set
\mathscr{P}
is closed under
\sqcap
(Definition
1.21
).
It is straightforward to check that
\mathscr{A}^{(n)}
is also closed under
\sqcap
by taking a pair of
\bar{a}
and
\bar{a}^{\prime}
and combining the sequences in the correct order.
Thus, by Theorem
3.10
, the subcategory
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
of
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
is abelian and, by Corollary
3.14
, embeds exactly into
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.

Remark 5.13
.

Given a
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}\in\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
\bar{a}
has length
m+n-2
, we see that every indecomposable projective representation of
\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)}
embeds into
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
as some
P_{\bar{x}}
.

Conversely, every
P_{\bar{x}}
is pixelated there is some
\mathfrak{P}_{bar{a}}
that pixelates
P_{\bar{x}}
, where
\bar{a}
again has length
m+n-2
.
Thus, there is some indecomposable projective representation of
\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)}
that lifts to a representation isomorphic to
P_{\bar{x}}
.

Proposition 5.14
.

We have the equality
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})=\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.
Moreover, every representation in
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
comes from a representation of an
n
th higher Auslander algebra of type
A_{m}
, for some
m
.

Proof.

Suppose
M
is a finitely
\mathcal{P}
-presented representation and that
\{P_{\bar{x}_{(i)}}\}_{i=1}^{m}
are the indecomposable projectives in
\mathcal{P}
that appear in the presentation.
Then
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(1)}}\sqcap\cdots\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{x}_{(n)}}=\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
for some
\bar{a}
.
We see that
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
pixelates the two terms in the projective presentation of
M
.
Thus, by Lemma
3.8
(
1
),
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
pixelates
M
and so
M
is in
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.

Let
M
be a representation in
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
and let
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}\in\mathscr{A}^{(n)}
such that
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
pixelates
M
(Definition
3.4
) and
\bar{a}
has length
m+n-2\geq n
.
By Proposition
5.11
, we know that the
\Bbbk
-linear category
\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)}
from the
n
th higher Auslander algebra of type
A_{m}
is isomorphic to
\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}})}
.
Thus, by Theorem
3.7
, there is some representation
\overline{M}
of
\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)}
that lifts to a representation
\widehat{M}
isomorphic to
M
.
It is straightforward to check that each projective
P_{\bar{x}}
comes from a projective indecomposable in
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)})
, where
\bar{x}
takes coordinates in
\bar{a}
(except
x_{1}
may be
0
).
Moreover, every projective indecomposable in
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)})
lifts to some
P_{\bar{x}}
.

By Proposition
3.9
, the embedding
\rpwf(\overline{\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}})})\to\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
is exact.
Thus, noting Remark
5.13
, the projective resolution of
\overline{M}
lifts to a projective resolution of
M
.
Since
M
is pwf, we know
\overline{M}
is finite-dimensional and thus finitely-presented.
Therefore,
M
is finitely
\mathcal{P}
-presented and comes from a representation of an Auslander algebra of type
A_{m}
.
∎

We introduce a type of indecomposable representation in
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.
For each pair of a projective source
\bar{x}
and
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
x_{n}<c\leq 1
, we have the indecomposable
M_{\bar{x},c}
whose support is given by

\supp M_{\bar{x},c}=\begin{cases}\{\bar{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid 0<w_{1}<x_{2}\leq w_{2}<\cdots x_{n}\leq w_{n}<c\leq 1\}&x_{1}=0\\
\{\bar{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid 0<x_{1}\leq w_{1}<x_{2}\leq w_{2}<\cdots x_{n}\leq w_{n}<c\leq 1\}&x_{1}>0.\end{cases}

For any nonzero morphism
[\gamma]
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

M([\gamma])=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{1}_{\Bbbk}&\gamma(0),\gamma(1)\in\supp M\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
c=1
then
M_{\bar{x},c}=P_{\bar{x}}
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Ever
M_{\bar{x},c}
such that
c<1
is finitely presented with a projective resolution of length exactly
n
.

Proof.

The projective resolution is the following:

\textstyle{P_{\bar{x}_{(n)}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{\bar{x}_{(n-1)}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\cdots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{\bar{x}_{(2)}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{\bar{x}_{(1)}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{\bar{x}_{(0)}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{\bar{x},c},}

where

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}

\displaystyle=\bar{x},

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-1},c\}

\displaystyle\text{by replacing }x_{n}\text{ with }c,

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-2},x_{n},c\}

\displaystyle\text{by replacing }x_{n-1}\text{ with }x_{n},

\displaystyle\ \ \vdots

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\{x_{1},x_{3},x_{4},\ldots,x_{n-1},x_{n},c\}

\displaystyle\text{by replacing }x_{2}\text{ with }x_{3},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{n}

\displaystyle=\{x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n-1},x_{n},c\}

\displaystyle\text{by replacing }x_{1}\text{ with }x_{2}.\qed

So, every
M_{\bar{x},c}
exists in
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.
Let
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
be full subcategory of
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
whose objects are isomorphic to one in
\{M_{\bar{x},c}\mid\bar{x}\text{ is a projective source},x_{n}<c\leq 1\}
as well as the
0
object.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
M_{\bar{x},c}
and
M_{\bar{y},d}
be nonzero objects in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
.
Then

\Hom_{\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})}(M_{\bar{y},d},M_{\bar{x},c})\cong\Bbbk
if and only if

x_{1}\leq y_{1}<x_{2}\leq y_{2}<\cdots<x_{n}\leq y_{n}<c\leq d.

If the condition above is not satisfied,
\Hom_{\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})}(M_{\bar{y},d},M_{\bar{x},c})=0
.

Proof.

Suppose
\Hom_{\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})}(M_{\bar{y},d},M_{\bar{x},c})\cong\Bbbk
and let
f:M_{\bar{y},d}\to M_{\bar{x},c}
be a nonzero morphism.
Then
\supp M_{\bar{x},c}\cap\supp M_{\bar{y},d}\neq\emptyset
.
Notice that if, for some nonzero
\bar{w}
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
M_{\bar{x},c}(\bar{w})=0
, then
f_{\bar{z}}:M_{\bar{y},d}(\bar{z})\to M_{\bar{x},c}(\bar{z})
is the 0 map for all
\bar{z}
such that
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{w},\bar{z})\neq 0
.
In particular, if
\bar{y}\neq 0
in
\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}
, then
\bar{y}\in\supp M_{\bar{x},c}
.

For the rest of the proof we need a sequence
\{\bar{w}_{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\bar{w}_{(i)}\in\supp M_{\bar{y},d}
for each
i
,
{\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}}\bar{w}_{(i)}=\bar{y}
, and

\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{w}_{(i+1)},\bar{w}_{(i)})\cong\Bbbk

for all
i
.
As in the argument in the proof of Proposition
5.5
, we may assume that there is some
W\in\mathbb{N}
such that, if
i>W
,
(w_{k})_{(i)}=y_{k}
for
1<k\leq n
.
Again like the proof of Proposition
5.5
, for every
\bar{w}\in\supp M_{\bar{y},d}
there is some
\bar{w}_{(i_{w})}
such that
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{w}_{(i)},\bar{w})\cong\Bbbk
.

For contradiction, suppose
d<c
.
Then there is some element
\bar{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n-1},c)
in
\supp M_{\bar{x},c}
but not in
\supp M_{\bar{y},d}
.
This means
M_{\bar{x},c}(\bar{w}_{(i_{w})},\bar{w})\circ f_{\bar{w}_{(i_{w})}}\neq 0
but
f_{\bar{w}}\circ M_{\bar{y},d}(\bar{w}_{(i_{w})},\bar{w})=0
.
Since
f
is a map of representations, this is a contradiction.
Therefore, the condition in the lemma holds.

Now suppose the condition in the lemma does not hold.
Then, since the condition is false, there is some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
i\geq N
, we have
\bar{w}_{(i)}\notin\supp M_{\bar{x},c}
.
Without loss of generality,
N\geq W
.
Then, the only way that the condition
f_{\bar{w}}\circ M_{\bar{y},d}(\bar{w}_{(i_{w})},\bar{w})=M_{\bar{x},c}\circ f_{\bar{w}_{(i_{w})}}
is satisfied, for all
\bar{w}\in\supp M_{\bar{x},c}\cap\supp M_{\bar{y},d}
, is if
f=0
.
If
\supp M_{\bar{x},c}\cap M_{\bar{y},d}=\emptyset
then
f
must be 0, anyway.
This concludes the proof.
∎

The following definition of an
n
-tilting cluster tilting subcategories comes from Iyama
[
11
]
.

Definition 5.17

(
n
-cluster tilting subcategory)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be an abelian category.
A subcategory
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathcal{D}
is an
n
-cluster tilting subcategory
if
\mathcal{T}
is functorially finite and

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle=\{M\in\Ob(\mathcal{D})\mid\Ext^{i}(\mathcal{T},M)=0,\,0<i<n\}

\displaystyle=\{M\in\Ob(\mathcal{D})\mid\Ext^{i}(M,\mathcal{T})=0,\,0<i<n\}.

We want to show that
\mathrm{add}\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
is
(n-1)
-cluster tilting.
This means
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
must also contain the indecomposable injectives.

Proposition 5.18
.

The indecomposable injective objects in
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
are precisely the
M_{\bar{x},c}
in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
such that
x_{1}=0
and every
M
in
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
has an injective coresolution.

Proof.

First we will show the existence of the coresolution.
Then we will show the desired objects’ injectivity.

Let
M
be an object in
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})=\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
and let
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
be a screen in
\mathscr{A}^{(n)}
that pixelates
M
.
Without loss of generality, we assume
\bar{a}
has length
m+n-2\geq n
.
As in the proof of Proposition
5.14
, we find
\overline{M}
in
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m})
such that
M
comes from
\bar{M}
(Theorem
3.7
).
In
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m})
we take the injective coresolution of
\overline{M}
.

We now show that each injective indecomposable in the coresolution of
\overline{M}
embeds into
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
as an object in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
.
Each
\overline{I}_{\bar{\imath}}
in the coresolution has support

\{\bar{\jmath}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}^{n}\mid j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq\ldots\leq j_{m},\,\forall 1\leq k\leq m,j_{k}\leq i_{k},\,\text{and }e_{\bar{\imath}}\Lambda e_{\bar{\jmath}}\neq 0\},

where
\Lambda
is the
n
th higher Auslander algebra of type
A_{m}
.
Recall the bijection
\Phi
in the proof of Proposition
5.11
.
We see

\Phi^{-1}(\bar{\imath})=(i_{1}-1,i_{2},i_{3}+1,i_{4}+2,\ldots,i_{n}+(n-2)).

Let
\bar{y}=(0,a_{i_{1}-1},a_{i_{2}},a_{i_{3}+1},\ldots,a_{i_{n-1}+(n-3)})
and
d=a_{i_{n}+(n-2)}
.
Then we see
\bar{I}_{\bar{\imath}}
embeds into
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
as
M_{\bar{y},d}
.

Since the embedding
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}^{(n)}_{m})\hookrightarrow\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
. is exact, we have an exact sequence of the form

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i}^{m_{1}}}M_{\bar{x}_{(i,1)},c_{i,1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i}^{m_{2}}}M_{\bar{x}_{(i,2)},c_{i,2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i}^{m_{p}}}M_{\bar{x}_{(i,p)},c_{i,p}},}

where each
(x_{1})_{(i,j)}=0
.

Notice also that if some representation
I
in
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
that is injective, it comes from a direct sum of
\bar{I}_{\bar{\imath}}
’s in some
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)})
.
Thus,
I
is a direct sum of
M_{\bar{x},c}
’s.

It remains to show that each
M_{\bar{x},c}
is injective in
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
when
x_{1}=0
.
Suppose
M_{\bar{x},c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\leftarrow}}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\hookrightarrow}}N
is a diagram in
\rpA(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})=\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
, where
x_{1}=0
.
Then there is some
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
that pixelates each of
M_{\bar{x},c}
,
M
, and
N
. By Theorem
3.7
and our observations in the previous paragraph, these come from
\bar{I}_{\bar{\imath}}
,
\overline{M}
, and
\overline{N}
in
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)})
, respectively, where
\bar{I}_{\bar{\imath}}
is the injective at
\bar{\imath}
.
In this particular case, we can “push down” the morphisms
f
and
g
to
\bar{f}:\overline{M}\to\overline{N}
and
\bar{g}:\overline{M}\to\bar{I}_{\bar{\imath}}
, respectively, where
\bar{f}
is still mono.

Since
\bar{I}_{\bar{\imath}}
is injective, there is
\bar{h}:\overline{N}\to\bar{I}_{\bar{\imath}}
such that
\bar{h}\bar{f}=\bar{g}
.
Then the lower commutative triangle, now with
\bar{h}
, embeds into
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
as a commutative triangle with
f
,
g
, and now
h
.
This completes the proof.
∎

It should be noted that it is possible, but exceedingly tedious, to show
M_{\bar{x},c}
, with
x_{1}=0
, is also injective in
\rpwf(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.

The proof of the following proposition is a dual computation to that for Proposition
5.15
.

Proposition 5.19
.

Every
M_{\bar{x},c}
in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
such that
x_{1}>0
has an injective coresolution of length exactly
n
.

Proposition 5.20
.

The subcategory
\mathrm{add}\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
of
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
is an
(n-1)
-cluster tilting subcategory.

Proof.

By Propositions
5.14
and
5.18
, we see every object in
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
is finitely-presented and finitely-copresented by objects in
\add\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
.
Thus,
\add\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
is functorially finite.
Moreover, in the proofs of the same propositions we have seen that each indecomposable projective and injective is in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
.

Let
M_{\bar{x},c}
and
M_{\bar{y},d}
be representations in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
and
N
some indecomposable representation not in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
.
By the proof of Proposition
5.14
, we have
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{1}}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{2}}
, and
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{3}}
that pixelate
M_{\bar{x},c}
,
M_{\bar{y},d}
, and
N
, respectively.

Since
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{1}}
,
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{2}}
, and
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{3}}
are in
\mathscr{A}^{(n)}
, so is
\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}=\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{1}}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{2}}\sqcap\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}_{3}}
, where
\bar{a}
is the combined lists of
\bar{a}_{1}
,
\bar{a}_{2}
, and
\bar{a}_{3}
, arranged in ascending order with duplicates removed.
For ease of notation, let
\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{\bar{a}}
.
Then there is some
n
th Auslander algebra
\Lambda
of type
A_{m}
such that
\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)}
is equivalent to
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Furthermore, there are
\overline{M}_{1}
,
\overline{M}_{2}
, and
\overline{N}
representations of
\text{\framebox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}}$}}^{\mathfrak{P}}
whose lifts to
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
are isomorphic to
M_{\bar{x},c}
,
M_{\bar{y},d}
, and
N
, respectively.

By construction,
\overline{M}_{1}
and
\overline{M}_{2}
are in the
(n-1)
cluster tilting subcategory
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)}
(using the models in
[
14
,
12
]
).
We may then use the fact that
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)})\to\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
is an exact embedding (Remark
3.13
) and the fact that
\Ext^{i}(\overline{M}_{1},\overline{M}_{2})=0
for
0<i<n-1
to see that
\Ext^{i}(M_{\bar{x},c},M_{\bar{y},d})=0
for
0<i<n-1
.
Also by construction,
\overline{N}
is
not
in the
(n-1)
cluster tilting subcategory of
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)})
.
Then there is some
\overline{M}_{3}
in the
(n-1)
cluster tilting subcategory of
\rpwf(\boldsymbol{A}_{m}^{(n)})
such that
\Ext^{i}(\overline{M}_{3},\overline{N})\neq 0
or
\Ext^{i}(\overline{N},\overline{M}_{3})\neq 0
, for some
0<i<n-1
.
The
\overline{M}_{3}
lifts to an object
M_{\overline{z},e}
in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
and either
\Ext^{i}(M_{\overline{z},e},N)\neq 0
or
\Ext^{i}(N,M_{\overline{z},e})\neq 0
.
In either case,
N
has an extension with something in
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
in degree
i
for
0<i<n-1
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
is an
(n-1)
cluster tilting subcategory of
\rfp(\mathcal{A}^{(n)}_{\mathbb{R}})
.
∎

Because we are working in the world of the continuum, we need to make a small modification to
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
.

Definition 5.21

(
\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}
)
.

We define
\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}
as the subcategory of
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
that omits the projective and injective objects.
That is, the objects of
\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}
are the the objects
M_{\bar{x},c}
where
x_{1}>0
and
c<1
.

We now present our analogue of a specific case of
[
11
, Corollary 1.16]
, more easily seen by comparing to
[
14
, Theorem/Construction 3.4]
and
[
12
, Theorem 2.3]
.

Theorem 5.22
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer.
Then
{\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}}^{\text{op}}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{(n+1)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

Let
M_{\bar{x},c}
be in
{\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}}^{\text{op}}
.
Then
M_{\bar{x},c}
is determined by

0<x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{n-1}<x_{n}<c<1.

If we set
x_{n+1}=c
, there is an immediate bijection between the nonzero objects of
{\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}}^{\text{op}}
and
\mathcal{A}^{(n+1)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Let
M_{\bar{x},c}
and
M_{\bar{y},d}
be nonzero objects in
{\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}}^{\text{op}}
.
By Lemma
5.16
, and noting we are in the opposite category, we see that
\Hom_{{\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}}^{\text{op}}}(M_{\bar{x},c},M_{\bar{y},d})\cong\Bbbk
if and only if

0<x_{1}\leq y_{1}<x_{2}\leq y_{2}<\cdots<x_{n}\leq y_{n}<c\leq d<1,

and otherwise the hom space is
0
.
Set
\bar{x}^{\prime}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},c)
and
\bar{y}^{\prime}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n},d)
.
Then the displayed condition is the same condition for
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{(n+1)}_{\mathbb{R}}}(\bar{x}^{\prime},\bar{y}^{\prime})
to be nonzero and isomorphic to
\Bbbk
.
Therefore,
{\underline{\mathcal{M}^{(n)}}}^{\text{op}}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{(n+1)}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
∎
